Discussion Name:
Is the Book of Mormon an authentic ancient scripture?

1. Thesis:
The Book of Mormon is an authentic ancient text.

  1.1. Pro:
  Longevity: The Book of Mormon has withstood the test of time.

    1.1.1. Pro:
    Frauds generally fade with time and become less rather than more important and widely believed. The Book of Mormon's importance and influence [1] has grown [2] over the past 200 years, suggesting is an authentic work.

      1.1.1.1. Con:
      Your argument is a no true Scotsman argument.

        1.1.1.1.1. Con:
        If it is not a conclusive argument, it still can have evidential weight.

        1.1.1.1.2. Con:
        A 'No True Scottsman" argument needs three things: 1)an initial generalization (in this case you may want to use "the BoM has stood the test of time.") 2) a refutation of that argument (in this case, there are only two statements in agreement, not a refutation); and 3) an adjustment of the original generalization that tries to disassociate the refutation from the generalization (in this case, there is no adjustment).

      1.1.1.2. Con:
      By this criteria Dianetics by L. Ron Hubbard and Scientology will have achieved the same level of legitimacy as the Book of Mormon by 2150 if its influence keeps growing.

      1.1.1.3. Con:
      The trajectory of nearly every modern religion has been increasing growth and influence over the past 200 years. If that is a measure of authenticity then it suggests all of their teachings are both authentic and contradictory. This would be impossible therefore growth and influence have no bearing on authenticity.

        1.1.1.3.1. Pro:
        In the 1800s, there were 204 million [3] Christians. By 2013, this number rose to 2355 million.

        1.1.1.3.2. Pro:
        There were 91 million [4] Muslims in the 1800s. By 2013, this number had risen to 1635 million.

        1.1.1.3.3. Pro:
        Much of the growth of a religion can be attributed to [5] the growth in the human population as well as geography.

          1.1.1.3.3.1. Pro:
          Three-quarters [6] of religious people live in a country where they form a majority of the population.

            1.1.1.3.3.1.1. Con:
            This does not apply: The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints is not the majority religion in any nation.

        1.1.1.3.4. Con:
        This is nonsensical. By limiting this to "every major religion," it factors out the thousands or tens of thousands of religions that have failed. The fact that the Book of Mormon is comparable to works like the Bible and Quran should be evidence of authenticity.

        1.1.1.3.5. Con:
        It is nearly impossible [7] for a "baby religion" to become a major religion. Thus, The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints becoming a major religion is a remarkable accomplishment.

        1.1.1.3.6. Con:
        This statement is false. For example, Indonesia alone has had 600 new religions in modern history [8]. The same is true of almost any locale with sufficient freedom to establish a new religion. The trajectory of almost every modern religion is toward extinction.

      1.1.1.4. Con:
      Assuming the Mormon “one true religion” worldview,” any other religion is false. And yet these religions do not fade. Instead, there are many older religions with many more adherents, as well as other, newer religions like Jehovah's Witnesses, Scientology, 7th Day Adventists, etc. Thus, the premise that falsehoods generally fade with time cannot be valid given that the trend with so many "false" religions is geared towards longevity instead of fading away

        1.1.1.4.1. Con:
        There are many faiths that believe they are the One True Church [9]

        1.1.1.4.2. Con:
        The language in the parent claim is 'generally' and 'suggests' while the language in this claim is 'will'. That is a strawman fallacy.

        1.1.1.4.3. Con:
        True principles are valuable no matter which religion they are found in. Most religions have any number of true and correct principles found in a true source (the Holy Bible) that guide them and help them grow. The Church of Jesus Christ is distinct because the Book of Mormon is the chief cornerstone of the faith. Many true principles they teach comes from it. Ergo, if these create growth, then they are true principles and the Book of Mormon is a true source.

      1.1.1.5. Con:
      There are numerous different religions, each of which claims to be the one true religion. Based on this, it is likely that all or most of these religions are successful frauds that haven't faded with time.

      1.1.1.6. Pro:
      There are presently 10,000 religions [10]that have not become prominent and countless religions that have not withstood the test of time.

      1.1.1.7. Con:
      This is a no true Scotsman fallacy [11]. There is no demonstrable correlation between veracity and longevity or popularity.

    1.1.2. Pro:
    The Book of Mormon's authenticity as an ancient text has been relentlessly attacked for almost two centuries and it still accepted as a sacred authentic text by growing number of millions today.

      1.1.2.1. Con:
      People accepting a text as scripture is not evidence of it being scripture. This is true of the Quran as well.

    1.1.3. Con:
    Being old, widely published, and widely believed does not make something accurate or true.  Any number of texts fit those criterion that are not accurate.  Ex.  The Protocols of the Elders of Zion is a complete fabrication yet is widely believed by any number of people.

      1.1.3.1. Con:
      The Protocols [12] appears to contain 62 pages of work. The Book of Mormon has 531 apart from their introductions.

      1.1.3.2. Pro:
      Religious texts such as the Quran and Hindu scriptures that are incompatible with the Book of Mormon have existed even longer than the Book of Mormon has, and their religions are still thriving.

        1.1.3.2.1. Pro:
        The Kama Sutra [13] is far older than the Book of Mormon.

    1.1.4. Con:
    Time does not necessarily diminish the prevalence of a false belief, no matter how much evidence has been presented otherwise.

      1.1.4.1. Con:
      No, but time would generally tend to diminish it. Truth can be pushed down for a time, and falsehood propped up, but in time frauds will fail and truth will stand.

    1.1.5. Pro:
    The Book of Mormon has stood as an important historical and religious text for close to 200 years.

      1.1.5.1. Con:
      Almost 200 years is not the test of time.

        1.1.5.1.1. Con:
        Whether or not 200 years is sufficient for "the test of time" is an opinion. Technically, anyone could argue that anything has not withstood the "test of time"

    1.1.6. Pro:
    The lip of truth shall be established for ever: but a lying tongue is but for a moment. - Proverbs 12:19

      1.1.6.1. Con:
      Using scripture to prove the authenticity of the Book of Mormon is begging the question. It is presuming authority of the scripture.

        1.1.6.1.1. Con:
        This is from a different and more widely accepted scripture than the Book of Mormon, the Bible. Thus, it is not question-begging, just an argument that might only be persuasive to Christians and Jews.

      1.1.6.2. Con:
      This proverb is about the lying tongue itself (and presumably the person attached to it), not the lies said by the tongue, being "but for a moment."

        1.1.6.2.1. Pro:
        Biblical commentators have said that this refers to that which is established by truth and by falsehood: "The lip of truth, i.e., the lip which speaketh truth, endures for ever (for truth, אמת equals אמנתּ, is just the enduring); but the tongue of falsehood is only for a moment, or a wink of the eye, for it is soon convicted, and with disgrace brings to silence; for a post-bibl." (Note: Vid., Duke's Rabbin. Blumenlese (1844), p. 231.) [14]

      1.1.6.3. Con:
      The Book of Mormon might be false even if Joseph Smith wasn't lying.

        1.1.6.3.1. Pro:
        Joseph Smith could have been deluded into believing the story was true.

          1.1.6.3.1.1. Pro:
          Mental illnesses like schizophrenia [15] commonly lead to delusions.

  1.2. Con:
  Joseph Smith, the purported translator of the Book of Mormon, is not credible.

    1.2.1. Pro:
    Joseph Smith was a false prophet.

      1.2.1.1. Pro:
      Joseph Smith lacked sufficient moral standing to be a prophet

        1.2.1.1.1. Pro:
        Joseph Smith was intemperate.

          1.2.1.1.1.1. Pro:
          According to David Stafford, who worked with Joseph Smith in Manchester, Smith was fined for breach of the peace after he and another co-worker filed a lawsuit against him for starting a fight during an argument on the job, during which Smith was intoxicated. (Howe 1834, p. 249 [16])

        1.2.1.1.2. Pro:
        Joseph Smith had a record of dishonesty.

          1.2.1.1.2.1. Pro:
          Joseph Smith and his family had a reputation for dishonesty among many of his neighbors in New York.

            1.2.1.1.2.1.1. Pro:
            Joseph Smith lied about the nature of his "persecution" while he was living in western New York and falsely claimed that he had to leave Manchester and move to Pennsylvania because of religious persecution.

              1.2.1.1.2.1.1.1. Pro:
              Joseph Smith's difficulties in Manchester before he moved to Pennsylvania was due to efforts from members of Joseph Smith's treasure digging company to obtain the golden plates [17] from Joseph Smith because they considered it property of the joint venture that Smith had appropriated for himself.

                1.2.1.1.2.1.1.1.1. Pro:
                After Smith purportedly received the golden plates [18], he took a digging job in Macedon [19], but had to return when his wife went after him from Manchester and told him that the plates were in danger of being discovered by Smith's treasure digging associates.

                  1.2.1.1.2.1.1.1.1.1. Pro:
                  According to Willard Chase, Joseph Smith told him that after he received the golden plates [20], "[h]e then went to the town of Macedon to work. After about ten days, it having been suggested that some one had got his book, his wife went after him; he hired a horse, and went home in the afternoon, staid long enough to drink one cup of tea, and then went for his book." (Howe 1834, p. 246 [21])

              1.2.1.1.2.1.1.2. Pro:
              Willard Chase said of Joseph Smith in late 1827: "His neighbors having become disgusted with his foolish stories [relating to the golden plates [22]], he determined to go back to Pennsylvania, to avoid what he called persecution." (Howe 1834, p. 246 [23])

            1.2.1.1.2.1.2. Pro:
            According to Parley Chase, who knew the Smith family in Manchester, New York, "not one of the male members of the Smith family were entitled to any credit, whatsoever. They were lazy, intemperate and worthless men, much addicted to lying. In this they frequently boasted of their skill." (Howe 1834, p. 248 [24])

          1.2.1.1.2.2. Con:
          In spite of intense persecution, tarrings and featherings, the death of children caused by the actions of mobs, and eventual martyrdom, Joseph Smith stayed true to his faith for his entire life.

            1.2.1.1.2.2.1. Con:
            If we accept willingness to suffer for religious claims as indicating they must be true, then we would have to accept multiple contradictory religions that make similar claims.

            1.2.1.1.2.2.2. Con:
            This proves the point that he lacked character and refused to come clean no matter who got hurt.

            1.2.1.1.2.2.3. Pro:
            "On Saturday, March 24, Joseph was dragged from his bedroom in the dead of night. His attackers strangled him until he blacked out, tore off his shirt and drawers, beat and scratched him, and jammed a vial of poison against his teeth until it broke. After tarring and feathering [25] his body, they left him for dead. Joseph limped back to the Johnsons' house and cried out for a blanket. Through the night, his friends scraped off the tar until his flesh was raw."

            1.2.1.1.2.2.4. Pro:
            the mob broke down the front door, took Smith's oldest surviving adopted child from his arms, dragged Smith from the room, leaving his exposed child on a trundle bed...The child was knocked off the bed onto the floor in the doorway of the home as Smith was forcibly removed from his home. The child died from exposure...five days after the event from the condition that doctors said he developed the night of the mob violence. [26]

            1.2.1.1.2.2.5. Pro:
            "[A] mob of about 200 armed men, their faces painted black with wet gunpowder, stormed the Carthage Jail in the late afternoon of June 27, 1844...[Joseph Smith] was shot twice in the back and that a third bullet, fired from a musket on the ground outside, hit him in the chest [27]."

              1.2.1.1.2.2.5.1. Con:
              Although Smith didn't recant his faith in Mormonism at this time, that was because doing so would be unlikely to stop all 200 men in the mob from trying to attack him, not because he believed Mormonism.

            1.2.1.1.2.2.6. Con:
            Joseph Smith was attacked for his criminal acts. Yes, he was viewed as a charlatan and chased out of town for his preying on the innocent.

          1.2.1.1.2.3. Con:
          Joseph Smith was driven from his home on many occasions because of his belief in the authenticity of the Book of Mormon and its implications.

            1.2.1.1.2.3.1. Pro:
            Because of persecution, Joseph Smith left his home in Harmony, Pennsylvania [28] to complete the translation of the Book of Mormon.

            1.2.1.1.2.3.2. Pro:
            In large part because of persecution, in December 1830, Joseph Smith and the young Church moved from Palmyra, New York to Kirtland, Ohio [29].

            1.2.1.1.2.3.3. Pro:
            In January 1838, because of persecution the Prophet and his family, they were forced to leave their home in Kirtland [30] and take refuge in Far West, Missouri.

            1.2.1.1.2.3.4. Pro:
            Near the end of 1838, while Joseph Smith was imprisoned for defending himself against mob persecution, Joseph Smith's wife and children were driven from their home in Missouri [31] to Illinois.

            1.2.1.1.2.3.5. Con:
            There are other potential reasons Joseph may have been driven from his home that are unrelated to his beliefs.

              1.2.1.1.2.3.5.1. Pro:
              Religious families will often disown [32] and drive out their children if they are gay.

          1.2.1.1.2.4. Pro:
          Joseph Smith repeatedly lied about the Mormon practice of polygamy.

            1.2.1.1.2.4.1. Con:
            Joseph's actions were not illegal under the law as long as he did not announce them publicly.

            1.2.1.1.2.4.2. Con:
            If Joseph were to announce plural marriage in Nauvoo publicly, then that would have brought the mobs down on them.

            1.2.1.1.2.4.3. Con:
            There is no evidence Joseph Smith was intimate with any of these women.

              1.2.1.1.2.4.3.1. Con:
              John C. Bennet was an abortionist by trade and served on the First Presidency. His services are likely [33] the cause of Joseph's lack of children (evidence of intimate relationships)

                1.2.1.1.2.4.3.1.1. Con:
                This is hearsay written 40 years after the fact. Same source [34]. Sarah Pratt [35]

                1.2.1.1.2.4.3.1.2. Con:
                John Bennett confessed, under oath, that Joseph Smith did not engage in any inappropriate act [36].

                1.2.1.1.2.4.3.1.3. Con:
                John Bennet was an adulterer who had illegitimate children [37] and did not abort them.

                1.2.1.1.2.4.3.1.4. Con:
                An abortion was a risky and dangerous medical procedure. There is no record that any of Joseph Smith's alleged wives became ill or died [38] during the period when these abortions may have occurred.

                1.2.1.1.2.4.3.1.5. Con:
                There is no evidence that Bennett ever performed an abortion for Smith's wives; rather, Bennett offered to perform abortions for women with whom he sought to have an adulterous affair [39].

            1.2.1.1.2.4.4. Con:
            "Adoption" sealings were practiced for members who were married to non-member spouses. These relationships were not consummated and were merely to secure eternal marriage blessings. This practice has been discontinued.

            1.2.1.1.2.4.5. Pro:
            Joseph Smith repeatedly deceived his first wife Emma [40] about his practice of polygamy, even though Smith's revelation on polygamy required her consent (see D&C 132:61 [41]).

              1.2.1.1.2.4.5.1. Con:
              In verses 64-65, God says that the wife in this case is duty-bound to give that consent. If she does not, she is a transgressor and loses her right to have any say.

              1.2.1.1.2.4.5.2. Con:
              Emma gave her consent at some times, and then refused to give it at others. The situation was complicated to say the least.

            1.2.1.1.2.4.6. Pro:
            While practicing polygamy, Joseph Smith consented to a passage in the Mormon scriptural canon renouncing polygamy (1835 D&C 101:4 [42]).

              1.2.1.1.2.4.6.1. Con:
              Regardless of the fact that John C. Bennett's credibility regarding church practices and policies on "Spiritual Wifery" [43] is non-existent, there is no confirmed historical evidence that Joseph Smith had begun practicing polygamy by 1835. According to his own record, he had to be threatened with destruction before he fully committed to obey this particular commandment. Gospel Topics Essays - polygamy [44]

              1.2.1.1.2.4.6.2. Pro:
              Joseph Smith sanctioned other Mormons for teaching or practicing polygamy, while practicing it himself.

                1.2.1.1.2.4.6.2.1. Pro:
                In a church newspaper, Joseph Smith announced the excommunication of Hiram Brown for "preaching Polygamy, and other false and corrupt doctrines." (Times & Seasons, vol. 5, p. 423 [45])

              1.2.1.1.2.4.6.3. Pro:
              D&C 101:4 (1835 ed.) [46] states, "Inasmuch as this church of Christ has been reproached with the crime of fornication, and polygamy: we declare that we believe, that one man should have one wife; and one woman, but one husband, except in case of death, when either is at liberty to marry again."

              1.2.1.1.2.4.6.4. Pro:
              While Joseph Smith was practicing polygamy, a church newspaper for which he was the editor twice denied an exposé by John C. Bennett [47] accusing Smith of practicing polygamy, and identified 1835 D&C 101:4 [48] as the church's only allowed rule regarding marriage. (Times & Seasons 3, p. 909, 1842 [49]; Times & Seasons 3, p. 939, 1842 [50]).

            1.2.1.1.2.4.7. Pro:
            While practicing polygamy, Joseph Smith made or permitted statements to the public denying that he or his church practiced polygamy.

              1.2.1.1.2.4.7.1. Pro:
              -> See 1.2.1.1.2.4.6.4.

              1.2.1.1.2.4.7.2. Pro:
              After having married at least two dozen wives, Joseph Smith forcefully denied, to a Sunday congregation, accusations that he had more than one wife, and stated, "What a thing it is for a man to be accused of committing adultery and having seven wives when I can only find one." (Manuscript History F-1, p. 60 [51])

                1.2.1.1.2.4.7.2.1. Con:
                Joseph was only legally and eternally married to his first wife Emma. All of the others were 'adoption' or 'eternity only' sealings that were not consummated intimate relationships.

              1.2.1.1.2.4.7.3. Pro:
              With the consent and awareness of Joseph Smith, his brother Hyrum [52] published a letter in the church newspaper declaring polygamy as a false doctrine and denying that the church practiced it. (Times & Seasons, vol. 5, p. 474, March 1844 [53])

              1.2.1.1.2.4.7.4. Pro:
              -> See 1.2.1.1.2.4.6.2.1.

              1.2.1.1.2.4.7.5. Pro:
              After he had taught and practiced polygamy, a church newspaper asked Joseph Smith in 1838, "Do the Mormons believe in having more wives than one," to which Smith replied, "No, not at the same time. But they believe, that if their companion dies, they have the right to marry again." (Elders Journal, July 1838, p. 43 [54])

                1.2.1.1.2.4.7.5.1. Con:
                This statement was made in 1838. The revelation regarding plural marriage was recorded in 1843. Up to that point, the principle of polygamy had not been taught publicly to the body of the Church. Rather, it had been taught only to a select few by way of revealed command. At that time, and after it was taught to the church as a whole, no one could practice polygamy without authorization. So, regarding the body of the Church, this statement was true. Gospel Topics - Plural Marriage [55]

            1.2.1.1.2.4.8. Pro:
            Joseph Smith responded to accusations of polygamy by claiming that he had never committed "adultery," because under his belief system, polygamy was not considered adulterous.

              1.2.1.1.2.4.8.1. Con:
              If it's not a sin, and it's not against the laws of the land, (which it wasn't at that time), then there is no problem here.

              1.2.1.1.2.4.8.2. Pro:
              -> See 1.2.1.1.2.4.7.2.

          1.2.1.1.2.5. Pro:
          Joseph Smith participated in banking fraud relating to the Kirtland Safety Society [56] in 1836-37.

            1.2.1.1.2.5.1. Con:
            "The lack of a state bank charter was the key factor leading to the quick demise of the bank and to much of the bitterness that followed." [Source p.1 [57]] Fraud is wrongful or criminal deception intended to result in financial or personal gain. There is no evidence that fraud took place.

            1.2.1.1.2.5.2. Con:
            "Thousands of firms and individuals in Ohio were unable to meet their debt obligations in the late 1830s and early 1840s. A number of the banks in Ohio and virtually all of the banks in Michigan failed in the late 1830s. The fact that founders of the Kirtland Bank repaid the bulk of their debts shows their intentions were honorable, even if their banking activities were based on panic and false hopes." [Source p.14 [58]]

          1.2.1.1.2.6. Pro:
          Joseph Smith lied or changed his story about details relating to the golden plates [59].

            1.2.1.1.2.6.1. Pro:
            Joseph Smith changed his story about who other than himself would be able to see the golden plates [60], and when.

              1.2.1.1.2.6.1.1. Con:
              Much or most of the anti-Joseph Smith narratives (including the allegations that he had never seen the Golden Plates, or planned to monetise them) are from one book [61] written by Eber Howe which, at best, shows a borderline unethical anti-Joseph Smith bias.

                1.2.1.1.2.6.1.1.1. Pro:
                Eber Howe [62], the writer of these claims, never met Joseph Smith.

                1.2.1.1.2.6.1.1.2. Pro:
                The credibility of these reports is very thin. Howe purchased affidavits from Philastus Hurbert, an excommunicated member of the Church, who was employed by an anti-Mormon committee in Ohio to collect derogatory evidence against Joseph Smith and the Book of Mormon and used these the following year in his expose: Mormonism Unveiled. Howe as fraudster. [63]

                1.2.1.1.2.6.1.1.3. Pro:
                Eber Howe, the author of these claims, selectively did not include relevant information in his work that supported the truthfulness of the Book of Mormon in his work discrediting Joseph Smith and The Book of Mormon. Howe the fraudster. [64].

              1.2.1.1.2.6.1.2. Pro:
              Joseph Smith falsely promised or prophesied that he would reveal the golden plates [65] openly to the world by a certain date.

                1.2.1.1.2.6.1.2.1. Pro:
                According to Willard Chase, Joseph Smith told him in September 1827 that "he was commanded to keep [the golden plates [66]] two years, without letting it come to the eye of any one but himself. This commandment, however, he did not keep, for in less than two years, twelve men [67] said they had seen it." (Howe 1834, p. 245 [68])

                1.2.1.1.2.6.1.2.2. Pro:
                According to Willard Chase (relating what Martin Harris [69] reported in spring 1829), Joseph Smith falsely claimed that "the Prophet's wife [70], in the month of June following would deliver of a male child that would be able when two years old to translate the Gold Bible. The, said he, you will see Joseph Smith, Jr. walking through the streets of Palmyra, with a Gold Bible under his arm, and having a gold breast-plate on, and a gold sword [71] hanging by his side." (Howe 1834, pp. 246-47 [72])

                  1.2.1.1.2.6.1.2.2.1. Con:
                  -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.1.1.

                1.2.1.1.2.6.1.2.3. Pro:
                According to Isaac Hale, Joseph Smith's father-in-law, Smith said "that he was to exhibit the plates to the world, at a certain time, which was then about eighteen months distant." However, when the appointed time passed, Smith said that "he, himself was deceived, but that [Hale] should see them if [Hale] were where they were."  (Howe 1834, pp. 266-67 [73])

                1.2.1.1.2.6.1.2.4. Pro:
                According to Joshua M'Kune, one of Joseph Smith's neighbors in Pennsylvania, Smith claimed that the golden plates [74], as well as a sword [75], a breastplate, and a pair of spectacles [76] found with the plates "were to be shewn to all the world as evidence of the truth of what was contained in those plates," and that "he (M'Kune) and others should see them at a specified time." However, this never occurred. (Howe 1834, p. 267 [77]).

                1.2.1.1.2.6.1.2.5. Pro:
                According to Alva Hale, Joseph Smith's brother-in-law, "Smith told him, that he should see the Plates [78] from which he translated the book of Mormon." However, "at the time specified by Smith," Hale "called to see the plates, but Smith did not show them, but appeared angry." (Howe 1834, p. 268 [79])

                1.2.1.1.2.6.1.2.6. Pro:
                Joseph Smith, with his father and brother Hyrum, promised to show the golden plates [80] to neighbor William Stafford after the book was translated, but later said that they had "received an express commandment, forbidding them to show the plates." (Howe, 1834, p. 240 [81])

                1.2.1.1.2.6.1.2.7. Pro:
                According to Willard Chase, Samuel T. Lawrence [82] suggested to Joseph Smith that "it would not be prudent to let these plates [83] be seen for about two years, as it would make a great disturbance in the neighborhood." (Howe 1834, p. 243 [84])

                  1.2.1.1.2.6.1.2.7.1. Pro:
                  Willard Chase spoke with Samuel T. Lawrence [85] about his involvement in the story of the golden plates. (Howe 1834, p. 243 [86])

                    1.2.1.1.2.6.1.2.7.1.1. Con:
                    -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.1.1.

            1.2.1.1.2.6.2. Pro:
            Joseph Smith admitted on some occasions that he never had the golden plates [87] or that he was never able to see in a stone [88].

              1.2.1.1.2.6.2.1. Pro:
              Willard Chase said that Joseph Smith "told one of my neighbors that he had not got any such book [as the golden plates [89]]; nor never had such an one; but that he had told the story to deceive the d—d fool, (meaning me,) to get him to make a chest." (Howe 1834, p. 246 [90])

                1.2.1.1.2.6.2.1.1. Con:
                -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.1.1.2.

              1.2.1.1.2.6.2.2. Con:
              All of the statements about his claim of seeing into a stone are hearsay and against actual eyewitness testimony provided by Emma Smith.

              1.2.1.1.2.6.2.3. Con:
              -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.1.1.

              1.2.1.1.2.6.2.4. Pro:
              Before claiming to translate the Book of Mormon, Joseph Smith told his brother-in-law Alva Hale that his scrying [91] practice with seer stones [92] was"all d[amne]d nonsense," that Smith "was deceived himself but did not intend to deceive others," and "that he intended to quit the business, (of peeping) and labor for his livelihood." (Howe 1834, p. 268 [93]).

                1.2.1.1.2.6.2.4.1. Con:
                Alva Hale's statement is unreliable.

                  1.2.1.1.2.6.2.4.1.1. Pro:
                  There is no physical evidence accompanying this statement.

                  1.2.1.1.2.6.2.4.1.2. Pro:
                  This is hearsay, and would not be allowed in a court because of its inherent unreliability.

                  1.2.1.1.2.6.2.4.1.3. Pro:
                  Alva Hale's testimony was contradicted [94] by others. For example, he claimed the Martin Harris was "an impostor, and a liar," while John Stafford said Martin Harris was an honorable farmer.

                1.2.1.1.2.6.2.4.2. Con:
                -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.1.1.2.

              1.2.1.1.2.6.2.5. Pro:
              When Joseph Smith first met his father-in-law Isaac Hale in 1827, to gain his acceptance and help, Smith "acknowledged that he could not see in a stone now, nor never could; and that his former pretensions in that respect, were all false. He then promised to give up his old habits of digging for money and looking into stones." (Howe, 1834, pp. 234-35 [95] (statement of Peter Ingersoll))

                1.2.1.1.2.6.2.5.1. Con:
                Joseph Smith did not translate the Book of Mormon until 1828, so this is irrelevant.fairmormon.org [96]

                1.2.1.1.2.6.2.5.2. Con:
                -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.1.1.2.

              1.2.1.1.2.6.2.6. Pro:
              According to Joseph Smith's neighbor Peter Ingersoll, Smith confided in him that when he brought home a frock containing what he said were the golden plates [97], the frock actually contained several quarts of white sand.  (Howe, 1834, pp. 235-36 [98])

                1.2.1.1.2.6.2.6.1. Con:
                -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.1.1.2.

            1.2.1.1.2.6.3. Pro:
            Parley Chase, who knew the Smith family in Manchester, said of the male members of the Smith family, "Digging for money was their principal employment. In regard to their Gold Bible speculation, they scarcely ever told two stories alike." (Howe 1834, p. 248 [99])

              1.2.1.1.2.6.3.1. Con:
              -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.1.1.2.

            1.2.1.1.2.6.4. Con:
            Joseph Smith used both the Urim and Thummim [100] as well as seer stone [101]s to translate the Book of Mormon, which is consistent with both Biblical [102] and Book of Mormon [103] references to the Urim and Thummim as a transmission [104] device for divine aid.

              1.2.1.1.2.6.4.1. Pro:
              Stones are symbolically linked to Jesus Christ [105] and Peter [106] and represent strength and revelation. A seer stone is referenced in Rev 2:17 [107] as being white and containing writing which is to be given to the faithful. John saw the new Jerusalem as a clear stone [108] crystal.

            1.2.1.1.2.6.5. Pro:
            Joseph Smith falsely claimed to have translated the Book of Mormon using a set of spectacles, called the Urim and Thummim [109], that he found with the golden plates [110].

              1.2.1.1.2.6.5.1. Con:
              -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.4.

              1.2.1.1.2.6.5.2. Pro:
              In 1842, Joseph Smith described the Urim and Thummim [111] he purportedly found with the golden plates [112], and stated, "Through the medium of the Urim and Thummim I translated the record by the gift, and power of God." (Times & Seasons, 1 Mar. 1842, p. 707 [113])

              1.2.1.1.2.6.5.3. Pro:
              Joseph Smith used a seer stone [114] to produce the entire text of the Book of Mormon.

                1.2.1.1.2.6.5.3.1. Con:
                -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.4.

              1.2.1.1.2.6.5.4. Pro:
              In an 1838 church newspaper, Joseph Smith was asked, "How, and where did you obtain the book of Mormon?" His reply deceptively stated that he used the Book of Mormon "interpreters" [115] to translate the book: "I obtained [the golden plates], and the Urim and Thummim with them; and by the means of which, I translated the plates; and thus came the book of Mormon." (Elders Journal, July 1838, p. 43 [116])

                1.2.1.1.2.6.5.4.1. Con:
                This is entirely consistent with the story of translation and is thus not deceptive.

            1.2.1.1.2.6.6. Pro:
            Joseph Smith falsely claimed to have run miles while carrying the golden plates [117] from a temporary hiding place near Cumorah [118] to his home in Manchester, while at the same time fending off chasers who were trying to get them from him.

              1.2.1.1.2.6.6.1. Pro:
              According to Joseph Smith, the distance he ran with the golden plates [119] from their temporary hiding place to his home in Manchester was at least two miles.

                1.2.1.1.2.6.6.1.1. Pro:
                According to Willard Chase, when Joseph Smith went to Cumorah [120] on September 22, 1827 to recover the golden plates, "he said he then took the book out of the ground and hid it in a tree top, and returned home." (Howe 1834, p. 246 [121])

                1.2.1.1.2.6.6.1.2. Pro:
                Because Joseph Smith left his wife [122] waiting in the wagon while he climbed Cumorah [123] and purported to receive and hide the golden plates, the purported hiding place would not have been far from the hill.

                  1.2.1.1.2.6.6.1.2.1. Pro:
                  According to Willard Chase, Joseph Smith told him that hill Cumorah [124] was "a distance of thirty or forty rods from the road" where he left his wife [125] in the wagon. (Howe 1834, p. 245-46 [126])

                1.2.1.1.2.6.6.1.3. Pro:
                According to Willard Chase, about 10 days after September 22, 1827, Smith returned to the temporary hiding place of the golden plates [127], "found it safe, took off his frock, wrapt it round it, put it under his arm and run all the way home, a distance of about two miles." (Howe 1834, p. 246 [128])

                1.2.1.1.2.6.6.1.4. Pro:
                The distance from Cumorah [129] to the Smith home near Palmyra, NY was about three miles.

              1.2.1.1.2.6.6.2. Con:
              Smith was easily physically capable of running miles and fending off chasers.

                1.2.1.1.2.6.6.2.1. Pro:
                Joseph Smith was "was tall, over six feet in height [130] (possibly as much as six feet two), this being considerably above normal in size for that period of time. He possessed strong arms and legs, a muscular chest, broad shoulders, and weighed around 200 pounds."

                1.2.1.1.2.6.6.2.2. Pro:
                Joseph Smith's body was "conditioned from years of chopping wood [131], building fences, digging wells and ditches, and cutting down trees and removing stumps, as well as moving heavy rocks on the family farm."

                1.2.1.1.2.6.6.2.3. Pro:
                Skilled Wrestler: Joseph Smith beat John Brassfield, the champion wrestler in the country [132], in "the first two falls in out of a match of three." He also handily beat the champion wrestler of Daviess county after Smith was weakened from spending 4-5 months in an unsanitary prison.

                1.2.1.1.2.6.6.2.4. Pro:
                Sheer strength: Stick pulling (two men sit and pull a stick. The stronger lifts the other off the ground to win): There is no record that Smith was ever beaten. Smith beat the strongest man in the county with one hand [133] and when two men tried together against him, they did not prevail.

                1.2.1.1.2.6.6.2.5. Pro:
                Smith competed in the standing broad jump, played ball games with youth, and was athletic throughout his life [134].

                1.2.1.1.2.6.6.2.6. Con:
                Joseph Smith walked with a limp [135] because of a childhood illness.

              1.2.1.1.2.6.6.3. Pro:
              Witnesses who said they lifted the golden plates [136] estimate their weight from 30 pounds or less to about 60 pounds.

                1.2.1.1.2.6.6.3.1. Pro:
                According to Willard Chase, Joseph Smith told him about the golden plates [137] that "he should think it would weigh sixty pounds, and was sure it would weigh forty." (Howe 1834, p. 246 [138])

                1.2.1.1.2.6.6.3.2. Pro:
                Emma Smith [139] stated in 1879 to a church newspaper that while Joseph Smith was translating the golden plates [140], she moved them (wrapped in a cloth) "from place to place on the table, as it was necessary in doing my work." (Saints Herald, Aug. 15, 1879 [141])

              1.2.1.1.2.6.6.4. Pro:
              Joseph Smith told others that when he brought the golden plates [142] home, he had to physically fend off two or three assailants.

                1.2.1.1.2.6.6.4.1. Pro:
                According to Willard Chase, on Joseph Smith's return home while carrying the golden plates [143] wrapped under his arm, "he said he was attacked by two men in the woods, and knocked them both down and made his escape, arriving safe and secured his treasure." (Howe 1834, p. 246 [144])

            1.2.1.1.2.6.7. Pro:
            On at least some occasions, Joseph Smith intentionally represented a box or cloth wrapping as containing the golden plates [145], when it did not actually contain the plates.

              1.2.1.1.2.6.7.1. Pro:
              In the fall of 1827, Joseph Smith allowed William T. Hussey and Azel Vandruver to look inside the chest and see a thick canvas inside which Smith represented as covering the golden plates. After Hussey removed the canvas despite Smith's protest, the men observed a tile brick. In explanation, Smith "claimed that his friends had been sold by a trick of his." (Tucker 1867, 31-32 [146])

              1.2.1.1.2.6.7.2. Con:
              The plates were made of gold (consistent with ancient customs since discovered). Joseph had to hide their location by various means to prevent its theft.

                1.2.1.1.2.6.7.2.1. Con:
                Smith could have prevented the theft of the plates simply by refusing to say where they were, not by lying about where they were.

                1.2.1.1.2.6.7.2.2. Con:
                The golden plates [147], based on descriptions of them, could not have been made of gold as claimed.

                  1.2.1.1.2.6.7.2.2.1. Con:
                  Mormon's Plates had an appearance of gold [148] but may have been a metal called Tumbaga [149]. Tumbaga [150], when treated with citric acid, can have the appearance of gold. Tumbaga was used during the time period that Mormon and Moroni claim to have authored and stored the Book of Mormon.

                  1.2.1.1.2.6.7.2.2.2. Pro:
                  The weight of the plates more closely matches contemporary tin rather than ancient gold. The church admits [151] gold is too heavy and tin [152] has the right weight matching descriptions.

                  1.2.1.1.2.6.7.2.2.3. Pro:
                  -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.6.3.

              1.2.1.1.2.6.7.3. Pro:
              According to Governor Thomas Ford of Illinois, unnamed Mormon insiders told him that Joseph Smith showed the Eight Witnesses of the Book of Mormon an empty box and made them pray until they saw the plates in the box. (Thomas Ford, A History of Illinois, p. 257-8 [153])

                1.2.1.1.2.6.7.3.1. Con:
                This is Hearsay on steroids. 'Governor Ford said some unamed person said some unidentified member of the 8 witnesses said Joseph Smith said...something very suspicious...'

              1.2.1.1.2.6.7.4. Pro:
              -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.2.6.

              1.2.1.1.2.6.7.5. Pro:
              In 1827, Joseph Smith buried a chest purportedly containing the golden plates [154] under a hearth on his parents' property, but when local treasure seekers found the chest and discovered it empty, Smith claimed that the chest had been hiding upstairs, all along, in a barrel of beans.

              1.2.1.1.2.6.7.6. Pro:
              According to neighbor Peter Ingersoll, Joseph Smith confided in him that when he was unable to get a chest made to contain the purported golden plates [155], Smith constructed a box of clap-boards, which he wrapped in a pillowcase and allowed people to lift and feel it as if the pillowcase contained the plates. (Howe, 1834, p. 236 [156])

                1.2.1.1.2.6.7.6.1. Pro:
                In early September 1827, Joseph Smith asked Willard Chase to build him a chest on the false premise that it was designed to hold the golden plates [157] he would soon retrieve, in exchange for a share in the plates. Without any convincing evidence that the plates existed, Chase refused. (Howe 1834, p. 245 [158] (Willard Chase statement))

                  1.2.1.1.2.6.7.6.1.1. Pro:
                  Peter Ingersoll said that Joseph Smith told him "he actually went to Willard Chase, to get him to make a chest, in which he might deposit his golden Bible [159]. But, as Chase would not do it, he made a box himself." (Howe 1834, p. 236 [160])

                  1.2.1.1.2.6.7.6.1.2. Pro:
                  -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.2.1.

                  1.2.1.1.2.6.7.6.1.3. Con:
                  Willard Chase could have exaggerated much of what transpired.

                  1.2.1.1.2.6.7.6.1.4. Con:
                  -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.1.1.1.

                  1.2.1.1.2.6.7.6.1.5. Con:
                  -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.1.1.2.

                  1.2.1.1.2.6.7.6.1.6. Con:
                  -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.1.1.3.

            1.2.1.1.2.6.8. Pro:
            Joseph Smith kept the ostensible location of the stone box containing the golden plates [161] secret, even after purportedly removing the plates from the box, in order to avoid the ability to falsify his story.

              1.2.1.1.2.6.8.1. Pro:
              According to Willard Chase, Joseph Smith's father [162] said that after Smith first took the golden plates [163] out of the stone box, he "fear[ed] that some one might discover where he got it." (Howe 1834, p. 242 [164])

            1.2.1.1.2.6.9. Pro:
            Joseph Smith lied or changed his story about what, if anything, was in the stone box with the golden plates.

              1.2.1.1.2.6.9.1. Pro:
              -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.1.2.2.

              1.2.1.1.2.6.9.2. Pro:
              Early Mormon missionaries Lyman E. Johnson [165] and Orson Pratt [166] taught that the stone box in which Joseph Smith found the golden plates [167] "in thickness was about 6 inches, and about 7 by 5 otherwise." (Catholic Telegraph, Apr. 14, 1832, p. 204 [168]) This would not have been large enough to hold items other than the golden plates.

              1.2.1.1.2.6.9.3. Pro:
              -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.5.4.

              1.2.1.1.2.6.9.4. Pro:
              According to Willard Chase, Joseph Smith said he had not discovered anything in the stone box with the golden plates [169] until Samuel T. Lawrence [170] suggested (some time after Smith's 1824 annual visit to Cumorah [171]) there might be "a large pair of specks [172] with the plates," and after multiple tries with his seer stone [173], Smith said he was able to see "a pair of spectacles, the same with which Joseph says he translated the Book of Mormon." (Howe 1834, p. 243 [174]).

                1.2.1.1.2.6.9.4.1. Con:

            1.2.1.1.2.6.10. Pro:
            According to Willard Chase, not long after taking Samuel T. Lawrence to Cumorah [175], Joseph Smith "altered his mind, and said L. was not the right man [to allow him to obtain the golden plates [176]], nor had he told him the right place." (Howe 1834, p. 243 [177])

              1.2.1.1.2.6.10.1. Con:

          1.2.1.1.2.7. Pro:
          Joseph Smith and his family had a background of deception associated with divination and treasure seeking.

            1.2.1.1.2.7.1. Pro:
            Joseph Smith used the false promise of hidden treasures to obtain free work or assistance from his acquaintances.

              1.2.1.1.2.7.1.1. Pro:
              According to Willard Chase, Joseph Smith manipulated Samuel T. Lawrence [178] into paying his expenses for travel to Pennsylvania (and giving Smith a positive recommendation to his future wife Emma [179]) based on a false statement that Smith had seen with his own eyes a silver mine on the bank of the Susquehannah river that could be loaded into boats and sold in Philadelphia, and that Lawrence would have a share in the profit. (Howe 1834, p. 243-44 [180])

              1.2.1.1.2.7.1.2. Pro:
              According to Willard Chase, Josiah Stowell moved Joseph Smith and his new bride from Pennsylvania to his parents house in western New York, based on Smith's promise that he had found a large bar of gold stuck in a cave near the area, and Stowell would share the prize with him if he provided transportation. Afterwards, Smith refused to go to the cave, and Stowell returned home empty-handed. (Howe 1834, pp. 244-45 [181])

              1.2.1.1.2.7.1.3. Pro:
              -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.7.6.1.

              1.2.1.1.2.7.1.4. Con:
              Joseph likely hoped to be able to sell the gold from the plates at some point, not knowing what the Lord's final plan for the plates was.

              1.2.1.1.2.7.1.5. Con:
              Willard Chase was likely upset that Joseph wouldn't show him the plates.

              1.2.1.1.2.7.1.6. Con:
              -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.1.1.

            1.2.1.1.2.7.2. Con:
            Seer Stones have been used in the Bible. It is possible he had the same ability.

            1.2.1.1.2.7.3. Pro:
            Joseph Smith was examined before a court in 1826 and provisionally found guilty for fraudulent scrying [182], pending a later trial that Smith was able to avoid.

              1.2.1.1.2.7.3.1. Pro:
              Joseph Smith's 1826 legal proceeding was an "examination" by a justice of the peace, with the purpose of making a provisional determination of guilt or innocence and determining whether or not the accused should stand trial at a later date before the Court of Special Sessions (a tribunal of three justices).

                1.2.1.1.2.7.3.1.1. Pro:
                In reference to Joseph Smith's proceeding before his court, Justice of the Peace charges the State of New York $2.68, dated March 20, 1826, with the explanation, "To my fees in examination of the above Cause." (Trial Bill of Albert Neely, Nov. 8, 1826 [183])

                1.2.1.1.2.7.3.1.2. Pro:
                In reference to Joseph Smith's 1826 proceeding, Constable Philip De Zeng charged the State of New York for "Serving Warrant on Joseph Smith & travel," for "Subpoening 12 Wittnesses & travel," for "Attendance with Prisoner two days & 1 Night," for "Notifying two Justices," and for "16 miles travel with Mittimus to take him." (Trial Bill of Philip M. De Zeng, 1826 [184])

                1.2.1.1.2.7.3.1.3. Pro:
                The bills of Justices Tarble, Bigelow and Humphrey, who were the only three Justices of the Peace who could have joined Judge Albert Neely on a Court of Special Sessions tribunal to try Joseph Smith in 1826, do not include any bills relating to Joseph Smith. (Michael Marquardt, 2010 [185])

              1.2.1.1.2.7.3.2. Pro:
              At the conclusion of Joseph Smith's examination in 1826, Justice Albert Neely determined that Joseph Smith was guilty and should stand trial before the Court of Special Sessions.

              1.2.1.1.2.7.3.3. Pro:
              The charges against Joseph Smith in 1826 were for being a "disorderly person" under New York law as a result of fraudulent scrying [186].

                1.2.1.1.2.7.3.3.1. Pro:
                The trial bill of Justice Albert Neely captions Joseph Smith's case as "[People] vs. Joseph Smith The Glass looker," and indicates it is for a misdemeanor. (Trial Bill of Albert Neely, Nov. 9, 1826 [187])

            1.2.1.1.2.7.4. Pro:
            Joseph Smith falsely professed a supernatural ability to see things in a seer stone [188].

              1.2.1.1.2.7.4.1. Pro:
              -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.2.

              1.2.1.1.2.7.4.2. Con:
              Emma Smith's testimony: "Why my husband was translating the Book of Mormon, I wrote part of it, as he dictated each sentence, word for word, and when he came to proper names he could not pronounce, or long words, he spelled them out, and while I was writing them, if I made any mistake in spelling, he would stop me and correct my spelling, although it was impossible for him to see how I was writing them down at the time."

              1.2.1.1.2.7.4.3. Con:
              The classical view of science is based on inductive reasoning, or knowledge by observations. Since revelation through different mediums has yet to independently observed, it must be false.

                1.2.1.1.2.7.4.3.1. Pro:
                Immanuel Kant and others have noted the problem of inductive [189] reasoning is that it is deductively invalid. Case in point - all swans were said to be white until a black one was found.

                1.2.1.1.2.7.4.3.2. Pro:
                A similar unprovable theory called uniformitarianism [190] states that current observations are true and will always hold true.

              1.2.1.1.2.7.4.4. Pro:
              There is no scientific evidence supporting the efficacy of any form of scrying [191].

                1.2.1.1.2.7.4.4.1. Con:
                -> See 1.2.1.1.2.7.4.3.

              1.2.1.1.2.7.4.5. Pro:
              Joseph Smith believed it was possible that people could be deceived by the information they purportedly received from a seer stone [192].

                1.2.1.1.2.7.4.5.1. Pro:
                In 1830, Joseph Smith dictated a revelation stating that when rival prophet Hiram Page [193] wrote revelations from a seer stone [194], "those things which he hath written from that Stone are not of [God] & that Satan deceiveth him." (Revelation, September 1830-B [later D&C 28] [195])

              1.2.1.1.2.7.4.6. Pro:
              There is no credible evidence that Joseph Smith ever divined the location of buried treasure using his seer stone.

              1.2.1.1.2.7.4.7. Pro:
              Joseph Smith professed an ability to divine [196] the location of buried treasure and lost objects using a seer stone [197].

                1.2.1.1.2.7.4.7.1. Con:
                -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.1.1.

                1.2.1.1.2.7.4.7.2. Pro:
                Joseph Smith's method of divining the location of buried treasure involved placing a seer stone [198] in the bottom of his hat, and placing his face in the hat to block out the light, so that he could exercise his clairvoyance.

                  1.2.1.1.2.7.4.7.2.1. Pro:
                  According to Manchester neighbor William Stafford, Joseph Smith purported to see buried treasure "by placing a stone of singular appearance in his hat, in such a manner as to exclude all light; at which time [the Smith family] pretended he could see all things within and under the earth." (Howe, 1834, pp. 237-38 [199])

                1.2.1.1.2.7.4.7.3. Pro:
                Joseph Smith, Sr. [200] told neighbor William Stafford that his son "Joseph Jr. had been looking in his glass, and had seen, not many rods from his house, two or three kegs of gold and silver, some feet under the surface of the earth; and that none others than the elder Joseph and [Stafford] could get them." (Howe, 1834, p. 238 [201])

                  1.2.1.1.2.7.4.7.3.1. Con:
                  To paraphrase: Eber Howe said Willam Stafford said Joseph Smith, Sr. said Joseph Smith, Jr said... This degree of gossip would be impressive even in a public high school.

            1.2.1.1.2.7.5. Pro:
            Peter Ingersoll, a neighbor of the Smith family, accused Joseph Smith Sr. [202] of taking, hiding, and milking his cattle, and then using a divining rod [203] to pretend to find them. (Howe, 1834, p. 234 [204])

            1.2.1.1.2.7.6. Pro:
            -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.2.5.

            1.2.1.1.2.7.7. Pro:
            Joseph Smith and his family sought to profit by practicing magic [205] to find lost treasure.

              1.2.1.1.2.7.7.1. Pro:
              Joseph Smith, Sr. [206] purported to practice divination, often in the context of buried treasure.

                1.2.1.1.2.7.7.1.1. Con:
                -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.1.1.

                1.2.1.1.2.7.7.1.2. Pro:
                Joseph Smith, Sr. [207] attempted, unsuccessfully, to teach Peter Ingersoll how to use a divining rod [208] to find buried treasure. (Howe, 1834, p. 232 [209])

                1.2.1.1.2.7.7.1.3. Pro:
                Joseph Smith, Sr. [210] and his oldest son Alvin [211] used a seer stone [212] in a hat to locate buried treasure. (Howe, 1834, pp. 232-33 [213] (statement of Peter Ingersoll))

                1.2.1.1.2.7.7.1.4. Pro:
                Joseph Smith, Sr. [214] believed that the heat of the summer sun "caused the chests of money to rise near the top of the ground." (Howe, 1834, p. 233 [215] (statement of Peter Ingersoll))

              1.2.1.1.2.7.7.2. Pro:
              The Smith family spent much of their time in the Manchester, New York area in treasure-hunting and other magical pursuits.

                1.2.1.1.2.7.7.2.1. Pro:
                -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.3.

                1.2.1.1.2.7.7.2.2. Pro:
                According to David Stafford, who knew the Smith family in Manchester, "It is well known, that the general employment of the Smith family was money digging and fortune-telling." (Howe 1834, p. 249 [216])

                  1.2.1.1.2.7.7.2.2.1. Con:
                  Stafford's claim is contradicted by Emmons, Eaton, Dickinson and Cake, who all claim it was Joseph Smith who organized and employed the band of money diggers. Emmons, pg 101 [217], Eaton [218], Dickinson, pg 30 [219] Cake, pg 13,14 [220]

                  1.2.1.1.2.7.7.2.2.2. Con:
                  David Stafford claims the money digging was a secret activity and that Joseph Smith Sr. and two other men with hoes and shovels "on seeing me they ran like wild men to get out of my sight." Mormonism Unvailed p249 [221] which is contradicted by by Joseph Capron who says that Joseph Jr. would "often tell his neighbors of his wonderful discoveries and urge them to embark in the money digging business." Unvailed p260 [222] but then claims they were unable to pay their creditors with the treasures.

                  1.2.1.1.2.7.7.2.2.3. Con:
                  Stafford's claims as an eye witness to the Smith's treasure seeking are problematic and throw doubt on the authenticity of his statements. For example, he said the Smith's employed a "worthless gang" of treasure hunters, but then he admits he joined on one occasion to satisfy his curiosity. Later, he mentioned a second attempt at satisfying his curiosity when he claimed to donate a large, fat sheep to a money digging operation and then he didn't even go on the excursion.  Mormonism Unvailed p239 [223]

                  1.2.1.1.2.7.7.2.2.4. Con:

                1.2.1.1.2.7.7.2.4. Con:
                Two separate claims say it was actually money diggers who employed Joseph Smith. R.C. Doud who says that he was employed with a gang of 14 in 1822 by Oliver Harper and that Joseph Smith was not even present, though he was somehow guiding the operation. They were searching for hidden treasure. History of Susquehanna County p580 [224] Isaac Hale said in 1825 Joseph stayed at his house with other men employed by money diggers. Joseph was the eyes of the operation. Mormonism Unvailed p263 [225]

                1.2.1.1.2.7.7.2.5. Con:
                Eaton claims that Joseph Smith's diggers never found anything, but incredibly he was able to to keep the pointless operation going for seven or eight years. (1820 - 1827) Eaton p2 [226]

                1.2.1.1.2.7.7.2.6. Con:
                It's not believable that somehow over the course of 7 years, people continued to pay Joseph Smith, or Joseph employed others, to find treasure which was never found. He always gave a routine explanation for why he never found anything and yet no suits were ever brought against him and his employees continued to dig undeterred by failure.

                1.2.1.1.2.7.7.2.7. Con:
                Many claims have the Smiths starting their treasure hunting in different years - Mahaffey in 1817 with divining rod and seer stone, Kennedy, a peepstone in 1819, Tucker magic stone 1820, W Chase, peepstone in 1822. They can't all be right and may all be wrong as each account can't be independently verified. Mahaffey p14 [227], Kennedy p19 [228], Tucker p22 [229], Chase p240 [230]

                1.2.1.1.2.7.7.2.8. Con:
                -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.1.1.

                1.2.1.1.2.7.7.2.9. Pro:
                Neighbor Peter Ingersoll said of the Smith family that "[t]he general employment of the family, was digging for money. I had frequent invitations to join the company, but always declined being one of their number." (Howe, 1834, p. 232 [231])

                1.2.1.1.2.7.7.2.10. Pro:
                Neighbor William Stafford said of the Smith family, "A great part of their time was devoted to digging for money: especially in the night time.... I have heard them tell marvellous tales, respecting the discoveries they made in their peculiar occupation of money digging." (Howe, 1834, p. 237 [232])

                  1.2.1.1.2.7.7.2.10.1. Con:

                1.2.1.1.2.7.7.2.12. Pro:
                According to Willard Chase, the Smith family was engaged in the treasure digging business from at least 1820 to late 1827. (Howe, 1834, p. 240 [233])

              1.2.1.1.2.7.7.3. Pro:
              Joseph Smith and his family professed that magical treasure guardians protected buried treasure at numerous sites near their home in Manchester, New York.

                1.2.1.1.2.7.7.3.1. Con:
                -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.1.1.

                1.2.1.1.2.7.7.3.2. Pro:
                Joseph Smith said that during his first attempt to obtain the golden plates in 1823, he persisted in trying to get the plates after being shocked by an invisible power, because he believed the "power of enchantment, and a thousand like stories, which held the hidden treasures of the earth" was responsible for holding the plates under its spell, and could be overcome by physical exertion. (Oliver Cowdery 1835, pp. 197-98 [234]).

                1.2.1.1.2.7.7.3.3. Pro:
                The Smith family believed, with regard to buried treasure in the vicinity of Manchester, New York, that Joseph Smith could, using his seer stone [235], "discover the spirits in whose charge these treasures were, clothed in ancient dress." (Howe, 1834, p. 238 [236] (statement of William Stafford))

              1.2.1.1.2.7.7.4. Pro:

              1.2.1.1.2.7.7.5. Pro:
              Magical [237] rituals and symbols were essential elements of the treasure excavations in which Joseph Smith and his family participated.

                1.2.1.1.2.7.7.5.1. Con:
                -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.1.1.

                1.2.1.1.2.7.7.5.2. Pro:
                In one treasure digging ritual, Joseph Smith, Sr. [238] made two concentric circles using witch hazel sticks to ward off evil spirits, walked three times around the periphery muttering incantations, stuck a steel rod in the center to mark the treasure, and enjoined complete silence on the diggers, lest they arouse the guardian spirit. After consulting his son Joseph, he said the dig was unsuccessful because of an early error in the ritual. (Howe, 1823, pp. 238-39 [239] (William Stafford statement))

                1.2.1.1.2.7.7.5.3. Pro:
                According to Joseph Smith and his family, treasure digging rituals were often unsuccessful because, by supernatural forces, the treasure sunk deeper into the earth during the dig.

                  1.2.1.1.2.7.7.5.3.1. Pro:
                  According to Manchester neighbor William Stafford, one treasure dig was unsuccessful because, as Joseph Smith purportedly saw in his seer stone [240], an evil spirit "caused the money to sink" during the dig. (Howe, 1834, p. 239 [241])

                1.2.1.1.2.7.7.5.4. Pro:
                One treasure digging ritual led by the Smith family involved cutting the throat of a black sheep and leading it in a circle around the dig site while bleeding. Due to some mistake in the ritual, however, the dig was unsuccessful. (Howe, 1834, p. 239 [242] (statement of William Stafford))

              1.2.1.1.2.7.7.6. Pro:
              Joseph Smith earned more money working as a treasure seer than he would have made as a farm laborer.

                1.2.1.1.2.7.7.6.1. Pro:
                In an 1838 church newspaper, in response to the question, "Was not Jo Smith a money digger," Joseph Smith replied, "Yes, but it was never a very profitable job to him, as he only got fourteen dollars a month for it." (Elders Journal, July 1838, p. 43 [243])

                  1.2.1.1.2.7.7.6.1.1. Pro:
                  Factoring in inflation, fourteen dollars a month in 1820s corresponds to at least $300 a month today.

                  1.2.1.1.2.7.7.6.1.2. Pro:
                  In 1826, when Joseph Smith was working as a treasure seer, the average monthly earnings of a farm laborer in the New England area was $11.65 per month. (Historical Statistics of the United States, House Doc. No. 93-78, 93d Congress, 1st Session, p. 163 [244])

          1.2.1.1.2.8. Pro:
          Joseph Smith believed that honesty was served when his words were literally true, but deceptive.

            1.2.1.1.2.8.1. Con:
            It is part of basic human nature to say things that are literally true, but are calculated to intentionally mislead another human. The only case to be made here is whether Joseph did it with intent to do good or cause harm.

            1.2.1.1.2.8.2. Pro:
            On a trip from his parents home to his in-laws in Pennsylvania, Smith promised a tollkeeper that he would "hand" him the fare of 12 1/2 cents on his return trip. On the return trip, Smith handed the tollkeeper 25 cents and received 12 1/2 cents in change, without comment. Smith's explanation was that he only "agreed to 'hand' [the earlier fare] to him, and I did, but he handed it back again." (Howe, 1834, p. 235 [245] (statement of Peter Ingersoll)).

              1.2.1.1.2.8.2.1. Con:
              The ferryman had his agency and intelligence and was given the money he was owed. He gave some back to Joseph of his own free will. There is no dishonesty.

            -> See 1.2.1.1.2.4.8.

New moon and good Friday, I believe, were regarded as the most favorable time for obtaining these treasures." (Howe, 1834, p. 238 [249])

      1.2.1.2. Con:
      Joseph Smith performed and facilitated miracles.

        1.2.1.2.1. Pro:
        Joseph Smith miraculously healed the lame arm [250] of the wife of John Johnson of Hiram.

        1.2.1.2.2. Pro:
        At the dedication of the Kirtland Temple in 1836, Joseph Smith prayed that the Lord would "have mercy upon the children of Jacob, that Jerusalem, from this hour, may begin to be redeemed” (D&C 109:62 [251]). Almost immediately, a wave of events transpired that led to Judaism, formerly a minority religion, to become the clear majority religion [252].

        1.2.1.2.3. Pro:
        Joseph Smith "immediately healed" a large number of people when an illness swept through a town. en.wikipedia.org [253]

        1.2.1.2.4. Pro:
        Joseph Smith sent Wilford Woodruff to heal a ferryman's ill children when he did not have time. Woodruff was able to heal them as directed by Smith. en.wikipedia.org [254]

        1.2.1.2.5. Pro:
        Fanny Stenhouse, a disillusioned and apostate member of the Church, affirmed that she saw Joseph Smith heal an old woman who had been bedridden for years.en.wikipedia.org [255]

        1.2.1.2.6. Con:
        Joseph Smith failed in attempts to perform miracles.

          1.2.1.2.6.1. Con:
          The failure to perform a miracle does not suggest that the individual is a false prophet or that the Book of Mormon is false. Miracles, by definition, can only be performed in harmony with God's will.

          1.2.1.2.6.2. Pro:
          During an early Mormon conference, Joseph Smith took the hand of a congregant whose hand was rendered defective because of an accident, and commanded twice the man to straighten his hand, but the man was unable to comply. (Howe, 1834, p. 190 [256] (statement by Ezra Booth [257]))

          1.2.1.2.6.3. Pro:
          During an early Mormon conference, Joseph Smith approached a man with a disabled leg and commanded him in the name of Jesus Christ to walk. However, he was not healed. (Howe, 1834, p. 190 [258] (statement by Ezra Booth [259]))

      1.2.1.3. Pro:
      Joseph Smith had a poor track record as a prophet or translator.

        1.2.1.3.1. Con:
        Joseph Smith led tens of thousands of people in spiritual truth.

          1.2.1.3.1.1. Pro:
          The Holy Spirit has testified of this truth to many millions living today.

            1.2.1.3.1.1.1. Pro:
            One such testimony: gregtrimble.com [260]

              1.2.1.3.1.1.1.1. Con:
              Your source provides no evidentiary basis for his claims and fails to answer even his own questions that he requires a clear and correct response in order to support his claim.

              1.2.1.3.1.1.1.2. Pro:
              Revelation is a means of coming to know the truth. Reason and Revelation, Oaks. [261]

            1.2.1.3.1.1.2. Con:
            If the standard for truthfulness and accuracy is wide acceptance then the Hindu text are even more truthful given that they have even more followers.

            1.2.1.3.1.1.3. Con:
            This assumes that these people are hearing from God. But we cannot confirm this, they could be deceived.

              1.2.1.3.1.1.3.1. Pro:
              He could have been deceived by the devil since Satan masqueraded as an angel of light (2 Cor. 11:14 [262]) and the heart is deceitful (Jer. 17:9 [263])

            1.2.1.3.1.1.4. Pro:
            One cannot disprove that God communicates with his children.

              1.2.1.3.1.1.4.1. Con:
              It is a fallacy [264] to claim that X exists unless proven that there is no X.

            1.2.1.3.1.1.5. Con:
            The Holy Ghost has testified of the falseness of the Book of Mormon to many evangelicals.

            1.2.1.3.1.1.6. Con:
            Argument from popularity argument is a logical fallacy. If this is actual evidence then you must accept that the Quran as even more true then the Book of Mormon.

            1.2.1.3.1.1.7. Con:
            The claim that the LDS organization has 15 million members, most of whom were born into this lifestyle, and the fact LDS members do good works does not prove anything when it comes to the authenticity of the Book of Mormon.

          1.2.1.3.1.2. Pro:
          Earnest study of the Book of Mormon brings joy into people's lives beyond what any fraudulent work could.

            1.2.1.3.1.2.1. Con:
            If the Book or Mormon is fradulent it could bring as much joy to people as if it would be authentic.

            1.2.1.3.1.2.2. Con:
            People get joy from reading and studying the Quran but that doesn't mean the Quran is authentic.

            1.2.1.3.1.2.3. Con:
            Just because a book brings joy into people's lives when it is studied is not proof of its authenticity.

            1.2.1.3.1.2.4. Pro:
            Joy is a fruit of the Spirit [265] and the Holy Spirit testifies of truth [266].

        1.2.1.3.2. Con:

        1.2.1.3.3. Con:

        1.2.1.3.4. Con:

          Joseph Smith did not live to see this prophecy's fulfillment, making it more impressive.

        1.2.1.3.5. Con:

        1.2.1.3.6. Con:

        1.2.1.3.7. Pro:
        The Book of Mormon contains false prophecies.

          1.2.1.3.7.1. Con:
          Just because prophecies have yet to be fulfilled does not mean they are false.

          1.2.1.3.7.2. Con:
          Prophecies are vague and metaphorical. Therefore, claiming they are false does not create a strong argument against authenticity.

          1.2.1.3.7.3. Con:
          No scripture is perfect. Inclusion of one or more false prophecies does not speak to overall authenticity.

          1.2.1.3.7.4. Pro:
          The Book of Mormon falsely prophesies that in the last days, the Lamanites [273] would be persuaded of their purported Israelite ancestry.

            1.2.1.3.7.4.1. Pro:
            2 Nephi 3:7 [274] in the Book of Mormon prophecies that Joseph Smith's work "shall be of great worth unto [the Lamanites [275]], even to the bringing of them to the knowledge of the covenants which I have made with [the biblical Joseph [276]'s] fathers."

            1.2.1.3.7.4.2. Pro:
            2 Nephi 3:12 [277] prophecies that the bible and the Book of Mormon would result in "establishing peace among the [Lamanites [278]], and bringing them to the knowledge of their fathers in the latter days, and also to the knowledge of my covenants."

          1.2.1.3.7.5. Pro:
          The Book of Mormon falsely prophecies that those who seek to destroy Joseph Smith "shall be confounded." (2 Nephi 3:14 [279])

            1.2.1.3.7.5.1. Con:
            He was repeatedly [280] jailed and vindicated of any crimes.

            1.2.1.3.7.5.2. Pro:
            Joseph Smith was killed [281] on June 27, 1844 by people seeking to destroy him.

          1.2.1.3.7.6. Pro:
          The Book of Mormon falsely prophecies that in the last days, a "mighty" descendant of Joseph [282] (a character in the Book of Mormon) would rise up and "work mighty wonders...unto the bringing to pass much restoration unto the house of Israel, and unto the [Lamanites [283]]." (2 Nephi 3:23-24 [284])

            1.2.1.3.7.6.1. Con:
            The last days have not ended; therefore this prophecy cannot (yet) be considered false.

          1.2.1.3.7.7. Pro:
          The Book of Mormon falsely prophecies that nobody will come to the Americas "save they shall be brought by the hand of the Lord." (2 Nephi 1:6 [285])

            1.2.1.3.7.7.1. Con:
            All of man's wisdom, understanding, and ability to act comes from God, so the statement is true.

            1.2.1.3.7.7.2. Con:
            Lehi was brought to the Americas by the power of God, yet they built and sailed the ship themselves. God helps those who help themselves.

            1.2.1.3.7.7.3. Con:
            Christopher Columbus believed that in his discoveries he was an instrument [286] in God's hands.

        1.2.1.3.8. Pro:
        Joseph Smith claimed to translate the Kinderhook Plates [287], which turned out to be a hoax.

          1.2.1.3.8.1. Con:
          The evidence that Joseph Smith claimed to translate the Kinderhook plates is now known to have come from the journal of William Clayton. This excerpt was printed in the Times and Seasons newspaper in 1859. No known translation exists and Joseph Smith did not leave any documentation to verify the claim that he translated the Kinderhook plates. Kinderhook plates [288]

          1.2.1.3.8.2. Pro:
          A more in-depth discussion of the kinderhook plates can be found here [289] which contain multiple references to the fake plates including documents from the church itself [290]. Another link with additional links is here [291].

        1.2.1.3.10. Pro:
        Joseph Smith was not able to independently re-produce two purported translations of the same source material.

          1.2.1.3.10.1. Pro:
          Joseph Smith's ability to translate ancient documents is undermined by the fact that passages in the Book of Mormon which were purportedly derived from an ancient Old Testament manuscript [299], circa 600 BCE, are different from a re-translation of the bible that he undertook after the Book of Mormon was published.

            1.2.1.3.10.1.1. Con:
            Joseph's translation of the Book of Mormon was from the original language on the plates.  His translation of the same Bible passage was a "re-translation" of the KJV. Since both sources were different they resulted in slightly different wording.

          1.2.1.3.10.2. Con:
          Joseph never attempted to retranslate the lost 116 [300] manuscript pages. He was actually forbidden to do so by God.

          1.2.1.3.10.3. Con:
          -> See 1.2.1.3.10.1.1.

          1.2.1.3.10.4. Pro:
          Joseph Smith provided his own translation of Matthew 26 [301] twice, and the two versions contain substantial differences. (Jackson & Jasinski, BYU Studies 42:2 [302])

        1.2.1.3.11. Pro:
        The Book of Abraham [303] is not a translation of its purported source papyri (pg.704 [304]).

          1.2.1.3.11.1. Pro:
          Joseph Smith's ability to translate ancient Egyptian papyri into the book of scripture called the Pearl of Great Price are dubious. Modern Egyptologists [305] have translated from the papyri he used and it is clear they are not about Abraham.

        1.2.1.3.12. Pro:
        Joseph Smith held false notions about the nature of Egyptian [306] and other ancient languages.

          1.2.1.3.12.1. Pro:
          Looking in Joseph Smit's book Egyptian Alphabet and Grammar [307] it is clear that he is deriving whole phrases from single letters of the papyrus.

            1.2.1.3.12.1.1. Pro:
            At the time the main assumption had been that individual hieroglyphs symbolized ideas (some what like Chinese). It was later shown to be, like Arabic writing, a consonant only alphabet where the vowel sounds are inferred. As such multiple characters are needed to form a single word.

      1.2.4.3. Pro:
      Joseph Smith was imprisoned many times for his belief in the authenticity of the Book of Mormon and its implications.

        1.2.4.3.1. Pro:
        Joseph Smith was arrested 1838 and imprisioned in Liberty Jail.lds.org [314]

          1.2.4.3.1.1. Pro:
          "[T]he Prophet and his companions languished under inhumane conditions. They were confined to the jail’s dungeon—a dark, cold, and unsanitary cellar—and given food so bad that they could not eat until driven to it by hunger."lds.org [315]

        In 1838, Joseph Smith and other Church leaders were arrested for defending themselves against mob violence.History of the Church, 3:294 [316]

        1.2.4.3.3. Pro:
        In 1844, Joseph Smith was arrested and jailed in Carthage, Illinois, where he would be martyred by a mob while under government protection.lds.org [317]

        1.2.4.3.4. Con:
        Joseph Smith was imprisoned [318] many times for illegal activities.

          1.2.4.3.4.1. Con:
          Almost every arrest was supposedly due to "illegal activities" which he was never found [319] guilty of and is well laid out in en.wikipedia.org [320].

            1.2.4.3.4.1.1. Con:
            Joseph Smith appears to have fled [321] before any decision could be rendered for a number of charges brought against him.

      1.2.4.4. Pro:
      Some of Joseph's children were killed as a result of mob activity against him.

      1.2.4.5. Con:
      The logic that an individual made personal sacrifices for their cause could also be consistent with someone who is motivated by other reasons such as power or greed. Sacrifice does not equal belief.

        1.2.4.5.1. Con:
        When Elder Holland pointed out that Joseph Smith turned to the Book of Mormon for comfort right before his Martyrdom, it is clear his sacrifice was motivated by belief.

          1.2.4.5.1.1. Con:
          Holland did not know Smith. His speculations into Smith's intentions or feelings are simply his speculations.
