    1.2.5. Con:
    Almost all accounts of those who personally knew Joseph Smith, whether believers in the Book of Mormon or not, said his character was impeccable.

      1.2.5.1. Pro:
      Almost all accounts of those who personally knew Joseph Smith and who were never members of the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints reported that his character was impeccable.

        1.2.5.1.1. Pro:
        "By any measurement, Joseph Smith was a remarkable person. His combination of organizational acumen with spiritual originality and personal decorum and modesty is rare in the history of religion...he was exceptionally humble." - Stephen H. Webb [323] (who was not a member of the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints)

        1.2.5.1.2. Pro:
        "[Joseph Smith] is a man of the highest order of talent and great independence of character--firm in his integrity--and devoted to his religion" James Gordon Bennett [324], a New York Herald writer in 1842.

        1.2.5.1.3. Pro:
        "[Joseph Smith] came into my neighborhood, being then about eighteen years of age, and resided there two years; during which time I became intimately acquainted with him. I do know that his character was irreproachable; that he was well known for truth and uprightness; that he moved in the first circles of the community, and he was often spoken of as a young man of intelligence and good morals." Attorney John S. Reed [325] (non-member of the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints).

        1.2.5.1.4. Pro:
        "He is kind and obliging, generous and benevolent, sociable and cheerful, and is possessed of a mind of a contemplative and reactive character. He is honest, frank, fearless and independent, and as free from dissimulation as any man to be found." Dr. John M. Bernhisel [326], a boarder in Joseph Smith's house for nine months.

        1.2.5.1.5. Con:
        People who became disaffected and disillusioned with the Church of LDS is a category which necessarily includes many who became sceptical of Joseph Smith's character. Excluding these people will disproportionately highlight positive reports.

        1.2.5.1.6. Con:
        A sample of opinions which excludes Joseph Smith's critics within the LDS will show an artificially high proportion of positive opinions. Therefore, that sample is not meaningful.

          1.2.5.1.6.1. Con:
          This sample is meaningful. It excludes only a handful of disgruntled excommunicated members. It also excludes a city's worth of devoted Church members who trusted Joseph Smith's integrity and uprightness implicitly.

        1.2.5.1.7. Pro:
        A sample of non-church members who personally knew Joseph Smith is probably the most meaningful and objective sample available.

      1.2.5.2. Pro:
      Joseph Smith's associates recognized he was a largely uneducated and imperfect person, but trusted and respected his character.

        1.2.5.2.1. Pro:
        "[Joseph Smith was] like other men, as the prophets and apostles of old, liable to errors and mistakes which were not inspired from heaven, but managed by...[his] own judgment." Parley P. Pratt [327]

        1.2.5.2.2. Pro:
        "Though I admitted in my feelings and knew all the time that Joseph was a human being and subject to err, still it was none of my business to look after his faults." Brigham Young [328] and "I am bold to say that, Jesus Christ, excepted, no better man ever lived or does live upon this earth." Brigham Young [329]

    1.2.6. Pro:
    The notorious Nauvoo Expositor [330] was produced by Joseph Smith’s former followers, and detailed accounts of his deceitfulness and sexual predations.

      1.2.6.1. Con:
      As described in the Book of Mormon people that leave the church (Like the people of Ammonihah [331]) have a tendency to become very deplorable people. The people at Ammonihah burn scriptures and slay believers. Like Judas, you can expect people that leave truth to hate it and make up all sorts of Charges and writings.

    1.2.7. Con:
    Joseph Smith’s character or reliability are superfluous matters. We have the purported text ready to be analyzed. Just as one does not determine ultimately if a rock is or is not a diamond based on the claims or reliability of the person who initially brought the rock forward. All details about how the rock came to be are subservient details to chemical analysis of the rock. We have swaths of ancient texts and we have an emerging statistical science of textual analytics. Let the book speak.

      1.2.7.1. Con:
      The lack of any persisting physical evidence of an original version of the book of Mormon having ever existed makes Smith's character as one of only a handful of witnesses (mostly non expert family members and friends to Smith) claiming to have seen the original plates relevant to the discussion of whether or not the book is authentic.

    1.2.8. Pro:
    -> See 1.2.1.1.2.

    1.2.9. Pro:
    Joseph Smith did not obtain or translate the golden plates [332] in the way he claimed.

      1.2.9.1. Pro:
      -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.2.

      1.2.9.2. Pro:
      The Book of Mormon text is not consistent with Joseph Smith’s description of a precise, word-for-word translation method.

        1.2.9.2.1. Pro:
        The original Book of Mormon transcript contains grammatical errors.

          1.2.9.2.1.1. Con:
          There are rather few faults in Book of Mormon grammar, even in the early manuscripts.

        1.2.9.2.2. Pro:
        Joseph Smith edited the Book of Mormon to change the meaning of certain passages as they appeared in Smith’s original dictation.

        1.2.9.2.3. Pro:
        In descriptions by witnesses of Joseph’s Smith’s translation process for the Book of Mormon, it was a matter of mechanically reading words exactly as they appeared in Joseph Smith's seer stone [333].

          1.2.9.2.3.1. Pro:
          David Whitmer [334], who witnessed Joseph Smith dictate the Book of Mormon by looking at a stone in his hat, stated that Smith would see each character from the golden plates [335], along with its English interpretation, on "[a] piece of something resembling parchment," and that it would remain there until the scribe correctly recorded and repeated the translation back to Smith, after which the next character and its translation would appear (Whitmer 1887, p. 12 [336]).

          1.2.9.2.3.2. Pro:
          Joseph Knight [337], one of Joseph Smith's early confidants, said that when Smith looked at the stone to translate the Book of Mormon, "he would take a sentance and it would apper in Brite Roman Letters. Then he would tell the writer and he would write it. Then that would go away the next sentance would Come and so on. But if it was not Spelt rite it would not go away till it was rite, so we see it was marvelous. Thus was the hol [whole] translated." (Jessee, BYU Studies Q.17:1, p. 4 [338])

          1.2.9.2.3.3. Pro:
          Martin Harris [339], a Book of Mormon scribe, said when Joseph Smith dictated his translation of the golden plates [340], "sentences would appear and were read by the Prophet and written by Martin, and when finished he would say, 'Written,' and if correctly written, the sentence would disappear and another appear in its place; but if not written correctly it remained until corrected, so that the translation was just as it was engraven on the plates, precisely in the language then used." (Stevenson 1882 [341])

      1.2.9.3. Con:
      Consistent with Joseph Smith's claim that the Book of Mormon was translated with divine help, Emma Smith stated that Joseph never went back to review a translated passage. As Joseph began translation on a given day, he never asked Oliver to review where he had left off the previos day. Elder Neal A. Maxwell, "By the Gift and Power of God." [342]

      1.2.9.4. Con:
      An Impressive First Draft: The Book of Mormon was published without ever having been reviewed, yet apart from minor grammatical changes, it has remained virtually unchanged for over 180 years.

        1.2.9.4.1. Con:
        Changes have been made to the text that effect far more than just grammar. [343] In some cases edits have been made that affect core doctrinal teachings, such as the nature of the Godhead.

      1.2.9.5. Pro:
      There is extensive published evidence [344] that Joseph Smith plagiarized much of the "Golden Bible" from a romance novel written but unpublished by Rev. Solomon Spaulding.

        1.2.9.5.1. Con:
        The Solomon-Spaulding theory is untenable [345].

          1.2.9.5.1.1. Pro:
          This theory claims that Sidney Rigdon wrote the religious portions of the Book of Mormon. This is impossible for an number of reasons. Eber D. Howe, Mormonism Unvailed (Painesville, Ohio: Telegraph Press, 1834). [346]

            1.2.9.5.1.1.1. Pro:
            Sydney Rigdon did not meet Joseph Smith until after the Book of Mormon was published.

              1.2.9.5.1.1.1.1. Pro:
              Emma Smith, Joseph Smith's wife, said “no acquaintance was formed between Sidney Rigdon and the Smith family till after the Church was organized” and the Book of Mormon had already been published. Joseph Smith, III, “The Spaulding Story Reexamined,” 11, tract published in Saints’ Herald, 17 March 1883.

            1.2.9.5.1.1.2. Pro:
            Sidney Rigdon did not see the Book of Mormon until after it was published.

              1.2.9.5.1.1.2.1. Pro:
              Sidney Rigdon saw a copy of the Book of Mormon for the first time when Parley P. Pratt personally gave him one after 15 October 1830 [347], well after the Book's March 1830 initial publication.

          1.2.9.5.1.2. Pro:
          This theory claims that the historical parts of the Book of Mormon were plagiarized from an unpublished manuscript written in 1812 by Solomon Spaulding that Sidney Rigdon secretly acquired. Howe, Mormonism Unveiled, 290 [348]. This is false for a number of reasons.

            1.2.9.5.1.2.1. Pro:
            Philastus Hubert secured the Solomon Spaulding manuscript he thought Rigdon and Smith plagiarized. When he examined it, he saw it was clear that the Book of Mormon was not plagiarized from Spaulding's work. He therefore concluded that there was a "lost" manuscript that Smith and Rigdon used. Howe, Mormonism Unveiled, 290 [349]

              1.2.9.5.1.2.1.1. Pro:
              Another version of the story claims that the "lost" manuscript was stolen [350].

            1.2.9.5.1.2.2. Pro:
            Hubert, the author of the Spaulding theory, heard first about the Manuscript from the Jackson family, friends of the late Solomon Spaulding. Hubert asked Mr. Jackson to sign an affidavit showing similarities between Spaulding's manuscript and the Book of Mormon. Jackson refused, stating "there is no agreement between them" and "express[ing] his indignation and contempt" [351] for what Hubert was doing.

            1.2.9.5.1.2.3. Pro:
            L.L. Rice unknowingly purchased the Spaulding manuscript in 1834. Rice, the president of Oberlin College and several others compared the manuscript to the Book of Mormon and stated that they "could detect no resemblance between the two, in general or detail.” New York Observer, 5 February 1885.

            1.2.9.5.1.2.4. Pro:
            After comparing the Spaulding manuscript and the Book of Mormon, James Fairchild, the Oberlin College President, concluded "the theory of the origin of the Book of Mormon in the traditional manuscript of Solomon Spaulding will probably have to be relinquished.” New York Observer, 5 February 1885.

            1.2.9.5.1.2.5. Con:
            Oliver Cowdery firmly denied [352] these allegations in a conference.(p.278)

          1.2.9.5.1.3. Con:
          The Solomon Spaulding plagiarism theory is not touted by informed critics of the Book of Mormon today, and is therefore being employed as a straw man argument here. See this list of many possible influences on the BofM text [353], and note the absence of the Solomon Spaulding manuscript.

      1.2.9.6. Con:
      Joseph Smith did not make use of any notes while dictating the Book of Mormon to scribes.

        1.2.9.6.1. Pro:
        By his wife Emma's testimony, Joseph dictated with his head in his hat for hours at a time and was in view of her. fairmormon.org [354]

        1.2.9.6.2. Con:
        While dictating the Book of Mormon, Joseph Smith would at least on some occasions put up a curtain between himself and others in the room.

          1.2.9.6.2.1. Con:
          Even if Joseph had opportunity to consult a Bible, this argument fails to provide any explanation for the other 93% of the Book of Mormon.

          1.2.9.6.2.2. Pro:
          Martin Harris [355] told another resident of Palmyra, NY, that when he was Joseph Smith's scribe for the Book of Mormon, "a thick curtain or blanket was suspended between them, and Smith concealed behind the blanket," and Harris was commanded not to look at Smith during the translation. (Clark, 1842, pp. 230-31 [356])

          1.2.9.6.2.3. Pro:
          David Whitmer [357], a scribe for the Book of Mormon, told an interviewer in 1886 that Joseph Smith "was separated from the scribe by a blanket." (Nathan A. Tanner Jr. to Nathan A. Tanner, Feb. 17, 1909, p. 5 [LDS Archives])

          1.2.9.6.2.4. Pro:
          As reported in an 1831 newspaper in Palmyra, NY, Martin Harris [358] said that "when he acted as amanuenes, and wrote the translation, as Smith dictated, such with his fear of the Divine displeasure that a screen (sheet) was suspended between the prophet and himself." (Palmyra Reflector, Mar. 19, 1831, p. 126) [359])

          1.2.9.6.2.5. Pro:
          According to Martin Harris [360], one of the Book of Mormon, at one time during the translation, "the presence of the Lord was so great, that a screen was hung up between him and the Prophet [Joseph Smith]." (Howe 1834, p. 14 [361])

          1.2.9.6.2.6. Pro:
          According to a resident of Palmyra, Joseph Smith translated from behind a blanket or curtain while Oliver Cowdery [362] acted as scribe. (Tucker 1867, p. 36 [363], 49 [364])

          1.2.9.6.2.7. Pro:
          According to the typesetter of the Book of Mormon who lived in Palmyra, NY, Joseph Smith dictated the book to scribeOliver Cowdery [365] while "concealed behind a curtain." (John Gilbert, Detroit Post, 1877, p. 715 [366])

          1.2.9.6.2.8. Pro:
          According to one of the printing staff for the first publication of the Book of Mormon, Martin Harris [367] said that during translation, "[Joseph] Smith and the plates containing the original of the Mormon Bible were hid from view of the scribe and Martin Harris by a screen." (Lin, 1902, p. 48 [368] [letter from Albert Chandler])

          1.2.9.6.2.9. Pro:
          Martin Harris [369] once told his fellow stagecoach travelers that while translating the Book of Mormon, "[Joseph] Smith had a sheet put up in one corner & went behind it from observation when he was writing the bible."William S. Sayre to James T. Cobb., Aug. 31, 1878, Vogel, Early Mormon Documents, vol. 4, p. 145)

          1.2.9.6.2.10. Pro:
          According to interviews of people around Harmony, Pennsylvania [370], during translation, "The Prophet [Joseph Smith] and his precious trust were screened even from the sight of his clerks by blankets nailed to the walls. The nails remained for many years just as they were driven by the Prophet, and it was not until some repairing was done a short time ago that they were drawn out." ("Mormon History," The Republican, July 29, 1880 [371])

          1.2.9.6.2.11. Pro:
          According to Joseph Smith's adjoining neighbor in Pennsylvania while Smith produced the Book of Mormon, "The little low chamber in Smith's house was used as a translating-room. The prophet and his plates were screened even from the sight of his scribes, Martin Harris, Oliver Cowdery and Reuben Hale, by blankets secured with nails." (Mather, Lippincott's Magazine 26:152, 1880, p. 201 [372])

        1.2.9.6.3. Con:
        While dictating the Book of Mormon, Joseph Smith would occasionally locate himself out of view  while calling out the dictation to his scribe in a different room.

          1.2.9.6.3.1. Con:
          -> See 1.2.9.6.2.1.

          1.2.9.6.3.2. Pro:
          According to Martin Harris [373], one of the Book of Mormon scribes, sometimes "the Prophet [Joseph Smith] would sit in a different room, or up stairs, while the Lord was communicating to him the contents of the plates." (Howe 1834, p. 14 [374])

        1.2.9.6.4. Con:
        Joseph Smith likely had an eidetic memory for passages of text.

          1.2.9.6.4.1. Con:
          -> See 1.2.9.6.2.1.

          1.2.9.6.4.2. Pro:
          Joseph Smith's father [375] had an eidetic memory.

            1.2.9.6.4.2.1. Pro:
            William Hyde, one of the neighbors of Joseph Smith in Palmyra, NY, said of Joseph Smith, Sr. [376], "There wasn't a subject he couldn't discuss intelligently, and my opinion of him was high. His memory was something extraordinary. He could repeat several chapters of a book verbatim after it had been read rapidly." (Chicago Times, Oct. 14, 1888 [377])

          1.2.9.6.4.3. Pro:
          Joseph Smith said that he did not need to use seer stones to dictate the revelation that became D&C 132 [378], because "he knew the revelation perfectly from beginning to end." (History of the Church, vol. 5, p. XXXII [379])

          1.2.9.6.4.4. Pro:
          Early in Joseph Smith's translation of the Book of Mormon, people suspected him of memorizing material beforehand and then merely repeating it while looking at the stone in the presence of Martin Harris [380]. (LDS Millennial Star 48, p. 390 [381])

        1.2.9.6.5. Con:
        According to his scribe David Whitmer [382], Joseph Smith would occasionally say during the translation that his seer stone was not working, claiming that he needed to be alone in prayer for a while, after which he would return to find the seer stone working again. (The Saints Herald, vol. 33, p. 706 [383])

          1.2.9.6.5.1. Con:
          -> See 1.2.9.6.2.1.

      1.2.9.7. Pro:
      The story of Joseph Smith obtaining and translating the golden plates [384] is not credible because it includes magic [385] and occultism [386].

        1.2.9.7.1. Pro:
        Joseph Smith purported to find the location of the golden plates using a seer stone [387].

          1.2.9.7.1.1. Pro:
          Willard Chase said that Joseph Smith "observed that if it had not been for that stone, (which he acknowledged belonged to me,) he would not have obtained the [golden plates [388]]." (Howe 1834, p. 246 [389])

            1.2.9.7.1.1.1. Pro:
            Joseph Smith, his brother Alvin, and Willard Chase found Joseph Smith's brown seer stone while digging a well on the Chase property in September 1822.

              1.2.9.7.1.1.1.1. Pro:
              Willard Chase understood that the seer stone [390] that Joseph Smith used to purportedly translate the Book of Mormon was the same stone he had obtained on the Chase property in 1822. (Howe, 1834, p. 247 [391])

              1.2.9.7.1.1.1.2. Pro:
              According to Willard Chase, he hired Joseph Smith and his brother Alvin in 1822 to dig a well, and Chase found a seer stone [392] lying 20 feet below the surface, which Joseph borrowed for scrying purposes. By the fall of 1826, Smith had refused to return it, and its ownership was a source of dispute. (Howe 1834, pp. 240-42, 246-47 [393])

        1.2.9.7.2. Pro:
        Joseph Smith purported to translate the Book of Mormon using the same scrying [394] method he used, unsuccessfully, to divine the location of buried treasure in money-digging ventures.

          1.2.9.7.2.1. Pro:
          -> See 1.2.1.1.2.7.4.

          1.2.9.7.2.2. Pro:
          -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.5.3.

          1.2.9.7.2.3. Pro:
          Joseph Smith used the same brown seer stone [395] to translate the Book of Mormon as he had previously used when he was hired to divine the location of buried treasure.

            1.2.9.7.2.3.1. Pro:
            For translating the Book of Mormon, Joseph Smith used a chocolate colored seer stone [396] currently owned by the LDS Church. (see photos [397])

            1.2.9.7.2.3.2. Pro:
            -> See 1.2.9.7.1.1.1.

          1.2.9.7.2.4. Pro:
          -> See 1.2.1.1.2.7.4.7.2.

          1.2.9.7.2.5. Pro:
          Joseph Smith purported to translate the Book of Mormon by placing a seer stone [398] at the bottom of a hat, placing his face in the hat to block out the light, and observing the translation in the reflections on the stone.

            1.2.9.7.2.5.1. Pro:
            Michel Moore's account, "when Joseph was translating the Book of Mormon, he, (Morse), had occasion more than once to go into his immediate presence, and saw him engaged at his work of translation. The mode of procedure consisted in Joseph's placing the Seer Stone in the crown of a hat, then putting his face into the hat, so as to entirely cover his face...dictating, word for word, while the scribe - Emma, John Whitmer, O. Cowdery, or someone else, wrote it down. "

        1.2.9.7.3. Pro:
        In order to successfully obtain the golden plates [399], Joseph Smith told others that he had to follow a strict set of magical performances.

          1.2.9.7.3.1. Pro:
          Joseph Smith told his family that to obtain the golden plates, the guardian of the plates strictly required him to wear particular clothing to the place that the plates were buried.

            1.2.9.7.3.1.1. Pro:
            When retelling Joseph Smith's story of the golden plates in June 1827, his father [400] said that the guardian spirit [401] required Smith to wear black clothing to the location where the plates were buried. Thus, Smith's family "fitted out Joseph with a suit of black clothes." (Howe, 1834, p. 242 [402] ; Historical Magazine, May 1870 , p. 305 [403])

          1.2.9.7.3.2. Pro:
          Joseph Smith told others that to successfully obtain the golden plates, he had to take a black horse to the place where the plates were buried.

            1.2.9.7.3.2.1. Pro:
            On September 22, 1827, Joseph Smith took a one-horse wagon to the hill Cumorah [404] to ostensibly retrieve the golden plates [405].

              1.2.9.7.3.2.1.1. Pro:
              According to Willard Chase, Joseph Smith told him that "on the 22d of September [1827], he arose early in the morning, and took a one horse wagon, of some one [406] that had stayed over night at their house, without leave or license; and, together with his wife [407], repaired to the hill which contained the book. He left his wife in the wagon, by the road, and went alone to the hill" where he retrieved the golden plates [408]. (Howe 1834, pp. 245-46 [409])

            1.2.9.7.3.2.2. Pro:
            When retelling Joseph Smith's story of the golden plates [410] in June 1827, his father [411] said that the guardian spirit [412] required Smith to be "rid[e] a black horse with a switch tail" to the location of the buried plates. Thus, he "borrowed a black horse" to approach the designated location. (Howe, 1834, p. 242 [413] (Willard Chase statement))

            1.2.9.7.3.2.3. Pro:
            Some time before September 22, 1827, Joseph Smith told the family of one of his neighbors that "he must get a black horse or a mule to go & get the plates." (Edmund L. Kelley interview with Lorenzo Saunders, 1884 [414])

            1.2.9.7.3.2.4. Pro:
            Joseph Smith's father [415] told two Palmyra residents in 1830 that Joseph Smith rode a horse to a certain tree shown to him by a man in a dream [416], and then after dismounting the horse, followed a sequence of designated objects leading along a path to the location of the golden plates [417]. (Historical Magazine, May 1870 [418] (statement by Fayette Lapham))

          1.2.9.7.3.3. Pro:
          Joseph Smith told his family that in order to obtain the golden plates [419], the guardian spirit [420] strictly required Smith to call for the plates by a certain name. (Howe 1834, p. 242 [421] (statement of Willard Chase))

          1.2.9.7.3.4. Pro:
          Joseph Smith told others that his failure to obtain the golden plates on the first attempt in 1823 is because he failed to obey the angel's strict commandments in every particular. (Katherine Smith Salisbury 1895 [422], p. 14; Oliver Cowdery 1835 [423], pp. 197-98; William Smith 1883 [424], p. 10; Joseph Smith 1832, p. 5 [425]; Joseph Knight 1833 [426], p. 2; Lucy Mack Smith 1852, p. 85 [427])

            1.2.9.7.3.4.1. Pro:
            Joseph Smith's father [428] said a spirit [429] told Smith that after picking up the golden plates [430], "he must go directly away, and neither lay it down nor look behind him." After retrieving the plates, "fearing some one might discover where he got it, he laid it down to place back the top stone, as he found it"; however, the book returned to the closed box and Smith could not take it out again. The spirit explained, "you have not obeyed your orders." (Howe 1834, p. 242 [431] (Willard Chase statement))

          1.2.9.7.3.5. Pro:
          Joseph Smith told others that after his first unsuccessful attempt to obtain the golden plates, the angel guarding them required him to bring the "right person" in order to succeed in the future. He only succeeded in obtaining the plates when he brought the "right person," his new wife Emma, in 1827. (Joseph Knight 1833, p. 2; Katherine Smith Salisbury 1895 [432], p. 14; Fayette Lapham 1870 [433], p. 307)

            1.2.9.7.3.5.1. Pro:
            According to Joseph Smith, the first "right person" that would have unlocked his ability to obtain the golden plates [434] was his brother Alvin [435]. However, because Alvin died before Smith's second annual visit to the hill Cumorah in 1824, that attempt to obtain the plates failed.

              1.2.9.7.3.5.1.1. Pro:
              According to Willard Chase, after failing to obtain the golden plates [436] on his first try, Joseph Smith told his father that Smith inquired to the spirit [437] "when he could have them, and was answered thus: come one year from this day, and bring with you your oldest brother [438], and you shall have them."  However, the spirit did not give Smith the plates because "[b]efore the expiration of the year, his oldest brother died; which the old man said was an accidental providence!" (Howe, 1834, pp. 242-43 [439])

            1.2.9.7.3.5.2. Pro:
            Joseph Smith took Samuel T. Lawrence [440] with him on his third annual visit to the hill Cumorah [441] in 1825, and claimed that Lawrence was the "right person" that would unlock Smith's ability to obtain the golden plates [442].

              1.2.9.7.3.5.2.1. Pro:
              According to Willard Chase, on Joseph Smith's second annual attempt to obtain the golden plates [443], the spirit [444] "commanded him to come again, in just one year, and bring a man with him" that Smith would know when he saw him. Smith believed the man was Samuel T. Lawrence [445], and "went with him to a singular looking hill, in Manchester, and shewed him where the treasure was." Smith later changed his mind about Lawrence. (Howe 1834, p. 243 [446])

                1.2.9.7.3.5.2.1.1. Pro:
                -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.1.2.7.1.

              1.2.9.7.3.5.2.2. Pro:
              -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.10.

            1.2.9.7.3.5.3. Pro:
            Joseph Smith's bride Emma [447] was the "right person" that, according to Smith's story, ultimately unlocked his ability to obtain the golden plates [448] on September 22, 1827.

              1.2.9.7.3.5.3.1. Pro:
              -> See 1.2.9.7.3.2.1.1.

              1.2.9.7.3.5.3.2. Pro:
              Some time before 1827, Joseph Smith told a neighbor family that "[t]he Angel [449] told him he must go & get him a wife [450] & then he could take his wife & go & get the plates [451]." (Edmund L. Kelley interview with Lorenzo Saunders, 1884 [452])

          1.2.9.7.3.6. Pro:
          -> See 1.2.1.1.2.7.7.5.

          1.2.9.7.3.7. Pro:
          -> See 1.2.9.7.3.4.1.

          1.2.9.7.3.8. Pro:
          Joseph Smith told others that to successfully obtain the golden plates, he had to be in a proper mental state.

            1.2.9.7.3.8.1. Pro:
            Joseph Smith told one of his Palmyra acquaintances that to obtain the golden plates, the angel guarding them required him to "give thanks to God," and that because on his first visit to the site he had forgotten this requirement, he was struck by lightning and unable to obtain the plates. (N.Y. Herald 1893 [453] (based on interview with Benjamin Saunders))).

            1.2.9.7.3.8.2. Pro:
            According to Joseph Smith, the angel guarding the golden plates told him that in order to successfully obtain the golden plates, his mind must be free of any thought of monetary gain, and that his first attempt to obtain the plates was unsuccessful because he failed that requirement. (Smith 1832, p. 5 [454]; Smith 1838, p. 6 [455]).

        1.2.9.7.4. Pro:
        The Book of Mormon portrays Joseph Smith's manner of translating the Book of Mormon in terms consistent with necromancy [456].

          1.2.9.7.4.1. Pro:
          2 Nephi 26:16 [457] portrays the Book of Mormon translation as follows: "For [the book's authors] shall speak unto [Lamanites [458]] out of the ground, and their speech shall be low out of the dust, and their voice shall be as one that hath a familiar spirit [459]; for the Lord God will give unto [Joseph Smith] power, that he may whisper concerning them, even as it were out of the ground; and their speech shall whisper out of the dust." (c.f. Isaiah 29:4 [460])

            1.2.9.7.4.1.1. Con:
            It's difficult to accept the veracity of a text when the primary evidence for said text is the text itself.

          1.2.9.7.4.2. Pro:
          In Adam Clarke's commentary to Isaiah 29:4 [461], he writes: "The pretenders to the art of necromancy [462], who were chiefly women, had an art of speaking with a feigned voice...they could make the voice seem to come from beneath the ground."

        1.2.9.7.5. Pro:
        Joseph Smith's first understanding of Moroni [463], the angel guarding the golden plates [464], was as a treasure guardian of the type commonly found in magical lore.

          1.2.9.7.5.1. Pro:
          In early versions of the story of Joseph Smith obtaining the golden plates [465], Smith described Moroni [466] as a shape-shifting animal, which was a common type of treasure guardian in magical lore.

            1.2.9.7.5.1.1. Pro:
            In an early version of the story about Joseph Smith finding the golden plates [467], when Smith opened the box purportedly containing the plates, "[h]e saw in the box something like a toad, which soon assumed the appearance of a man, and struck him on the side of his head." (Howe, 1834, p. 242 [468] (statement of Willard Chase))

            1.2.9.7.5.1.2. Pro:
            In an early version of the story about the golden plates [469], Joseph Smith told Benjamin Saunders, "I was about to remove the plates when an enormous toad appeared, squatting upon the pages.... Instantly the beast arose and expanded as large as a dog, then as a bullock, then it rose far above me, a flaming monster with glittering eyes, until it seemed to fill the heavens, and with a blow like lightning it swept me from the mountain into the valley beneath." (New York Herald, June 25, 1893, p. 12 [470])

          1.2.9.7.5.2. Pro:
          -> See 1.2.1.1.2.7.7.3.

          1.2.9.7.5.3. Pro:
          Moroni [471] was just one of several treasure guardians that Joseph Smith claimed to see through his powers as a seer.

            1.2.9.7.5.3.1. Pro:
            -> See 1.2.1.1.2.7.7.3.3.

          1.2.9.7.5.4. Pro:
          Early versions of the golden plates [472] story describe Moroni [473] as a spirit.

            1.2.9.7.5.4.1. Pro:
            When retelling Joseph Smith's story of the golden plates [474] in June 1827, Joseph Smith, Sr. [475] described Moroni [476] as a "the spirit of the prophet who wrote this book." (Howe, 1834, pp. 242-43 [477])

        1.2.9.7.6. Pro:
        According to early descriptions by Joseph Smith, the golden plates [478] were protected by a magical enchantment.

          1.2.9.7.6.1. Pro:
          According to Joseph Smith, on his first attempt to obtain the golden plates from their buried location, the guardian struck him multiple times with lightning of increasing force to prevent him from getting the plates. (Oliver Cowdery 1835, pp. 197-98 [479]; LaFayette Lapham 1870 [480], p. 306; Lucy Mack Smith 1853 [481], p. 86; Willard Chase 1833 [482], p. 242; Lorenzo Saunders 1884 [483]; Lewis & Lewis 1879 [484], p. 1; Katherine Salisbury Smith 1895 [485], p. 14; David Whitmer 1875 [486]; Joseph Smith 1832 [487], p. 4)

            1.2.9.7.6.1.1. Pro:
            According to Willard Chase, Joseph Smith's father said that when Smith first tried to obtain the golden plates [488], the guardian spirit [489] "struck him on the side of his head.--Not being discouraged at trifles, he again stooped down and strove to take the book, when the spirit struck him again, and knocked him three or four rods, and hurt him prodigiously." (Howe 1834, p. 242 [490])

          1.2.9.7.6.2. Pro:
          -> See 1.2.1.1.2.7.7.3.2.

        1.2.9.7.7. Pro:
        Joseph Smith's story of the golden plates [491] includes elements of astrology [492].

          1.2.9.7.7.1. Pro:
          Joseph Smith and his family practiced astrology.

          1.2.9.7.7.2. Pro:
          September 21-22, 1823, the days Joseph Smith said he found the golden plates [493], had astrological significance to the Smith family.

            1.2.9.7.7.2.1. Pro:
            September 21, 1823 was the day after a full moon.

            1.2.9.7.7.2.2. Pro:
            September 22, 1823 was the day before the autumnal equinox.

            1.2.9.7.7.2.3. Pro:
            September 21, 1823, was the only night of the week ruled by Jupiter, Joseph Smith's ruling planet and the most prominent astrological symbol on the Smith family's Holiness to the Lord lamen, which was designed for allowing a pure youth to summon a good spirit.

          1.2.9.7.7.3. Pro:
          September 22, 1824, the date of Joseph Smith's second attempt to retrieve the golden plates [494], had astrological significance because it was both a new moon and the autumnal equinox.

          1.2.9.7.7.4. Pro:
          September 22, 1827, the day that Joseph Smith claimed to recover the golden plates [495], had astrological significance to the Smith family.

      1.2.9.8. Pro:
      Joseph Smith admitted that aspects of the translation were not authentic.

        1.2.9.8.1. Pro:
        -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.2.

        1.2.9.8.2. Pro:
        -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.2.4.

      1.2.9.9. Con:
      By his wife Emma's testimony: Joseph Smith was not sufficiently educated to dictate a well worded letter let alone a whole book. fairmormon.org [496]

      1.2.9.10. Con:
      By David Whitmer's testimony, Joseph Smith was not sufficently educated to pronounce the words he was translating. criticaltext.byustudies.byu.edu [497]

      1.2.9.11. Pro:
      -> See 1.2.1.1.2.7.4.

      1.2.9.12. Pro:
      Joseph Smith initially wondered whether his purported experiences with Moroni [498], the guardian of the golden plates [499], were a dream.

        1.2.9.12.1. Con:
        The fact that Smith questioned his own revelation at first demonstrates critical thinking.

        1.2.9.12.2. Pro:
        Martin Harris [500] told Jonathan A. Hadley, editor of the Palmyra Freeman [501], that after Joseph Smith's first encounter with Moroni [502], "he supposed this 'vision,' as he used to call it, to be no more nor less, than 'the baseless fabric of a dream,' and paid no attention to it." (Hadley, Lowell Courier, July 28, 1842 [503])

        1.2.9.12.3. Pro:
        In Joseph Smith's 1832 history, he wrote that when he went to the hill Cumorah [504] to get the golden plates, he "made three attempts to get them and then being exceedingly frightened I supposed it had been a dreem [sic] of Vision." (Smith, 1832 History, p. 4 [505])

      1.2.9.13. Con:
      Eyewitnesses: There are credible witnesses who personally saw the plates [506] from which Joseph Smith translated the Book of Mormon.

        1.2.9.13.1. Pro:
        Though all eleven of the witnesses who attested to the golden plates had a falling-out with Mormon founder Joseph Smith, none of them ever recanted their attestation.

          1.2.9.13.1.1. Pro:
          David Whitmer, one of the three witnesses of the Book of Mormon, was excommunicated in 1838. He died fifty years later in 1888. On his tombstone is etched: "The record of the Jews and the record of the Nephites are one." en.wikiquote.org [507]

          1.2.9.13.1.2. Pro:
          Oliver Cowdery, one of the three witnesses, was excommunicated in 1838 and became a practicing attorney. One one occassion during a very public murder trial, a rival attorney mocked and jeered Cowdery for believing in a "golden bible." Though having bitterly left the Church, Cowdery affirmed that he saw the golden plates: "Before God and man I dare not deny what I have said, and what my testimony contains and as written and printed on the front page of the Book of Mormon." moroni10.com [508]

          1.2.9.13.1.3. Pro:
          Martin Harris, one of the three witnesses of the Book of Mormon, testifed on his deathbed "The Book of Mormon is no fake. I know what I know. I have seen what I have seen and I have heard what I have heard. I have seen the gold plates from which the Book of Mormon is written." mormonthink.com [509]

          1.2.9.13.1.4. Pro:
          All witnesses were excommuniated [510] at a certain point.

        1.2.9.13.2. Pro:
        The Three and Eight Witnesses received no tangible benefit and experienced substantial hardship from claiming they had seen and held the plates.

          1.2.9.13.2.1. Con:
          The three and eight witnesses actually did receive or were promised tangible benefits.

            1.2.9.13.2.1.1. Pro:
            Elders, high priests are to  have their families supported by the property alloted to the bishop. The Bishop himself should also be provided for in this way. - Doctrine of the Covenants 42:71-73

            1.2.9.13.2.1.2. Pro:
            Properties are to be used to support church officers - Doctrine of the Coveneants 42:73

          1.2.9.13.2.2. Pro:
          Many lost material possessions, reputation, and were driven from their homes with their families for witnessing to others what they saw.

            1.2.9.13.2.2.1. Pro:
            Hyrum Smith, one of the eight witnesses, was driven from his home, imprisoned, and was ultimately martyred for his witness. He said, "I felt a determination to die, rather than deny the things which my eyes had seen, which my hands had handled, and which I had borne testimony to, wherever my lot had been cast." [511]

              1.2.9.13.2.2.1.1. Con:
              There is a vast difference between saying you are willing to be martyred for your testimony and actually being martyred. A person merely claiming they are willing to be martyred could be a liar who's planning to recant as soon as it seems they might actually be martyred.

            1.2.9.13.2.2.2. Pro:
            Because of his belief, Samuel Smith, one of the eight witnesses, was driven from his home in Missouri [512] and settled in Illinois.

        1.2.9.13.3. Pro:
        The testimonies of the Three Witnesses and the Eight Witnesses are persuasive because they appear above their signatures, which represent their word of honor.

          1.2.9.13.3.1. Pro:
          Compared to today, in the culture of 1820s America, a person's word was a very grave thing, and people were much less likely to falsify.

          1.2.9.13.3.2. Con:
          The alleged signatures of the Three and Eight Witnesses, found in the printer's manuscript [513] of the Book of Mormon, were written in the handwriting of Oliver Cowdery.

            1.2.9.13.3.2.1. Con:
            The implication that Oliver Cowdery forged signatures against the will of the witnesses is not supported.  The printers manuscript was not a legal document.  Its purpose was to simply document the witness names and spellings for the printer.

            1.2.9.13.3.2.2. Con:
            The eleven witnesses didn't complain that their signatures were forged or that the testimonies in the Book of Mormon didn't represent them.  If fact, throughout their lives, the eleven witnesses acknowledged and reiterated their testimonies as documented in the Book of Mormon.  (see Anderson, R. L. (1981), Investigating the Book of Mormon Witnesses. Salt Lake City: Deseret Book)

          1.2.9.13.3.3. Con:
          In comparison to testimonies contrary to the authenticity of the golden plates such as those found in Eber D. Howe's Mormonism Unvailed [514] (1834), the the testimonies of the Three and Eight Witnesses were not given under oath, subject to penalty for perjury.

        1.2.9.13.4. Pro:
        Mary Musselman Whitmer (1778–1856), the mother of five Witnesses who took care of the household in Fayette, New York, where much of the translation occurred, said that an angel showed her the plates and thus made her more content to continue her daily labors.(See "David Whitmer Interview with Edward Stevenson, 9 February 1888,"in Dan Vogel, Early Mormon Documents John Whitmer said that his grandmother always referred to the supernatural visitor as "Brother Nephi.")

          1.2.9.13.4.1. Con:
          Supernatural demonstration is not widely accepted as evidence of a credible eyewitness account.

        1.2.9.13.5. Pro:
        There are two separate witness testimonials; the Three Witnesses [515] and the Eight Witnesses [516] which differ in stories but not in the conclusion that the Golden Plates are real.

          1.2.9.13.5.1. Pro:
          The Three Witnesses saw the golden plates [517] because of an angel [518] while the Eight Witnesses [519] were shown the golden plates by Smith himself.

          1.2.9.13.5.2. Con:
          All of the eight witnesses belonged to just two families [520], so their testimony doesn't carry as much weight as eight unrelated people's testimony would.

          1.2.9.13.5.3. Con:
          There is evidence [521] that none of these witnesses actually saw the plates apart from supernatural and subjective experiences.

        1.2.9.13.6. Con:
        After Smith's death the new leadership under James Strang [522] produced new plates and all witnesses accepted [523] those too. Thus, their belief in the truth of the first set was not absolute.

          1.2.9.13.6.1. Con:
          Separate witnesses to one thing do not affect the veracity of other witnesses to another thing.

            1.2.9.13.6.1.1. Con:
            If someone bares witness to two things, and one of those is found to be false, the trustworthiness of the other claim loses validity.

        1.2.9.13.7. Pro:
        The Three Witnesses: Martin Harris, Oliver Cowderly, and David Whitmer each saw the plates, the engravings on the plates, and heard the voice of God witnessing it was a true record. Testimony of the Three Witnesses [524]

          1.2.9.13.7.1. Pro:
          Each of the three witnesses who saw the plates left the church in bitterness. None ever denied that they saw the plates nor that the Book of Mormon was of divine origin.

          1.2.9.13.7.2. Con:
          A letter by High Priest Stephen Burnett [525] explains that he and others, including three apostles, left the church because Martin Harris revealed publicly that he and the other witnesses hadn't actually seen the plates. Burnett also stated that the eight witnesses were persuaded to sign that they had seen them, although they had not.

            1.2.9.13.7.2.1. Con:
            Burnett's letter was written after he had become disaffected with the church and thus, heavily biased against the church. This is hearsay and slander which does not constitute credible evidence. [526]

            1.2.9.13.7.2.2. Con:
            This is hearsay [527].

            1.2.9.13.7.2.3. Con:
            This contradicts testimony given by Martin Harris [528], even much later in his life.

        1.2.9.13.8. Pro:
        Christian Whitmer, Jacob Whitmer, Peter Whitmer, Jun., John Whitmer, Hiram Page, Joseph Smith, Sen., Hyrum Smith, and Samuel H. Smith each saw the plates, handled them and saw the engravings that were on it, "all of which has the appearance of ancient work, and of curious workmanship." Testimony of the Eight Witnesses [529].

          1.2.9.13.8.1. Con:
          Many of the Eight Witnesses belonged to Smith's family [530] or where otherwise personally attached to him. Their testimonial is not credible.

            1.2.9.13.8.1.1. Con:
            -> See 1.2.9.13.1.

        1.2.9.13.9. Con:
        The testimony of the witnesses [531] to the metal plates from which James Strang claimed he translated The Book of the Law of the Lord [532] is as credible as that of the eleven witnesses of the Book of Mormon.

          1.2.9.13.9.1. Con:
          The count of the witnesses is less (7) and are not corroborated by any other sources to the extent the Book of Mormon witeness are [533]. Making a claim on a thread this weak is a red herring, and obfuscates the true argument.

          1.2.9.13.9.2. Pro:
          Like the witnesses to the golden plates of the Book of Mormon, the witnesses to the metal plates of the Book of the Law of the Lord claimed [534] that they "examined them with our eyes, and handled them with our hands."

          1.2.9.13.9.3. Con:
          The Strangite witnesses didn't see anything supernatural [535].

          1.2.9.13.9.4. Pro:
          Unlike the witnesses of the Book of Mormon, there weren't significant subsets of the Strangite witnesses that all belonged to the same family.

          1.2.9.13.9.5. Con:
          An ex-Strangite named Isaac Scott claimed that Caleb P. Barnes helped Strang to forge the plates [536].

        1.2.9.13.10. Con:
        Some of the witnesses said their view of the golden plates [537] was in a vision, rather than with their natural eyes.

          1.2.9.13.10.1. Pro:
          The Testimony of Three Witnesses [538] of the Book of Mormon states that the plates were shown to them "by the power of God," which implies that the experience was part of a vision rather than a purely physical experience.

          1.2.9.13.10.2. Pro:
          In 1838, Martin Harris, one of the Three Witnesses of the Book of Mormon, announced to an Ohio audience that the eleven other witnesses, and Joseph Smith himself, had never seen the golden plates except in a vision.

            1.2.9.13.10.2.1. Con:
            On his deathbed Martin Harris maintained that he had seen the gold plates. Accounts that claim people heard him say he only saw the plates in a vision are hearsay. These accounts contradict what Martin Harris stated on his deathbed.

              1.2.9.13.10.2.1.1. Con:
              The idea that Harris saw the plates through his "spiritual eyes" is consistent with the fact that he believed he saw them.

            1.2.9.13.10.2.2. Pro:
            In a letter dated April 15, 1838, Stephen Burnett stated that he had heard Martin Harris state before a congregation that he "never saw the plates with his natural eyes only in vision or imagination, neither Oliver [Cowdery] nor David [Whitmer] & also that the eight witnesses never saw them & hesitated to sign that instrument [539] for that reason, but were persuaded to do it." (Stephen Burnett to Lyman E. Johnson, Joseph Smith Letterbook, 2:64-66 [540]).

            1.2.9.13.10.2.3. Pro:
            According to an 1838 editorial letter by Warren Parrish, Martin Harris stated that "he never saw the plates, from which the book [of Mormon] purports to have been translated, except in vision; and he further says that any man who says he has seen them in any other way is a liar, Joseph [Smith] not excepted." (Warren Parrish, Evangelist 6, Oct. 1, 1838, p. 226 [541])

            1.2.9.13.10.2.4. Pro:
            Three weeks after announcing that none of the witnesses had seen the golden plates except in vision, Martin Harris stated before a different congregation that he still believed in the golden plates, "but he never saw them only as he saw a city through a mountain," and that "he never should have told that the testimony of the eight was false, if it had not been picked out of air but should have let it passed as it was." (Stephen Burnett to Lyman E. Johnson, Joseph Smith Letterbook, 2:64-66 [542])

            1.2.9.13.10.2.5. Pro:
            One of Martin Harris's neighbors in Ohio said that Harris "never claimed to have seen [the plates] with his natural eyes, only spiritual vision." (Reuben P. Harmon statement, c. 1885, in Vogel, Early Mormon Documents 2:385).

              1.2.9.13.10.2.5.1. Con:
              Martin Harris never denied his original statement as found in the Book of Mormon. The 'neighbor' mentioned in this claim is not named. This claim should not be given greater weight than what Martin Harris maintained up to his moment of death.

          1.2.9.13.10.3. Pro:
          John Whitmer, one of the Eight Witnesses of the Book of Mormon, stated that the golden plates had been "shown to me by a supernatural power." (Theodore Turley, "Memoranda," 1845)

          1.2.9.13.10.4. Pro:
          -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.7.3.

          1.2.9.13.10.5. Pro:
          Around 1829-30, Martin Harris told residents of Palmyra, NY that he had only seen the golden plates with his spiritual eyes.

            1.2.9.13.10.5.1. Pro:
            John H. Gilbert, typesetter for the Book of Mormon, asked Martin Harris, "Martin, did you see those [golden] plates with your naked eyes?" Harris "looked down for an instant, raised his eyes up, and said, 'No, I saw them with a spiritual eye.'" (John H. Gilbert, "Memorandum," Sept. 8, 1892, in EMD 2:548)

            1.2.9.13.10.5.2. Pro:
            A respected Palmyra lawyer asked Martin Harris if he had seen the golden plates with his "bodily eyes, just as you see this pencil-case in my hand," and Harris replied, "Why I did not see them as I do that pencil-case, yet I saw them with the eye of faith; I saw them just as distinctly as I see any thing around me,--though at the time they were covered over with a cloth." (John A. Clark, Gleanings by the Way, 256-57 [543])

            1.2.9.13.10.5.3. Pro:
            Martin Harris told a resident of Palmyra, NY that he had seen the golden plates with "spiritual eyes." (Jesse Townsend to Phieas Stiles, Dec. 24, 1833, in Pomeroy Tucker, Origin, Rise, and Progress of Mormonism, 288-91 [544])

          1.2.9.13.10.6. Pro:
          A revelation dictated by Joseph Smith said to the Three Witnesses [545], regarding the golden plates, "it is by your faith that you shall obtain a view of them," and that "it is by my power that [Joseph Smith] has seen them." (Revelation, June 1829-E [546])

        1.2.9.13.11. Con:
        Even if the eyewitnesses saw the golden plates [547], they were not credible witnesses.

          1.2.9.13.11.1. Pro:
          The witnesses believed in supernatural occurrences.

            1.2.9.13.11.1.1. Pro:
            Oliver Cowdery was a rodsman [548]. This shows his affection to believe in supernatural occurences.

            1.2.9.13.11.1.2. Con:
            The Witnesse acted very rationally [549] for God and Bible-believing men.

            1.2.9.13.11.1.3. Con:
            This standard would make most people in the world unreliable witnesses. 84% of people in the world affiliate with a religion [550]. Many of the remaining 16% have a belief in the supernatural.

            1.2.9.13.11.1.4. Pro:
            Like Joseph Smith, Hiram Page [551] claimed to receive revelations from a seer stone [552].

              1.2.9.13.11.1.4.1. Pro:
              -> See 1.2.1.1.2.7.4.5.1.

            1.2.9.13.11.1.5. Pro:
            Martin Harris [553] claimed, falsely, to be a prophet.

              1.2.9.13.11.1.5.1. Pro:
              Martin Harris [554] prophesied, falsely, that "Within four years from September 1832, there will not be one wicked person left in the United States; that the righteous will be gathered to Zion, and that there will be no President over these United States after that time." (Howe, 1834, p. 14 [555])

              1.2.9.13.11.1.5.2. Pro:
              Martin Harris [556] prophesied, falsely, "I do hereby assert and declare that in four years from the date hereof, every sectarian and religious denomination in the United States, shall be broken down, and every Christian shall be gathered unto the Mormonites, and the rest of the human race shall perish. If these things do not take place, I will consent to have my hand separated from my body." (Howe, 1834, p. 14 [557])

              1.2.9.13.11.1.5.3. Pro:
              Martin Harris [558] once prophesied "that Christ would be on the earth within fifteen years, and all who did not believe the book of Mormon would be destroyed." (Howe, 1834, p. 15 [559])

            1.2.9.13.11.1.6. Pro:
            David Whitmer [560] prophesied, falsely, that the translation of the sealed portion of the golden plates [561] "should commence when nearly all the witnesses to the first plates [562] had passed away." (Poulsen, Deseret News, Aug. 21, 1878, p. 449 [563])

            1.2.9.13.11.1.7. Pro:
            -> See 1.2.1.1.2.9.

          1.2.9.13.11.2. Pro:
          The witnesses were not qualified to determine the plates' historical authenticity.

          1.2.9.13.11.3. Con:
          One argument against eyewitnesses is shared delusion. The chances of shared delusion go down exponentially with each new witness in on the deception. In this case we have 11 independent witnesses who actually saw the plates, and many others who saw them in a bag or other container. This is a preponderance of evidence that they existed regardless of the individual credibility of the witnesses.

          1.2.9.13.11.4. Con:
          There was no conspiracy since conspiracy generally requires some material benefit to the conspirators as reward for their deception.

          1.2.9.13.11.5. Con:
          Credible or not, every single one of them went to their graves without denying what they saw.

        1.2.9.13.12. Con:
        Descriptions of the golden plates [564] by those who said they saw them are inconsistent.

          1.2.9.13.12.1. Pro:
          Depending on which witness is describing the golden plates [565] and when, their purported thickness ranges from 1/8 inch thick to six inches thick.

            1.2.9.13.12.1.1. Pro:
            Joseph Smith, Sr. told a Palmyra resident that the golden plates were "1/8 inch thick." (Chicago Times, Oct. 14, 1888 [566])

            1.2.9.13.12.1.2. Pro:
            Joseph Smith, Sr. told a Palmyra, NY neighbor that the golden plates were "in the form of a book, half an inch thick." (Fayette Lapham interview, Historical Magazine 7, 1870) [567])

            1.2.9.13.12.1.3. Pro:
            Martin Harris said in an interview of the golden plates [568] that "when piled one above the other, they were altogether about four inches thick." (Tiffany's Monthly 5, 1859, p. 164 [569])

            1.2.9.13.12.1.4. Pro:
            Martin Harris [570] told Jonathan A. Hadley, editor of the Palmyra Freeman [571], that the golden plates [572] were "some dozen in number...about the thickness of tin." (Hadley, Lowell Courier, July 28, 1842 [573])

            1.2.9.13.12.1.5. Pro:
            Joseph Smith described the golden plates [574] as being "something near six inches in thickness." (Times & Seasons, 1 Mar. 1842, p. 707 [575])

            1.2.9.13.12.1.6. Pro:
            According to either Lyman Johnson [576] or Orson Pratt [577], who said an angel showed him the golden plates [578], they were "about as thick as window glass." (Catholic Telegraph, Apr. 14, 1832, p. 204 [579])

              1.2.9.13.12.1.6.1. Con:
              This is taken out of context. The complete quotation states also they were as thick as 'common tin:' "The box in thickness was about 6 inches, and about 7 by 5 otherwise; the plates themselves were about as thick as window glass, or common tin, pure gold, and well secured by silver rings or loops in the box as an effectual defence against all weather."(Catholic Telegraph, Apr. 14 1832, p. 204) [580]

          1.2.9.13.12.2. Pro:
          Depending on which witness is describing the golden plates [581] and when, the purported dimension of each leaf ranges from 16 by 10 inches to less than 7 by 5 inches.

            1.2.9.13.12.2.1. Pro:
            According to Joseph Smith, each leaf of the golden plates [582] was "six inches wide and eight inches long." (Times & Seasons, 1 Mar. 1842, p. 707 [583])

            1.2.9.13.12.2.2. Pro:
            Joseph Smith, Sr. [584] told a Palmyra resident that the golden plates [585] consisted of "seven gold plates 16 inches long by 10 wide." (Chicago Times, Oct. 14, 1888 [586])

            1.2.9.13.12.2.3. Pro:
            Joseph Smith, Sr. [587] told a Palmyra, NY neighbor that the golden plates [588] were "about six inches wide, and nine or ten inches long." (Fayette Lapham interview, Historical Magazine 7, 1870) [589])

            1.2.9.13.12.2.4. Pro:
            Martin Harris [590] said in an interview that the golden plates [591] "were seven inches wide by eight inches in length." (Tiffany's Monthly 5, 1859, p. 164 [592])

            1.2.9.13.12.2.5. Pro:
            Martin Harris [593] told Jonathan A. Hadley, editor of the Palmyra Freeman [594], that the golden plates [595] were "some nine inches long and six wide." (Hadley, Lowell Courier, July 28, 1842 [596])

            1.2.9.13.12.2.6. Pro:
            David Whitmer [597] claimed that the golden plates were "about eight inches square." (Howe, 1834, p. 16 [598])

            1.2.9.13.12.2.7. Pro:
            According to Lucy Mack Smith [599], who said she saw the golden plates [600], "they are about eight inches long, and six wide." (Caswall, 1843, p. 27 [601])

            1.2.9.13.12.2.8. Pro:
            According to either Lyman Johnson [602] or Orson Pratt [603], who said an angel showed him the golden plates [604], they were small enough to fit in a 7 by 5 inch box. (Catholic Telegraph, Apr. 14, 1832, p. 204 [605])

          1.2.9.13.12.3. Pro:
          -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.6.3.

          1.2.9.13.12.4. Pro:
          Witnesses of the golden plates [606] are inconsistent as to the nature of the "sealed portion" of the plates.

            1.2.9.13.12.4.1. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon teaches that a protective magical "seal" was put on the golden plates [607] and a set of magical "interpreters" [608] prior to their burial in the hill Cumorah [609].

              1.2.9.13.12.4.1.1. Pro:
              The Book of Mormon refers to the golden plates [610] as "sealed": "And behold the book shall be sealed." (2 Nephi 27:7 [611]). "For the book shall be sealed by the power of God." (2 Nephi 27:10 [612]). "And [Martin Harris] shall say: I cannot bring the book, for it is sealed." (2 Nephi 27:17 [613]).

              1.2.9.13.12.4.1.2. Pro:
              The Book of Mormon commands Joseph Smith, after he translates the golden plates, to "seal up the book again, and hide it up unto me, that I may preserve the words thou hast not read." (2 Nephi 27:22 [614])

              1.2.9.13.12.4.1.3. Pro:
              Moroni [615], a character in the Book of Mormon, says that the Lord commanded him to "seal up" the golden plates [616] and a set of magical interpreters [617]. (Ether 4:5 [618]; Moroni 10:2 [619] ("And I seal up these records, after I have spoken a few words by exhortation unto you."))

              1.2.9.13.12.4.1.4. Pro:
              The Book of Mormon describes the golden plates as follows: "They [620] shall write the things which shall be done among them, and they shall be written and sealed up in a book, and those who have dwindled in unbelief [621] shall not have them." (2 Nephi 26:17 [622])

              1.2.9.13.12.4.1.5. Pro:
              The Book of Mormon's reference to the golden plates containing a "seal" or being "sealed up" mirrors magical [623] terminology in which money or knowledge is "sealed" for protection from unworthy people or evil powers.

                1.2.9.13.12.4.1.5.1. Pro:
                According to a 1708 magical manuscript, "But none that come before this Door can know these Magical Powers, so as to unlock their Secrets or hid Treasures, but such only as are deeply rooted in true love & profound Humility; from all others they are fast bolted & sealed by the Power that none can open." (Caleb Gilman, p. 31 [624])

                1.2.9.13.12.4.1.5.2. Pro:
                An author described ancient treasure lore as follows: "For, it developed, anyone who buries money always put a 'seal' on it by saying certain words [so that] if anyone except the person in possession of the 'seal' attempts to take it, they'll be foiled by those unearthly beings--dog or cat or snake or other creatures chosen--whom the 'seal' has set to guard it." (Ann Hark, "Erdspiegel Mystery," The American-German Review 7 (June 1941): 11)

            1.2.9.13.12.4.2. Pro:
            According to David Whitmer [625], the golden plates [626] were "sealed" because "the leaves were divided equidistant between the back and the edge by cutting the plates in two parts, and again united with solder, so that the front might be opened, while the back part remained stationary and immovable." (Howe, 1832, p. 16 [627])

            1.2.9.13.12.4.3. Pro:
            According to Lucy Mack Smith [628], who said she saw the golden plates [629], "some of them are sealed together and are not to be opened, and some of them are loose." (Caswall, 1843, p. 27 [630])

            1.2.9.13.12.4.4. Pro:
            According to David Whitmer [631], the sealed portion of the golden plates [632] "appeared as solid to my view as wood," and "[a]bout half of the book was sealed. Those leaves which were not sealed, about the half of the first part of the book, were numerous, and the angel turned them over before our eyes." (Poulsen, Deseret News, Aug. 21, 1878, p. 449 [633])

          1.2.9.13.12.5. Pro:
          Witnesses of the golden plates [634] are inconsistent as to the nature of their binding ring(s).

            1.2.9.13.12.5.1. Pro:
            According to Lucy Mack Smith [635], who said she saw the golden plates [636], "They are all connected by a ring which passes through a hole in each plate." (Caswall, 1843, p. 27 [637])

            1.2.9.13.12.5.2. Pro:
            Martin Harris [638] said that the golden plates [639] "were put together on the back by three silver rings." (Tiffany's Monthly, Aug. 1859, p. 164 [640])

            1.2.9.13.12.5.3. Pro:
            David Whitmer [641] said of the golden plates [642] that "the back was secured with three small rings of the same metal [as the plates]." (Howe, 1834, p. 16 [643])

            1.2.9.13.12.5.4. Pro:
            According to either Lyman Johnson [644] or Orson Pratt [645], who said an angel showed him the golden plates [646], they were "well secured by silver rings or loops." (Catholic Telegraph, Apr. 14, 1832, p. 204 [647])

            1.2.9.13.12.5.5. Pro:
            According to David Whitmer [648], the golden plates [649] were "bound together like the leaves of a book by massive rings passing through the back edges." (Kansas City Journal, June 5, 1881 [650])

            1.2.9.13.12.5.6. Pro:
            According to David Whitmer [651], the golden plates [652] "were fastened with rings thus [followed by a sketch of a D-shaped ring]." (Edward Stevenson diary, Dec. 22-23, 1877, in LDS Church Archives)

            1.2.9.13.12.5.7. Pro:
            In a sermon by William McLellin [653], in the presence of Hyrum Smith [654] and likely based on information Hyrum provided, "[t]he plates were minutely described as being connected with rings in the shape of the letter D, which facilitated the opening and shutting of the book." (Illinois Patriot, Sept. 16, 1831 [655])

          1.2.9.13.12.6. Pro:
          According to Manchester neighbor William Stafford, Joseph Smith Sr. [656] "would say that he had seen the plates, and that he knew them to be gold; at other times he would say that they looked like gold; and other times he would say he had not seen the plates at all." (Howe, 1834, p. 240 [657])

            According to Willard Chase, Joseph Smith contrived to get money from Martin Harris [671], a rich man, to move to Pennsylvania by saying to him, "'I have a commandment from God to ask the first man I meet in the street to give me fifty dollars, to assist me in doing the work of the Lord by translating the Golden Bible.' Martin being a credulous man, hands Joseph the money." (Howe 1834, p. 246 [672])

            1.2.9.16.7.1.2. Pro:
            According to neighbor Peter Ingersoll, Joseph Smith was looking for money to move to Pennsylvania so he "went to Palmyra; and, said he, I there met that dam[n] fool, Martin Harris [673], and told him that I had a command to ask the first honest man I met with, for fifty dollars in money, and he would let me have it. I saw at once, said Jo, that it took his notion, for he promptly gave me the fifty." (Howe, 1834, p. 236 [674])

          1.2.9.16.7.2. Pro:
