In response Martin Harris [676] encouraged him to continue, stating, "I have not come down here for nothing, and we will go on with it." (Howe, 1834, p. 183 [677] (statement by Ezra Booth [678])).

  1.3. Pro:
  The Text: The text of The Book of Mormon provides enough evidences of ancient origin to prove it is an authentic ancient book of scripture.

    1.3.1. Pro:
    Linguistics: The writing in the Book of Mormon is consistent with ancient Hebrew and Egyptian writing and language.

      1.3.1.1. Pro:
      Literal translations of Hebraic idioms are prevalent in the Book of Mormon.scholarsarchive.byu.edu [679]

      1.3.1.2. Pro:
      The use of Alma as a man's name in the Book, when it was known at the time as only a woman's name [680], is good Hebrew and suggests the authenticity of the book's origin.

        1.3.1.2.1. Pro:
        The Book of Mormon has been criticized because of its use of the feminine Latin name Alma for a male prophet. However, an ancient Hebrew document dating back to the second Jewish revolt clearly uses the name Alma in referencing a man's name (Yigael Yadin, Bar Kochba: The Rediscovery of the Legendary Hero of the Last Jewish Revolt Against Imperial Rome).

        1.3.1.2.2. Con:
        Alma was also [681] used as a male name in the 1700s and 1800s in America, which is where Smith could have gotten the idea of Alma as a male name.

      1.3.1.3. Pro:
      Chiasmus as used in the Book of Mormon are similar to those written in the Bible, an ancient Hebrew writing.

        1.3.1.3.1. Pro:
        Chiasmus involve a series of words or phrases that are repeated, exactly or contextually, in reverse order. Examples in the Bible include Psalms 3:7-8, Genesis 7:21-23, and Psalm 58. Examples in the Book of Mormon include Mosiah 3:18-19 and Mosiah 5:10-12.

          1.3.1.3.1.1. Pro:
          For example,  “My thoughts are not your thoughts, neither are your ways my ways” (Isaiah 55:8) is a simple chiasmus.

          1.3.1.3.1.2. Con:
          Joseph had the Bible and could have copied this pattern of writing in order to make his writing more "scripturistic".

            1.3.1.3.1.2.1. Con:
            Chiasmus was not formally discovered until after the translation of the Book of Mormon. rsc.byu.edu [682]

              1.3.1.3.1.2.1.1. Pro:
              While Publications in Europe talked about Chiastic structure in the 1800's, it was only known in certain intellectual and religious circles. The first articles published in the US did not appear until the 1920's, 90 years after The Book of Mormon's publications.bible-discernments.com [683]

                1.3.1.3.1.2.1.1.1. Pro:
                "Not until 1942, when Nils Lund published his groundbreaking book 'Chiasmus in the New Testament,' did researchers comprehend the full scope of parallelisms [684], particularly the way in which they formed the fundamental units of complex biblical chiasmus - a large-scale form."

              1.3.1.3.1.2.1.2. Con:
              Chiasmi were used by Shakespeare [685]. This illustrates that one can use a chiasmus even before it is 'discovered'.

                1.3.1.3.1.2.1.2.1. Con:
                Shakespeare, and many other authors, use Chiasmus in small ways, such as "Suit the action to the word, the word to the action" [686] from Hamlet. The Book of Mormon uses Chaismus extensively as a small and large-scale organizational technique that is not found in Shakespeare. See, for example, Alma 36 [687], which is an extensive Chiasmus.

                  1.3.1.3.1.2.1.2.1.1. Con:
                  Still, the use of chiasmus in Shakespeare shows that it was a known technique, and it's plausible that Joseph Smith could have gotten the idea to take these small uses of chiasmus and do it on a large scale.

                  1.3.1.3.1.2.1.2.1.2. Con:
                  The Late War [688] is an 1816 book that contains large-scale Chiasmus.

                    1.3.1.3.1.2.1.2.1.2.1. Con:
                    This is as false statement. The Late War uses chiasmus to a small degree but not not as an overarching organizational principle as does the Book of Mormon.

                  1.3.1.3.1.2.1.2.1.3. Con:
                  Shakespeare also used complex, large-scale [689] chiasmus.

              1.3.1.3.1.2.1.3. Pro:
              Joseph Smith would have had reason to draw attention to the hundreds of chiasmus in the Book of Mormon if he actually was the author trying to convince people of the validity of the Book of Mormon but there is no record of Joseph Smith teaching people about chiasmus or other Hebrew aspects of the literature.

                1.3.1.3.1.2.1.3.1. Con:
                Joseph Smith did not draw attention to chiasms and probably died never having heard the word. This is further evidence of the Book of Mormon's authenticity.

              1.3.1.3.1.2.1.4. Con:
              A literary device doesn't have to be "formally discovered" (given a name) for someone to notice it and try to replicate it in their own work.

              1.3.1.3.1.2.1.5. Pro:
              Before a formal discovery of a non-obvious literary technique such as multi-level Chiastic structuring, it would take years of dedicated study of and access to a variety of relevant works to intuitively come to understand and recreate this in as precisely as it is done in the Book of Mormon. Smith had neither access to libraries nor years of dedicated study.

                1.3.1.3.1.2.1.5.1. Con:
                Once someone is aware of the chiastic pattern (ABBA), moving to "multi-level chiastic structuring" simply requires adding more nested phrase pairs (e.g. ABCDDCBA). This amount of creativity is possible without divine inspiration or even much additional study.

        1.3.1.3.2. Pro:
        -> See 1.3.1.3.1.2.1.

        1.3.1.3.3. Pro:
        Scholars have found, with the aid of computers, very complex forms of Chiasmus in the Book of Mormon extending over many verses or even chapters. publications.mi.byu.edu [690]

        1.3.1.3.4. Pro:
        Chiasm have been found in ancient Mayan literature, the likely ancestors of the civilizations described in the Book of Mormon. publications.mi.byu.edu [691]

        1.3.1.3.5. Pro:
        Chiasmus are an ancient literary technique commonly found in scripture. As an ancient Hebrew writing tool, it was used to assist the reader in remembering the text by the use of words leading to a central point and then the reversal of the word order. rsc.byu.edu [692]

        1.3.1.3.6. Con:
        Revelation, a non-Hebrew text, also [693] has chaismus.

        1.3.1.3.7. Con:
        In the entry in Joseph Smith's Diary [694] for April 1, 1834 [695], which does not claim to be divinely inspired, there is also chiasmus.

        1.3.1.3.8. Con:
        The Solomon Spaulding manuscript [696], which was from around Joseph Smith's time but wasn't divinely inspired, also contains chiasmus.

      1.3.1.4. Pro:
      Apart from Chaismus, there are many forms of Hebraic writing in the text of the Book of Mormon which are natural to Hebrew yet awkward-sounding in English.Hugh W. Pinnock, "Finding Biblical Hebrew and Other Ancient Literary Forms in the Book of Mormon", FARMS, 1999 [697]

        1.3.1.4.1. Pro:
        Construct State: "plates of brass, "works of righteousness," "words of plainness," "night of darkness," "rod of iron." (rather than "brass plates," righteous works," "plain words or speech," "dark night," "iron rod.")

        1.3.1.4.2. Pro:
        Adverbials: "with patience," "with joy," "with gladness." (rather than "patiently," "joyfully," "gladly.")

        1.3.1.4.3. Pro:
        Cognates: "work all manner of work," "judge righteous judgment," "taxed with a tax," "cursed with a cursing."

        1.3.1.4.4. Pro:
        Conjunctions: "And he left his house, and the land of his inheritance, and his gold, and his silver, and his precious things, and took nothing with him, save it were his family and provisions and tents and he departed..."

        1.3.1.4.5. Pro:
        Naming conventions: "call the name of the city Moroni," "called their names Mosiah, and Helorum and Helaman."

        1.3.1.4.6. Pro:
        Possessive pronouns: "the words of me," "eyes of me," "power of him."

        1.3.1.4.7. Con:
        Research published in the BYU Studies Quarterly is of questionable accuracy given that the publishing university is owned [698] by the LDS church.

          1.3.1.4.7.1. Con:
          Christians publish Christian history, because they have an interest in it. That interest alone does not invalidate the veracity of the work. The BYU studies quarterly is an academic journal that publishes scholarly religious literature.

          1.3.1.4.7.2. Con:
          Based on this logic, any source from a non-Church of Jesus Christ publisher opposing the Book of Mormon's authenticity should be of questionable accuracy.

          1.3.1.4.7.3. Con:
          This is an ad hominem logical fallacy [699]: it attacks the source of the evidence rather than the evidence itself.

            1.3.1.4.7.3.1. Con:
            The funding source can influence the content of a study, such as when the sugar industry [700] paid scientists to forge results in the 1960s.

          1.3.1.4.7.4. Pro:
          The BYU has a vested interest to show the BoM to be true.

      1.3.1.5. Pro:
      Suffixes, prefixes and writing style, sentence structure and phraseology match ancient Egyptian and Hebrew languages.Franklin S. Harris, Jr. The Book of Mormon: Messages and Evidences, 1961, p. 121 [701]

        1.3.1.5.1. Pro:
        The Book of Mormon's opening statement: "I, Nephi, having been born of goodly parents..." is a model of the writing style of the ancient Egyptians. Hugh Nibley, Teachings of the Book of Mormon, Semester 1, p.15) [702]

        1.3.1.5.2. Pro:
        Academic researchers only discovered these patterns in ancient writing after Joseph Smith was killed. Smith, therefore, could not have imitated these patterns successfully.

        1.3.1.5.3. Pro:
        The phrase "And it came to pass" is used 1404 times in the Book of Mormon. The word Wayehi means "and it came to pass." Quote: "Wayehi is found about 1,204 times in the Hebrew Bible, but it was translated only 727 times as “and it came to pass” in the King James Version. Joseph Smith did not introduce such variety into the translation of the Book of Mormon. He retained the precision of “and it came to pass,” which better performs the transitional function of the Hebrew word." - www.lds.org [703]

          1.3.1.5.3.1. Con:
          Smith could have gotten the idea for "and it came to pass" from the KJV, and liked that phrase so much he decided to use it even more frequently in his own book.

      1.3.1.6. Pro:
      After the Book of Mormon was translated from reformed Egyptian [704], otherJewish and Semitic texts written in Egyptian characters [705] have been discovered giving credence that the gold plates were a historical artifact.

        1.3.1.6.1. Con:
        There is no evidence for the existence of reformed Egyptian, and there is no demonstrable link between any written languages in the new world and Egypt. The Changing World of Mormonism (Chicago: Moody Press, 1979) [706] 1956 Statements of Egyptologists on the Caractors Document (see pages 3 and 4) [707]

          1.3.1.6.1.1. Pro:
          Hugh Nibley's claim that Meroitic Egyptian was the source of Reformed Egyptian is not historically possible as examples of Meroitic Egyptian only reach from 300 BCE to 400 AD.

      1.3.1.7. Pro:
      Dr. Charles Anthon Certified the Translation was Authentic: Joseph Smith copied a portion of the writings on the metal plates and they were brought to Dr. Charles Anthon of Columbia College. Dr. Anthon certified "the translation was correct [708], more so than any he had before seen translated from the Egyptian." The other characters he said were "Egyptian, Chaldaic, Assyriac, and Arabic." When informed that the plates were received by an angel, Anthon tore up the certificate.

        1.3.1.7.1. Con:
        This account is related by Joseph Smith himself—a man who was not present at the meeting, and who would greatly benefit from this version of events being accepted as true.

        1.3.1.7.2. Con:
        Dr. Charles Anthon disputed that he ever authenticated the transcript. Instead he stated that he identified the writings as a hoax and part of a scheme to cheat Martin Harris of his money. [709]

      1.3.1.8. Con:
      The Book of Mormon uses literary techniques that did not exist in ancient Hebrew or ancient Egyptian, but instead existed in other languages that the individuals represented could not have known.

        1.3.1.8.1. Pro:
        William Hamblin argues [710] that 'literary topos' are used to represent many metals, which may or may not include the ones listed in Jarom 1.8 (“wood, …iron and copper, and brass and steel”) or 2 Ne 5.15 ("and of iron, and of copper, and of brass, and of steel, and of gold, and of silver, and of precious ores”) The technique of topoi is derived from Greek rhetoric and would not have been common knowledge to inhabitants of Jerusalem in 600 BC.

        1.3.1.8.2. Pro:
        Bidding farewell in a literary work due to imminent death in both Late Egyptian and ancient Hebrew would have been remarkably unusual. The use of 'adieu' at the end of Jacob 7:27 [711] becomes all the stranger not for the use of French, but for the reference of dying and saying goodbye to the reader.

      1.3.1.9. Con:
      Even when considering the renaissance of Middle Egyptian seen in the centuries after 664 BCE, the initial chapters of the Book of Mormon are not written as being directly instructional--the common style of educated Egyptians of the time. The chapters follow a narrative pattern that reflects a source from 1800s America far more closely.

      1.3.1.10. Con:
      The Book of Mormon uses names and terms that post-date the era in which the book is set.

        1.3.1.10.1. Con:
        The Book of Mormon is an ancient record translated into nineteenth century English. It is absurd to limit the translation to English words that existed between 600 BC and 421 AD.

          1.3.1.10.1.1. Con:
          The Dura-Europos [712] synagogue is commonly referred to as being the oldest "surviving" one dating to approximately 244 - 300 BC although it's possible future discoveries could change current understanding. The Hebrew word for synagogue [713] is translated as house of assembly, prayer, or worship which is consistent with Book of Mormon usage [714]. Psalms 74:8 [715] links the destruction of synagogues with the destruction of the temple by Nebuchadnezzar, which occurred in 589 BC, shortly after Lehi left. Commentary [716]

        1.3.1.10.2. Pro:
        The Book of Mormon uses idioms, based on Jesus sayings or parables in the King James Version of the New Testament, that would not have been known to the alleged pre-Christian author.

          1.3.1.10.2.1. Pro:
          The Book of Mormon uses the term "good shepherd" to refer to Jesus, allegedly written at a time before Jesus' "good shepherd" parable came into existence.

            1.3.1.10.2.1.1. Pro:
            In a passage allegedly authored by a prophet in 83 BCE, the Book of Mormon uses the Christian era (see John 10) term "good shepherd" in reference to Jesus, as well as Jesus' metaphors of attacking wolves and of bringing followers into his fold. (Alma 5 [717])

            1.3.1.10.2.1.2. Pro:
            In a passage allegedly authored by a prophet in the 20s BCE, the Book of Mormon uses the Christan era (see John 10) term "good shepherd" in a context that presumes, without explanation, the audience knows it refers to their god. (Helaman 7:18 [718])

          1.3.1.10.2.2. Pro:
          The Book of Mormon uses the term "Lamb of God," allegedly dated to the BCE era, under circumstances indicating that the author was aware of the language in John 1:29.

            1.3.1.10.2.2.1. Pro:
            In three instances purportedly dating to the BCE era, the Book of Mormon uses a phrase substantially identical to "Lamb of God, which taketh away the sin of the world" found in John 1:29 (KJV) [719].

              1.3.1.10.2.2.1.1. Pro:
              1 Nephi 10:10 [720] states, in a passage purportedly written in the early 500s BCE, that John the Baptist "baptized the Lamb of God, who should take away the sins of the world."

              1.3.1.10.2.2.1.2. Pro:
              2 Nephi 31:4-6 [721], in a passage purportedly written in the mid-500s BCE, refers to the prophet (John the Baptist) "that should baptize the Lamb of God, which should take away the sins of the world."

              1.3.1.10.2.2.1.3. Pro:
              In Alma 7:14 [722], a passage purportedly dated to 83 BCE, a prophet in the context of discussing baptism encourages his audience to "have faith in the Lamb of God, who taketh away the sins of the world."

          1.3.1.10.2.3. Pro:
          The Book of Mormon uses the idiom "light of the world" in passages dated to the BCE era, under circumstances indicating that the author had knowledge of John 8:12 [723]

          1.3.1.10.2.4. Pro:
          The Book of Mormon uses the idiom "true vine" in passages dated to the BCE era, under circumstances indicating that the author had knowledge of John 15:1-8 [724].

        1.3.1.10.3. Con:
        All of the claims to anachronisms with regard to a translation based on words used are a bit dubious because, if you're translating into a language that didn't exist at the time the original text being translated was written then you will, by necessity, be mapping words to the ancient writings that could not have existed. The whole concept of what it is to translate is being ignored in these claims about words, idioms and sayings. Here's a moredetailed look at the error in logic. [725]

        1.3.1.10.4. Con:
        -> See 1.3.1.10.1.1.

        1.3.1.10.5. Pro:
        The Book of Mormon uses the name "Isabel" in Alma 39:3 [726] even though Isabel was coined in the middle ages [727].

        1.3.1.10.6. Pro:
        The Book of Mormon, in a passage ostensibly written in the 500s BCE, uses the word "Christ" as a personal name for Jesus rather than merely a title, which dates the passage after the Christian era. (2 Nephi 10:3 [728])

          1.3.1.10.6.1. Con:
          God can and does reveals truth to mankind according to the faith of the asker.

          1.3.1.10.6.2. Con:
          God is omniscient and can therefore reveal what things to come such as the name of Jesus Christ.

          1.3.1.10.6.3. Con:
          The Book of Mormon is evidence that "the Christian Era" began at least as early as the 500s BCE.

        1.3.1.10.7. Pro:
        The Book of Mormon uses the term "church" anachronistically.

          1.3.1.10.7.1. Con:
          The Book of Mormon is an ancient record that is translated into ninteenth century English. Of course there will be words in a translation that are not of ancient origin.

          1.3.1.10.7.2. Con:
          Presumably there was a term in reformed Egyptian signifying a group of people formally gathered together to worship. The modern English equivalent would be "church."

          1.3.1.10.7.3. Con:
          The Book of Mormon is a record of people who gathered to worship Christ. Therefore it is not anachronsitic.

          1.3.1.10.7.4. Pro:
          The Book of Mormon refers to "the church" in Jerusalem, circa 600 BCE, where no such church existed. (1 Nephi 4:26 [729])

        1.3.1.10.8. Pro:
        The Book of Mormon uses the term "synagogue" anachronistically.

          1.3.1.10.8.1. Con:
          -> See 1.3.1.10.1.1.

          1.3.1.10.8.2. Pro:
          There is no evidence of a tradition of synagogue worship circa 600 BCE, when the Book of Mormon says that Lehi left Jerusalem.

      1.3.1.11. Con:
      The characters [730] that Joseph Smith ostensibly copied from the golden plates [731] are not from any authentic language.

        1.3.1.11.1. Con:
        The absence of a source materials does not preclude the Book of Mormon from consideration as an authentic ancient scripture.
        
        Source material for the New Testament, albeit most scripture from antiquity, are also not available for examination to determine the authenticity of the Apostles.
        
        25,000 Copies and no Complete Original Documents [732]

        1.3.1.11.2. Con:
        The written language was called Reformed Egyptian [733] by Moroni, the last author in the Book of Mormon. What is Reformed Egyptian? [734] In short Egyptian Hieroglyphs, while they were effective for chiseling into stone, Egyptian scripts were used for quick writing, see Hieratic [735] and Demotic [736]. Demotic [737] was used during Lehi's time [738]; he lived during the first year of the reign of Zedekiah [739]. Moroni's Reformed Egyptian likely changed over the 1000 year timespan of the Book of Mormon and was therefore unique.

      1.3.1.12. Con:
      Actually it's language is more in line with a 19th century understanding of the the King James version of the Bible. Any elements of Hebrew culture can be attributed to that source.

    1.3.2. Pro:
    Authorship: The Book of Mormon was authored by many different authors, consistent with its claim that it is an ancient American record.

      1.3.2.1. Pro:
      Consistent with the Book of Mormon being an authentic ancient text, authorship analyses show that the Book of Mormon had multiple authors. Stylometric Analysis of the Book of Mormon. [740]

        1.3.2.1.1. Con:
        It is not by any means impossible for a single author to write in a number of literary 'styles', particularly if he/she wanted to convey the impression that the 'books' came from different authors in differing time periods.

          1.3.2.1.1.1. Pro:
          When J.K. Rowling wrote lines for the fictional character Hagrid [741] in the Harry Potter books, she made him use noticably more contractions than the other characters.

        1.3.2.1.2. Pro:
        Larsen and Rencher 1982 [742] used "wordprint analysis" to show that the Book of Mormon had many authors, none of which are commonly proposed Book of Mormon authors that are contemporaries of Smith.

          1.3.2.1.2.1. Con:
          The way in which an author uses words is not necessarily constant across their work, which makes using "wordprint analysis" to determine authorship flawed.

            1.3.2.1.2.1.1. Pro:
            -> See 1.3.2.1.1.

            1.3.2.1.2.1.2. Pro:
            Aristotle [743] showed significant variation in his use of words across his writing.

            1.3.2.1.2.1.3. Pro:
            Herodotus [744] wasn't consistent in the frequency with which he used the definite article across his nine books.

          1.3.2.1.2.2. Con:
          Larsen and Rencher used an edited [745] version of the Book of Mormon, which is a problem because the editors could have introduced variations in style.

            1.3.2.1.2.2.1. Pro:
            These edits are likely to have influenced usage styles of the same types of words [746] the Larsen study examined most closely when determining authorship.

          1.3.2.1.2.3. Con:
          Larsen and Rencher used edited [747] versions of 19th century writing samples, which is a problem because the editors could have introduced variations in style.

      1.3.2.2. Pro:
      Through extensive computer analyses of the Book of Mormon [748], wordprint experts have concluded that The Book of Mormon was written by multiple different authors, none of whom are Joseph Smith or any of his known contemporaries.

        1.3.2.2.1. Pro:
        The syntax, sentence structure and vocabularies used in the various parts of the Book of Mormon are distinct from that of Joseph Smith and any known contemporary member of the Church of Jesus Christ or non-member.byustudies.byu.edu [749]

        1.3.2.2.2. Con:
        -> See 1.3.1.4.7.

      1.3.2.3. Pro:
      Wordprint experts state [750] it would be impossible to fake the word patterns and wordprints for the different authors.

        1.3.2.3.1. Con:
        The author of the BYU study cannot be considered a 'wordprint expert' given that their publication list [751] doesn't include a single book on the topic.

        1.3.2.3.2. Pro:
        "It has been demonstrated [752] that stylometric methods are able to detect an author’s word-use patterns even when he or she attempts to write in a different “voice” or to imitate another text’s style. The Book of Mormon’s lengthy texts and complex content would make it especially difficult for its true author(s) to fool the stylometric analysis, whether intentionally or inadvertently."

      1.3.2.4. Pro:
      Helpful Background Information. These sub-claims are intended to help make clear why authorship by multiple ancient authors is important in evaluating the Book of Mormon's authenticity.

        1.3.2.4.1. Pro:
        Ancient prophets, historians, and leaders [753] engraved their testimonies and history on golden plates. Later, the Prophet Joseph Smith, by the gift and power of God, translated an abridgment of those original plates.

          1.3.2.4.1.1. Pro:
          Nephi [754], the son of Lehi, was the main author of what are called the small plates of Nephi. Other writers of this record include Jacob, Nephi’s younger brother, and Enos, the son of Jacob, among others.

        1.3.2.4.2. Pro:
        The writers would include various pre-Columbian Americans named Nephi, Jacob, Mormon, Moroni, Alma, Helaman, among others.The Book of Mormon. [755]

        1.3.2.4.3. Pro:
        The Book of Mormon is primarily a religious history of an ancient American civilization [756], the Nephites, who existed between about 600 BC and 421 AD. It is a work of collective memory, and it therefore makes sense that many people contributed to its writing.

      1.3.2.5. Con:
      The Book of Mormon is plagiarized from an unpublished manuscript written by Solomon Spaulding.

        1.3.2.5.1. Con:
        -> See 1.2.9.5.1.

      1.3.2.6. Con:
      The Book of Mormon has so many similarities to a book written by Gilbert J. Hunt in 1816 called "The Late War" [757] it is reasonable to conclude Joseph Smith used it as a template to write the Book of Mormon.

        1.3.2.6.1. Pro:
        Joseph Smith plagiarized from the Late War [758].

          1.3.2.6.1.1. Con:
          Linguistic parallels between the Book of Mormon and The Late War are easily explainable as shared imitation of the language [759] used in the King James Bible, and not direct literary dependence.

            1.3.2.6.1.1.1. Con:
            It is implausible that [760] the Tecumseh/Teancum parallel came from shared reliance on the KJV, since neither Tecumseh nor Teancum are Biblical names.

              1.3.2.6.1.1.1.1. Con:
              More than likely Teancum didn't come from the KJV or The Late War, but came from Quiché [761], a native Mayan language of Guatemala. Teancum literally means “pole - up high - coat” or “coat up high on a pole”, which is a description of what Captain Moroni did in the Book of Mormon when protecting the land (Alma 46:12-13 [762]).

                1.3.2.6.1.1.1.1.1. Pro:
                Since Teancum directly led an army under Captain Moroni (Alma 50:35 [763]), it is plausible that Moroni gave Teancum his name or title for his contributions of military service.

      1.3.2.7. Pro:
      LDS scholars took up [764] the authorship question to demonstrate that the weight of scholarly evidence is on the side of authenticity.

        1.3.2.7.1. Con:
        LDS scholars have a strong conflict of interest in determining the authenticity of Joseph Smith's translation.

          1.3.2.7.1.1. Con:
          If no one else will defend it, who else but LDS scholars will be looking to defend Joseph Smith?

    1.3.3. Pro:
    Finished in 65 Days. The translation of the text in 65 days [765] with no rewrites is impressive. To author such a richness of text in such a short period of time would be nearly impossible.

      1.3.3.1. Con:
      Books can [766] be written in about 65 days without supernatural aid.

    1.3.4. Pro:
    Literary Analysis: The Book of Mormon's structure and complexity is evidence of its authenticity.

      1.3.4.1. Pro:
      The Book of Mormon explains a coronation ceremony consistent with ancient customs. Mosiah 2-4 [767]

        1.3.4.1.1. Pro:
        Mosiah 2-4 demonstrates the ancient Israelite jubilee year celebration. (see  Brown, Matthew B. (2000), All Things Restored: Confirming the Authenticity of LDS Beliefs, American Fork, Utah: Covenant Communications, pp. 224–225, ISBN 1577347129, OCLC 45100286)

        1.3.4.1.2. Pro:
        Similarities exist [768] between the Feast of Tabernacle [769] and material in Mosiah 2-4.

        1.3.4.1.3. Con:
        That can be explained by Joseph knowing the Old Testament or simple history known at the time.

      1.3.4.2. Pro:
      The Book of Mormon lays out a comprehensive and complex Christian theology.

        1.3.4.2.1. Pro:
        This theology is in some ways more consistent with Christianity as practiced immediately after the Resurrection of Christ.

          1.3.4.2.1.1. Pro:
          Similar to the early Christian church [770], the Book of Mormon rejects original sin [771].

        1.3.4.2.2. Pro:
        The Book of Mormon even supplements Christian theology with a number of unique insights.

          1.3.4.2.2.1. Pro:
          Many Christians exclude unbaptized children from the effects of the Atonement. The Book of Mormon tells us [772] that “little children need no repentance, neither baptism.".

          1.3.4.2.2.2. Pro:
          Unlike in the Bible, there is no ambiguity in the Book of Mormon regarding the resurrection of Jesus Christ. It is written [773] that "the death of Christ shall loose the bands of this temporal death … The spirit and the body shall be reunited again in its perfect form; both limb and joint shall be restored to its proper frame.”

          1.3.4.2.2.3. Pro:
          The Book of Mormon clarifies [774] a much-disputed New Testament passage concerning the extent and nature of Christ’s suffering in the Garden of Gethsemane: “Being in an agony he prayed more earnestly: and his sweat as it were great drops of blood falling down to the ground.”.

      1.3.4.3. Pro:
      Royal Skousen's claim that "most world scriptures were created over decades, if not centuries,often under rather obsure circumstances, and they achieved their current form only after lengthy process of editing. Nearly all of the Book of Mormon, as we have it, was revealed...from April through June 1829. See The Book of Mormon: The Earliest Text, Pg. viii.

        1.3.4.3.1. Con:
        The instruments for writing and publishing were well established in the 19th century compared to ancient times.  This would better account for the superior codification than divine intervention.

      1.3.4.4. Pro:
      The Book of Mormon has 180 named characters that are authenic ancient names, many of which were unknown to the western world at the time.

        1.3.4.4.1. Con:
        So does most works of fiction and epic myths. This in itself doesn't prove or disprove anything.

          1.3.4.4.1.1. Pro:
          So does game of thrones.

          1.3.4.4.1.2. Con:
          The above claim is oversimplified and misrepresents the parent. It is the correlation of these names to ancient names, and Josephs ignorance of these ancient names, that is the point.

        1.3.4.4.2. Con:
        The characters' names follow English phonetics which is further evidence that Joseph Smith made it up.

          1.3.4.4.2.1. Con:
          Not knowing how the translation actually occurred, it is reasonable that the translator had an impact on the translation. This actually occurs in all translation as it is an art. In the preface to the Book of Mormon, Joseph admits that there could be errors in translation.

        1.3.4.4.3. Pro:
        Many of the names are ancient Egyptian or Hebrew names, such as Moroni, Ammon, Nephi, Manti, and Lehi.

          1.3.4.4.3.1. Con:
          Smith could have inferred the name Ammon from the Ammonites described in Genesis 19:38 [775], 1 Samuel 14:47 [776], or Isaiah 11:14 [777].

        1.3.4.4.4. Pro:
        Some names previously thought not to be of Hebrew origin, such as Sariah [778] and Alma have since been found in ancient Jewish documents from Egypt. Paul Y. Hoskisson, "What's in a Name? Alma as a Hebrew Name," Journal of Book of Mormon Studies 7/1 (1998), p. 72-73.

          1.3.4.4.4.1. Pro:
          It is very unlikely that Joseph knew of these ancient Hebrew names, or even modern Jewish names, except what he knew in the Bible. At about this time there were only 15,000 Jews in America.en.wikipedia.org [779]

            1.3.4.4.4.1.1. Con:
            -> See 1.3.1.2.2.

        1.3.4.4.5. Pro:
        The letters "q", "x" and "w" are not in the proper names in the Book of Mormon. These letters are not found in ancient Hebrew proper names.John Witdsoe, Seven Claims of the Book of Mormon, p. 52 [780]

          1.3.4.4.5.1. Con:
          Joseph Smith was familiar enough with the Old Testament to learn what Old Testament names tend to look and sound like. At this point, Smith could easily have replicated these patterns in any BoM names he coined.

            1.3.4.4.5.1.1. Pro:
            Lucy Mack Smith wrote that Joseph Smith once refused to attend a worship service, saying "But I will take my Bible and go out into the woods and learn more in two hours than you could if you were to go to meeting two years" (p. 357 [781]).

            1.3.4.4.5.1.2. Pro:
            Lucy Mack Smith wrote that at age 16 Joseph had "never read the Bible through by course in his life" but that he "seemed much less inclined to the perusal of books than any of the rest of our children, but far more given to meditation and deep study" (p. 344 [782]).

            1.3.4.4.5.1.3. Pro:
            Joseph Smith's father told [783] him, "Thou hast sought to know [God's] ways, and from thy childhood thou hast meditated much upon the great things of his law."

            1.3.4.4.5.1.4. Pro:
            Smith wrote [784] in his First Vision account, "I was born ... of goodly Parents who spared no pains to instructing me in [the] christian religion at the age of about ten years".

        1.3.4.4.6. Con:
        Most of the similarities between Book of Mormon names and real ancient Hebrew names are probably coincidental.

          1.3.4.4.6.1. Pro:
          Many Book of Mormon names happen [785] to be similar to the names of places near New York, yet Mormon apologists would deny that this is anything but a coincidence. To be consistent, however, they would also have to concede that similarities between Book of Mormon names and real ancient Hebrew names are probably coincidental.

      1.3.4.5. Pro:
      The Book of Mormon contains "a chronology and internal geography that remain consistent through numerous subplots, flashbacks, journeys, and lengthy sermons." (See The Book of Mormon: The Earliest Text. Royal Skousen, Pg. x, Yale.)

        1.3.4.5.1. Con:
        Most authors of fiction create completely consistent chronologies and internal geographies.

        1.3.4.5.2. Con:
        If the Book of Mormon were an authentic ancient text, assembled over many different generations by many different authors, one would expect it to be inconsistent in places.

      1.3.4.6. Pro:
      Archaeology: The Book of Mormon narrative contains accurate descriptions of historical information that is supported by archaeology and anthropology which Joseph Smith could not have have independently known about at the time he translated The Book of Mormon.

      1.3.4.7. Con:
      It is possible for something to be complex and fictitious.

        1.3.4.7.1. Con:
        This argument misrepresents the parent. Original claim is not saying that in general "complexity = authenticity" but rather that the specific structure and complexity of The Book of Mormon itself is compelling evidence of its authenticity.

          1.3.4.7.1.1. Pro:
          The complexity further establishes the book to have been outside the abilities of Joseph Smith to create from scratch.

            1.3.4.7.1.1.1. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon exemplifies background and expertise beyond Smith's education or training.

              1.3.4.7.1.1.1.1. Con:
              It is very common for writers of works of fiction to display a broad range of knowledge about society and human nature. Joseph Smith was reportedly "extremely glib and fluent as a debater and story-weaver [786]", suggesting he had great ability to construct tales.

                1.3.4.7.1.1.1.1.1. Con:
                There is not a single witness who saw the 23 year-old Joseph Smith with the books or other resources that would be needed to develop a broad knowledge about society and human nature.

                1.3.4.7.1.1.1.1.2. Con:
                The accusations of fraud Smith faced evidence that a number of his contemporaries believed that he fabricated falsehoods.

              1.3.4.7.1.1.1.2. Con:
              A very large part of the Book of Mormon is borrowed from identifiable sources, especially the Bible, including names of some of the characters. It would not have been difficult for a man living in a fiercely religious state in very religious times to have replicated the style of the Bible as religious leaders frequently do.

                1.3.4.7.1.1.1.2.1. Con:
                Even religious scholars would not have been able to replicate the near eastern literary style. For example, the chiastic parallelisms characteristic of the Book of Mormon were not widely understood by scholars until the 20th century.

                1.3.4.7.1.1.1.2.2. Con:
                Duplicating text is a regular part of ancient and modern writing.

                  1.3.4.7.1.1.1.2.2.1. Pro:
                  A large part of the New Testament is borrowed from the Old Testament.

                  1.3.4.7.1.1.1.2.2.2. Pro:
                  Modern judicial opinions borrow from former judicial opinions.

                1.3.4.7.1.1.1.2.3. Con:
                It would be difficult for anyone as a first time author to replicate the style and the thematic elements of the bible, consistently, which the Book of Mormon does.

                1.3.4.7.1.1.1.2.4. Con:
                Only about 7% of the Book of Mormon duplicates identifiable sources fairmormon.org [787]. That 7% does not constitute a "very large part" of the book.

                1.3.4.7.1.1.1.2.5. Con:
                Joseph Smith was quite young when the Book of Mormon was said to have been translated and would not be a good representative of the learned religious scholar.

                  1.3.4.7.1.1.1.2.5.1. Pro:
                  Joseph Smith was a 23 year-old farm boy when he translated the Book of Mormon. lds.org [788]

                    1.3.4.7.1.1.1.2.5.1.1. Con:
                    The author [789] of Eragon was only 18 when the book was published.

                      1.3.4.7.1.1.1.2.5.1.1.1. Con:
                      Eragon is a fantasy novel with no historical or literary backdrop to remain consistent with compared to a text that had to remain consistent with volumes of Biblical understanding

                      1.3.4.7.1.1.1.2.5.1.1.2. Con:
                      The author of Eragon had access to the internet and an infinite number of books compared to Smith, who only possibly had access to a small rural, rental library in Manchester. He said “Weekly trips to the library were part of our [homeschool] routine. My sister and I became voracious readers, leaving the library every week with our arms filled with teetering towers of books [790].” Smith had no such opportunity.

                        1.3.4.7.1.1.1.2.5.1.1.2.1. Con:
                        There is no reason to assume that Joseph Smith was not exposed to books in ways other than the internet. Books are relatively small and easy to store, so even a small, rural library could have had plenty of books for the young Joseph Smith.

                      1.3.4.7.1.1.1.2.5.1.1.3. Con:
                      The author of Eragon had much more time than Smith: he was homeschooled and worked for three years on the novel. Smith was ever occupied in maintaining the family homestead on a rugged frontier town and was often hired out as a day laborer.

                        1.3.4.7.1.1.1.2.5.1.1.3.1. Con:
                        Joseph Smith had enough free time to spend time fabricating ancient sources.

                          1.3.4.7.1.1.1.2.5.1.1.3.1.1. Pro:
                          Joseph Smith had enough free time to participate in a "juvenile debating club."

                            1.3.4.7.1.1.1.2.5.1.1.3.1.1.1. Pro:

                          1.3.4.7.1.1.1.2.5.1.1.3.1.2. Pro:
                          Smith liked [792] to "laugh, play ball, wrestle, slide on the ice, and play games." This implies he had enough free time to do these things.

                  1.3.4.7.1.1.1.2.5.2. Pro:
                  Joseph Smith only had three years of formal schooling. mormonnewsroom.org [793]

              1.3.4.7.1.1.1.5. Pro:
              Joseph Smith's wife said that "Joseph Smith [as a young man] could neither write nor dictate a coherent and well-worded letter.” Emma Smith, in “Last Testimony of Sister Emma,” 290. [794]

              1.3.4.7.1.1.1.6. Pro:
              New England's pioneers, puritans, and libraries could not have produced a new Bible Testament from its own ministers, writers, and founders' expertise, background, and wits because of overarching differences with the spirit of American law, war, and news of the existing Bible.

              1.3.4.7.1.1.1.7. Pro:
              The Book of Mormon contains information about complex military strategies and tactics [795] for which Joseph Smith had no background, and which would have been difficult or impossible to produce on his own with the precision and detail that he did.

                1.3.4.7.1.1.1.7.1. Pro:
                Leadership: Morgan Deane, military historian and former Marine said, “Leaders in The Book of Mormon responded in realistic and organized fashion [796].”

                1.3.4.7.1.1.1.7.2. Pro:
                David E. Spencer, U.S. Army veteran with an academic and professional expertise in defense studies, concluded regarding the maneuvers in Alma 56 [797], that: “These seemingly sudden, illogical actions … speak volumes about the authenticity of the text and the military expertise of the author, as when all of the clues provided in the text are examined in depth, the logic becomes apparent [798].”

                1.3.4.7.1.1.1.7.3. Pro:
                The Book of Mormon appropriately describes use of missile weapons before hand-to-hand combat [799], scouts, surprise attacks, having stronger ground positions, strategic positioning relative to the sun, breakdown of leadership, and use and construction of defensive fortifications.

              1.3.4.7.1.1.1.8. Pro:
              The Book of Mormon contains ancient names that Joseph Smith could not have known when he dictated the book.

                1.3.4.7.1.1.1.8.1. Pro:
                The Book of Mormon refers to the place name Nahom [800] on the Arabian peninsula, which matches “NHM” inscriptions in Yemen, which Joseph Smith could not have known about when the Book of Mormon was produced.

                  1.3.4.7.1.1.1.8.1.1. Con:
                  NHM was most likely pronounced Nihm (its only extant pronunciation), not Nahom.

                  1.3.4.7.1.1.1.8.1.2. Con:
                  It is not surprising that one name in the long list of names in the Book of Mormon, when stripped of vowels, matches one of the many thousands of three-letter names found somewhere in ancient locations that are the setting of the Book of Mormon.

                  1.3.4.7.1.1.1.8.1.3. Con:
                  The bible is a more plausible source than NHM for the name "Nahom." (1 Chron. 4:19, Ne. 7:7, Na. 1:1, Ne. 1:1).

                  1.3.4.7.1.1.1.8.1.4. Con:
                  The Book or Mormon refers to Nahom as a place, whereas the ancient NHM inscriptions in Yemen refer to a tribe.

                    1.3.4.7.1.1.1.8.1.4.1. Con:
                    Places are often and almost always named after the people inhabiting that place. For example Yakima, Wa is named for the Yakima natives of that area.

              1.3.4.7.1.1.1.9. Pro:
              The Book of Mormon contains information about ancient legal systems for which Joseph Smith had no background to produce on his own.

                1.3.4.7.1.1.1.9.1. Pro:
                The heaviest ire found in the Book of Mormon plays out perhaps in Alma 60, where Captain Moroni makes wind censuring King Lamoni for not providing support for Nephite troops.

              1.3.4.7.1.1.1.10. Pro:
              The Book of Mormon is too complex for Joseph Smith to have created solely by his own wits.

                1.3.4.7.1.1.1.10.1. Pro:
                Though a complex narrative spanning thousands of years and involving hundreds of characters, The Book of Mormon never contradicts itself.

                  1.3.4.7.1.1.1.10.1.1. Con:
                  The Lord of the Rings, Game of Thrones as well as Harry Potter contains thousands of pages and are not contradictory. Yet, they are clearly not true.

                    1.3.4.7.1.1.1.10.1.1.1. Con:
                    The Lord of the Rings was written by a highly educated literary genius. There is no evidence that anyone with even remotely comparable education or experienc to J.R.R. Tolkien was involved in the coming forth of the Book of Mormon.

                      1.3.4.7.1.1.1.10.1.1.1.1. Pro:
                      -> See 1.3.4.7.1.1.1.2.5.2.

                    1.3.4.7.1.1.1.10.1.1.2. Con:
                    The Lord of the Rings took 17 years to writ [801]e and was reworked many times. GOT has as many as 7 writers per episode [802] and the first book of Harry Potter took 6 years [803]. None of these books' storylines are nearly as complex and none of them contain amazing life-changing doctrine.

                1.3.4.7.1.1.1.10.2. Con:
                There are lots of examples [804] of books written in less time than the Book of Mormon, with arguably equal complexities.

                1.3.4.7.1.1.1.10.3. Con:
                It is not clear that the Book of Mormon was written by only one individual.

                  1.3.4.7.1.1.1.10.3.1. Pro:
                  -> See 1.3.2.1.

                1.3.4.7.1.1.1.10.4. Con:
                Equating unanswered questions about details of the book's creation with evidence for one particular problematic explanation is an example of the fallacy "argument from ignorance". A similar example would be to say, "if you can't explain the magician's trick, you must concede that it is actual magic".

              1.3.4.7.1.1.1.11. Pro:
              The amount of time taken to translate the Book of Mormon was not enough for an uneducated youth to create the complexities of character, story, geography, etc, even if relying on fragments of stories from multiple sources.

                1.3.4.7.1.1.1.11.1. Pro:
                -> See 1.3.3.

                1.3.4.7.1.1.1.11.2. Con:
                -> See 1.3.4.7.1.1.1.10.2.

        1.3.4.7.2. Pro:
        The structure and complexity of Lord of the Rings is not evidence of ancient authenticity.

          1.3.4.7.2.1. Con:
          Both books are altogether unalike in their form, structure, complexity and purpose. Lord of the Rings does not suggest or imply that it is an authentic record, whereas the Book of Mormon does so, unequivocally.

          1.3.4.7.2.2. Con:
          The above claim is over simplified. If Tolkien wrote a book with complexity and also made the claim of ancient authenticity, and has no other way to show how the authenticity got there, and future scholarly efforts showed it to have merit, then its complexity would be evidence.

      1.3.4.8. Pro:
      The Book of Mormon is authentic because it "fulfills certain conditions lending it credibility as starting off in the Near East such as: It's local color is extremely plausible, describes a state of social organization which agrees exactly with archaeological and documentary evidence, the personal names are satisfactory for that period and region, and finally, there is nothing unreasonable in the story itself"The Troubled Orient [805]

      1.3.4.9. Con:
      There are mistranslations from the 1769 KJV edition [806] of the Bible present in the Book of Mormon. This would be impossible for the translation of a supposedly ancient artefact.

        1.3.4.9.1. Con:
        -> See 1.2.9.6.

      1.3.4.10. Con:
      The Book of Mormon contains modern influences.

        1.3.4.10.1. Pro:
        The way the Book of Mormon portrays indigenous Americans is best explained by an author from Joseph Smith's era.

          1.3.4.10.1.1. Pro:
          The Book of Mormon has credible parallels to View of the Hebrews [807] (1823), by Ethan Smith, which like the Book of Mormon argues that indigenous Americans originated as Israelite immigrants from Palestine.

            1.3.4.10.1.1.1. Pro:
            Oliver Cowdery likely introduced Joseph Smith to View of the Hebrews by Ethan Smith, so as to influence the content of the Book of Mormon.

              1.3.4.10.1.1.1.1. Pro:
              Oliver Cowdery lived in Poultney, Vermont, when Ethan Smith's View of the Hebrews was published, and was therefore likely familiar with the book.

              1.3.4.10.1.1.1.2. Pro:
              Oliver Cowdery's family attended the Congregationalist church where Ethan Smith was pastor, while Smith was writing View of the Hebrews; thus Cowdery is likely to have been familiar with the book during that time frame.

          1.3.4.10.1.2. Pro:
          Joseph Smith initially claimed [808] that indigenous North Americans were descendants of the Lost Tribes of Israel but later modified it to include all Mormons. Many doubt that the Ten Lost Tribes ever existed but this hasn't stopped many groups making the same claim, "ranging from Berbers to Moors to Mongolians [809]", the most likely reason being to confer a special 'chosen' status on themselves.

          1.3.4.10.1.3. Con:
          The Book of Mormon teaches that Lehi (and his family) is from the tribe of Manasseh.  1 Nephi 5:14, Alma 10:3, and was not among the "lost tribes." 1 Nephi 1:24, 2 Nephi 29:13, 3 Nephi 17:4.

          1.3.4.10.1.4. Pro:
          A wide variety of books, periodicals, and newspaper articles [810] describing aspects of Native American life circulated during Joseph Smith’s era. These books were also publicly available at this time. Anyone could have accessed this information.

          1.3.4.10.1.5. Pro:
          The Book of Mormon describes Lamanites [811] consistently with 19th century stereotypes of Native Americans.

            1.3.4.10.1.5.1. Con:
            Conspicuously missing is any mention that the Lamanites had "red skin" as was a common stereotype [812] of Native American's in Joseph Smith's day.

            1.3.4.10.1.5.2. Pro:
            2 Nephi 5:24 [813] describes Lamanites [814] as "an idle people, full of mischief and subtlety, and [seeking] in the wilderness for beasts of prey."

            1.3.4.10.1.5.3. Pro:
            Enos 1:20 [815] describes Lamanites [816] as "wild, and ferocious, and a blood-thirsty people, full of idolatry and filthiness; feeding upon beasts of prey; dwelling in tents, and wandering about in the wilderness with a short skin girdle about their loins and their heads shaven; and their skill was in the bow, and in the cimeter, and the ax. And many of them did eat nothing save it was raw meat."

            1.3.4.10.1.5.4. Pro:
            1 Nephi 12:23 [817] describes Lamanites [818] as "a dark, loathsome, and a filthy people, full of idleness and all manner of abominations."

            1.3.4.10.1.5.5. Pro:
            Alma 3:5 [819] states that "the heads of the Lamanites [820] were shorn; and they were naked, save it were skin which was girded about their loins, and also their armor, which was girded about them, and their bows, and their arrows, and their stones, and their slings, and so forth."

          1.3.4.10.1.6. Pro:
          In the Book of Mormon narrative, the Lamanites [821] are the indigenous peoples of the Americas [822].

            1.3.4.10.1.6.1. Pro:
            -> See 1.3.4.10.1.5.

          1.3.4.10.1.7. Pro:
          The Smith family had an early interest in indigenous American artifacts and purported history.

            1.3.4.10.1.7.1. Con:
            The comments by Peter Ingersoll can't be independently verified (no witnesses) and were likely fabricated considering he was an admitted enemy of the Smith's and had motivation to try to discredit the family after they left the area.

            1.3.4.10.1.7.2. Con:
            William Stafford's statement can't be independently verified (no witnesses) and is nothing more than gossip.

            1.3.4.10.1.7.3. Pro:
            Joseph Smith, Sr. [823] once told a neighbor that "the ancient inhabitants of this country used camels instead of horses," and that proof of this could be found in a nearby cave that contained treasure and what he said was a camel saddle hanging on a fossilized peg. (Howe, 1834, p. 233 [824] (statement of Peter Ingersoll))

            1.3.4.10.1.7.4. Pro:
            Before Joseph Smith said he found the golden plates, his father Joseph Smith, Sr. [825] told a neighbor "that in Canada, there had been a book found, in a hollow tree, that gave an account of the first settlement of this country before it was discovered by Columbus." (Howe, 1834, p. 234 [826] (statement by Peter Ingersoll))

            1.3.4.10.1.7.5. Pro:
            Joseph Smith and his family had an awareness of and interest in the Mound Builders [827] and possible mounds in their area.

              1.3.4.10.1.7.5.1. Con:
              -> See 1.3.4.10.1.7.2.

              1.3.4.10.1.7.5.2. Pro:
              During their time in western New York, the Smith family believed that "nearly all the hills in this part of New York, were thrown up by human hands, and in them were large caves" containing buried treasure. (Howe, 1834, pp. 237-38 [828] (statement of William Stafford))

          1.3.4.10.1.8. Pro:
          The Book of Mormon contains descriptions consistent with an early 19th century understanding of the Mound Builders [829].

            1.3.4.10.1.8.1. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon falsely states that the Jaredites [830], purportedly an indigenous American civilization that died out well before the Common Era, "did work in all manner of ore, and they did make gold, and silver, and iron, and brass, and all manner of metals; and they did dig it out of the earth; wherefore, they did cast up mighty heaps of earth to get ore, of gold, and of silver, and of iron, and of copper. And they did work all manner of fine work." (Ether 10:23 [831])

            1.3.4.10.1.8.2. Pro:
            -> See 1.3.4.10.1.7.5.

            1.3.4.10.1.8.3. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon prophesies that when the Jaredites became extinct, "their bones should become as heaps of hearth upon the face of the land." (Ether 11:6 [832])

            1.3.4.10.1.8.4. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon describes a great battle, after which "the bodies of many thousands are moldering in heaps upon the face of the earth." (Alma 28:11 [833])

            1.3.4.10.1.8.5. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon describes the slaughter of every soul in an entire great city, after which "their dead bodies were heaped up upon the face of the earth, and they were covered with a shallow covering." (Alma 16.11 [834])

            1.3.4.10.1.8.6. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon tells a story in which an army of soldiers that "were so numerous that they could not be numbered" fled into a wilderness where many of them died, "and their bones have been found, and have been heaped upon the earth." (Alma 2:35-38 [835])

        1.3.4.10.2. Pro:
        The Book of Mormon contains anachronisms. [836]

          1.3.4.10.2.1. Con:
          The Book of Mormon liberty narrative is distinct from that generally held by mid-ninteenth century Americans.

          1.3.4.10.2.2. Con:
          The Book of Mormon uses literary techniques consistent with ancient near eastern writings that were largely unknown to mid-ninteenth century Americans.

            1.3.4.10.2.2.1. Con:
            The Old Testament was thoroughly familiar to mid-nineteenth century Americans.

          1.3.4.10.2.3. Con:
          The dead sea scrolls also contain ideas that were once considered pre-Christian which shed new light on the idea of a messianic understanding in a Jewish setting prior to the coming of Christ.

          1.3.4.10.2.4. Pro:
          The Book of Mormon mentions items and technologies that archaeologists have never found in the book's purported place and time frame.

            1.3.4.10.2.4.1. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon refers to animals that did not exist in the Americas in the book's purported time frame.

              1.3.4.10.2.4.1.1. Con:
              What the Book of Mormon called the various animals may not be the same terms we are familiar with today, i.e. had Joseph Smith ever heard of a llama (or any of the other various animals unique to South America (supposing that the BofM took place there)) What would he have called them?

              1.3.4.10.2.4.1.2. Pro:
              The Book of Mormon refers to horses, which did not exist in the Americas during the book's purported time frame.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.1.2.1. Con:
                There is provisional evidence of a horse fossil [837] from the period.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.1.2.2. Con:
                According to Yvette Running Horse Collin [838], there were horses in Native culture before the settlers came.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.1.2.3. Pro:
                There is no evidence of depictions of horses [839] in the Americas before the arrival of the European colonists.

                  1.3.4.10.2.4.1.2.3.1. Con:
                  A horseman petroglyph [840] was found in Alto de Pitis, Peru.

                  1.3.4.10.2.4.1.2.3.2. Con:
                  A horse depiction [841] was found in a Mayan temple.

              1.3.4.10.2.4.1.3. Pro:
              The Book of Mormon refers to elephants, which did not exist in the Americas during the book's purported time frame.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.1.3.1. Con:
                In 1952, Ludwell H. Johnson (non mormon), published in the Scientific Monthly, numerous discoveries of the remains of Elephant species in the Americas up to 3000 years ago. This would put it squarely in the time line of the Jaredites, where Elephants are mentioned. fairmormon.org [842]

                  1.3.4.10.2.4.1.3.1.1. Con:
                  Johnson put this 3000 years as an absolute minimum, yet this is consistent for a much earlier date for the extinction of elephants in America.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.1.3.2. Pro:
                North American elephants went extinct about [843] 10,000 years ago.

                  1.3.4.10.2.4.1.3.2.1. Pro:
                  Haile, Froese, MacPhee et al. 2009 [844] argue that mammoths in North America went extinct around 10,500 years ago.

                  1.3.4.10.2.4.1.3.2.2. Pro:
                  Zazula, MacPhee, et al. 2014 [845] argue that mastodons went extinct in North America about 10,000 years ago.

                  1.3.4.10.2.4.1.3.2.3. Con:
                  Vartanyan, Garutt and Sher 1993 [846] describe 7,000 to 4,000-year-old dwarf mammoth teeth being found on Wrangel Island in North America.

                    1.3.4.10.2.4.1.3.2.3.1. Con:
                    Vartanyan et al. 1993 only supports the late existence of North American mammoths on Wrangel Island, which is not probably not relevant to the Book of Mormon narratives.

                      1.3.4.10.2.4.1.3.2.3.1.1. Pro:
                      Wrangel Island [847] is very far north, even north of Alaska, yet it is unlikely that the Book of Mormon narratives occurred here.

                      1.3.4.10.2.4.1.3.2.3.1.2. Pro:
                      The study says that the survival of the mammoths so late is probably due to [848] "local topography and climatic features," meaning it is unlikely this evidence can be extended to places outside Wrangel Island.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.1.3.3. Pro:
                If there were elephants in the New World in Book of Mormon times, we would have found elephant bones dating from that time by now.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.1.3.4. Pro:
                Ether 9:19 [849] refers to elephants.

              1.3.4.10.2.4.1.4. Pro:
              The Book of Mormon refers to domesticated cattle and cows, which did not exist in the Americas during the book's purported time frame.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.1.4.1. Con:
                Robert T Hatt, (Non Mormon) discovered bones of cows and horses in pre-columbian caves along side human bones. He drew the conclusion that there were domesticated horses and cows in the Americas before Columbus. fairmormon.org [850]

                1.3.4.10.2.4.1.4.2. Con:
                Like his contemporaries, Joseph Smith would have been of the opinion that cows were brought to the Americas by Europeans and there were no pre-columbian cows. So, since they are mentioned in the Book of Mormon, it shows that he was not writing from his own knowledge; rather he was blindly translating a text.

              1.3.4.10.2.4.1.5. Pro:
              The Book of Mormon refers to domesticated goats (distinct from "wild goats"), which were never domesticated in the Americas during the book's purported time frame.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.1.5.1. Con:
                It is reasonable to assume that each migrating group of the Book of Mormon brought their native domesticated animals. When domesticated animals are released (which would happen after massive genocide), they often go feral (wild) and/or extinct (hence why they would no longer exist in the Americas).

                1.3.4.10.2.4.1.5.2. Con:
                The Brocket deer is often described as a Goat. 16th-century Spanish friars used the word "goat" to refer to native Mesoamerican brocket deer. en.wikipedia.org [851] All of the people in the Book of Mormon knew what goats were and could easily see the resemblance in animals of the new world.

                  1.3.4.10.2.4.1.5.2.1. Con:
                  The same source [852] says, "There is no evidence that brocket deer were ever domesticated." However, in the Book of Mormon the goats were domesticated.

              1.3.4.10.2.4.1.6. Pro:
              The Book of Mormon refers to domesticated "swine," which did not exist in the Americas during the book's purported time frame.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.1.6.1. Con:
                The peccary is a pig like animal (often viewed as pigs) that has been in the Americas for 3 million years and continues to be there today. It is highly valued and often kept as pets which would make it a domesticated animal. en.wikipedia.org [853]

                  1.3.4.10.2.4.1.6.1.1. Con:
                  The word "peccary" was coined by 1610 [854], meaning Smith could have used this word instead of "swine" when translating the Book of Mormon, assuming he saw a Reformed Egyptian word meaning "peccary."

                    1.3.4.10.2.4.1.6.1.1.1. Pro:
                    An 1831-1832 Genius of Universal Emancipation article [855] uses the word "peccary" and its synonym "javelina."

                    1.3.4.10.2.4.1.6.1.1.2. Pro:
                    According to Merriam-Webster, peccary [856] was used in 1697.

                  1.3.4.10.2.4.1.6.1.2. Con:
                  Peccaries have several significant differences from pigs.

                    1.3.4.10.2.4.1.6.1.2.1. Pro:
                    Peccaries [857] have thick, brown fur; pigs [858] do not.

                  1.3.4.10.2.4.1.6.1.3. Con:
                  Translating "peccary" as "pig" is not enough for the most correct book on Earth [859].

                1.3.4.10.2.4.1.6.2. Pro:
                Ether 9:18 [860] refers to "swine."

                1.3.4.10.2.4.1.6.3. Pro:
                3 Nephi 14:6 [861] refers to "swine."

                1.3.4.10.2.4.1.6.4. Pro:
                If there were pigs in pre-Columbian America, pig bones should have been found dating from that time.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.1.6.5. Pro:
                If there were pigs in pre-Columbian America, pig depictions should have been found dating from that time.

              1.3.4.10.2.4.1.7. Con:
              Any translation is impacted by the translator. Joseph Smith points to this in the introduction admitting that any mistakes are a matter of translation. lds.org [862]

              1.3.4.10.2.4.1.8. Con:
              Even today, critics railed against the Book of Mormon because it talks about horses and cattle. Archaeology has since uncovered evidence of both. fairmormon.org [863]

              1.3.4.10.2.4.1.9. Pro:
              The Book of Mormon refers to honeybees, which did not exist in the New World at the time.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.1.9.1. Pro:
                Ether 2:3 [864] refers to honeybees in the New World in Book of Mormon times.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.1.9.2. Pro:
                Honeybees were not present in North America until the seventeenth century [865].

                  1.3.4.10.2.4.1.9.2.1. Con:
                  Evidence of the handiwork of early Maya beekeepers [866] has been unearthed at the ancient city of Nakum in northeastern Guatemala.

                    1.3.4.10.2.4.1.9.2.1.1. Pro:
                    Stingless bees, like those that the Maya beekeepers used, make honey, are found in both the Middle East and Latin America, and are sometimes attracted to dead carcasses [867] like the carcass described in Judges 14:8-9 [868].

              1.3.4.10.2.4.1.10. Pro:
              The Book of Mormon refers to domesticated sheep, yet there were no domesticated sheep in North America until European colonists [869].

                1.3.4.10.2.4.1.10.1. Pro:
                Ether 9:18 [870] refers to domesticated sheep.

            1.3.4.10.2.4.2. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon refers to crops that did not exist in the Americas during the book's purported time frame.

              1.3.4.10.2.4.2.1. Con:
              Since no specific scripture versus or crops are referred to, this claim cannot be argued. All crops mentioned in the Book of Mormon can be found in the Pre Columbian Americas.

              1.3.4.10.2.4.2.2. Pro:
              Wheat is mentioned in the Book of Mormon (Mosiah 9:9 [871]), yet wheat was introduced to the Americas by [872] the English colonists.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.2.2.1. Con:
                The wheat in the Book of Mormon is [873] amaranth, translated using 19th-century language.

                  1.3.4.10.2.4.2.2.1.1. Con:
                  "Amaranth" was a known word in English by the 1610s [874], and the BoM author could have used it instead of "wheat" if he really meant "amaranth."

                  1.3.4.10.2.4.2.2.1.2. Con:
                  Amaranth [875] and wheat [876] do not resemble each other.

                    1.3.4.10.2.4.2.2.1.2.1. Pro:
                    Amaranth is pink while wheat is yellow.

                  1.3.4.10.2.4.2.2.1.3. Con:
                  Taxonomically, wheat [877] and amaranth [878] belong to a different superorder.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.2.2.2. Con:
                Wheat existed in the Americas before colonists arrived but it died out.

            1.3.4.10.2.4.3. Con:
            Critics railed against the use of cement in the Book of Mormon. Since then, archaeologists have discovered [879] extensive use of cement.

            1.3.4.10.2.4.4. Con:
            Critics railed against the Book of Mormon for its multiple mentions of barley. Archaeology has since verified [880] three types of barley in the pre-Columbian Americas.

            1.3.4.10.2.4.5. Con:
            There are items and technologies in Greek records that archaeologists have never found in the record's purported place and time frame. Several examples will be highlighted here.

              1.3.4.10.2.4.5.1. Pro:
              Greek Fire: The Byzantines of the 7th to 12th centuries hurled a mysterious substance at their enemies in naval battle [881]. This liquid, shot through tubes or siphons, burned in water and could only be extinguished with vinegar, sand, and urine.

              1.3.4.10.2.4.5.2. Pro:
              Flexible Glass: A Roman invented flexible glass [882] that could be bent and would not break if dropped on a hard surface. There is no evidence of this beyond written records.

              1.3.4.10.2.4.5.3. Pro:
              Universal Poison Cure: Mithidrates developed a cure for all poisons [883] which cannot today be reproduced.

              1.3.4.10.2.4.5.4. Pro:
              The Archimedes Death Ray [884]: It supposedly consisted of a series of enormous mirrors that were arranged in such a precise way as to catch and focus sunlight to such a ferocious intensity that it could spontaneously ignite and burn Roman ships off the coast up to 1,000 feet away.

              1.3.4.10.2.4.5.5. Con:
              Book of Mormon critics can also consistently dispute the Greek records describing these supposed items and technologies.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.5.5.1. Pro:
                Some have expressed skepticism [885] over the story of Mithridates' panacea.

            1.3.4.10.2.4.6. Con:
            We have a very imperfect understanding of ancient technology. There are an array of ancient technologies that modern science cannot explain: [886] Damascus steel, the Antikythera Mechanism, Zhang Heng's Seismoscope, Viking Ulfberht Swords, the Iron Pillar of Delhi that won't rust, the Phaistos Disk.

              1.3.4.10.2.4.6.1. Con:
              We do [887] understand why the Iron Pillar of Delhi is resistant to rust.

              1.3.4.10.2.4.6.2. Con:
              We have [888] managed to produce Damascus steel in modern times.

            1.3.4.10.2.4.7. Pro:
            Based on known archaeology, someone from the ancient Middle East would not have had the technology to travel from the the Middle East to the Americas by ship or submarine as such voyages are described in the Book of Mormon.

              1.3.4.10.2.4.7.1. Con:
              The Phoenicians could have sailed around Africa as early as 600 BC as noted by Herodotus and demonstrated as possible by a modern voyage in a replica ship. Phoenician sailors located in nearby Sidon were among the best sailors of the times and favored by Xerxes who trusted himself to sail aboard their vessel. Lehi's party could have gone around the southern tip of Africa and caught the winds and currents to America. Ocean Voyage [889]

              1.3.4.10.2.4.7.2. Con:
              The Book of Mormon clearly states that the Nephites [890] and the Jaredites [891] did not use modern or ancient (or indeed any) navigation techniques. Rather, they were led by the Liahona [892] or pushed by currents.

              1.3.4.10.2.4.7.3. Con:
              -> See 1.3.4.10.2.4.6.

              1.3.4.10.2.4.7.4. Pro:
              There is no ancient precedent, circa 600 BCE or earlier, for a ship such as described in 1 Nephi of the Book of Mormon that would have been capable of transatlantic travel from the Middle East to the Americas.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.7.4.1. Con:
                The Book of Mormon claims that there should NOT be a precedent. Both the Jaredites and Nephites were taught by God on how to make these ships. If that is the claim, and we find a precedent, than that would be evidence that they were not instructed by God.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.7.4.2. Pro:
                It is implausible that if Lehi and his family knew how to make such a ship, this knowledge would have died out and never been used by his descendants to make ships of their own.

                  1.3.4.10.2.4.7.4.2.1. Con:
                  Some Polynesians have traces of Native American DNA ancestry [893].

                  1.3.4.10.2.4.7.4.2.2. Con:
                  Hagoth, who is a descendant of Lehi in the Book of Mormon, built ships and sailed away with many other Nephites (Alma 63:5-8 [894]).

              1.3.4.10.2.4.7.5. Con:
              The Kon-Tiki [895] expedition proves that it could have been possible to travel from the Middle East to America circa 600 BCE as described in the Book of Mormon.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.7.5.1. Con:
                The Kon-Tiki expedition used a recreation of Polynesian technology, rather than technology from the Middle East circa 600 BCE; therefore, it is not persuasive evidence that the voyage from the Middle East to the Americas described in the Book of Mormon would have been plausible.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.7.5.2. Con:
                The Kon-Tiki expedition [896] used modern navigation techniques, rather than any technique that would have been known in the Middle East circa 600 BCE; therefore, it is not persuasive evidence that the voyage from the Middle East to the Americas described in the Book of Mormon would have been plausible.

              1.3.4.10.2.4.7.6. Con:
              The use of updated technology is only a prerequisite if you don't believe ancient civilizations had great technology.  For example, expertise with the Ark of Noah would have made Nephi's ship feasible, yet one may still claim the Ark of Noah was not historic.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.7.6.1. Con:
                Archaeologists still scratch their heads on how Ancient peoples could have built the Pyramids, etc. We simply do not know what ancient technologies / techniques have been lost. ancient-origins.net [897]

              1.3.4.10.2.4.7.7. Pro:
              There is no ancient precedent, circa 600 BCE or earlier, for a compass-like device such as the Liahona [898] as described in 1 Nephi of the Book of Mormon, which would have allowed for ocean navigation.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.7.7.1. Pro:
                Even if Lehi was astonished by the Liahona, Alma 37:38 [899] says that Liahona "is, being interpreted, a compass." This means that according to the Book of Mormon, people in Book of Mormon times would have had a concept of a compass, yet the compass was not discovered [900] until centuries later.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.7.7.2. Con:
                An ancient Olmec compass [901] was found in Mexico. This is significant because the Olmecs lived around [902] 1200-400 BCE.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.7.7.3. Con:
                The Liahona [903] was provided by God, not created by man and therefore is unique like the Ark of the Covenant or Staff of Moses.

                  1.3.4.10.2.4.7.7.3.1. Con:
                  This doesn't help the Mormon apologist, since there is also no archaeological evidence for compass-like objects that point in the direction of travel rather than north.

                    1.3.4.10.2.4.7.7.3.1.1. Con:
                    Unlike any modern Christian has ever seen the original staff of Moses or the Ark of the Covenant, at least three modern individuals testify seeing the Liahona [904].

                      1.3.4.10.2.4.7.7.3.1.1.1. Con:
                      -> See 1.2.9.13.10.

                  1.3.4.10.2.4.7.7.3.2. Pro:
                  Unlike how a compass points magnetic north, the perfectly round Liahona only pointed the correct direction Lehi and his family should travel when living righteously (1 Nephi 18:12 [905]). If the Liahona were indeed a compass in this sense, it would be the only compass in the world that didn’t point consistently magnetic north or even consistently any particular direction.

                    1.3.4.10.2.4.7.7.3.2.1. Con:
                    -> See 1.3.4.10.2.4.7.7.3.1.

            1.3.4.10.2.4.8. Pro:
            Coins were not used by Native Americans [906], as the Book Of Mormon suggests [907]. None have been found, yet thousands of Roman coins [908] have been found throughout Europe.

              1.3.4.10.2.4.8.1. Con:
              The Book of Mormon does not specify that the objects used for exchange were literal coinage. They could have been any other items like shells or beads without contradicting the text. FAIRMormon [909]

              1.3.4.10.2.4.8.2. Pro:
              Alma 11:4 [910] refers to "pieces" of gold and silver that have "value."

            1.3.4.10.2.4.9. Con:
            The absence of such evidence does not necessarily constitute evidence of their absence [911]. It is rather an argument from ignorance [912].

              1.3.4.10.2.4.9.1. Con:
              If skilled archaeologists have spent a great deal of time and effor [913]t trying to find these artifacts but haven't found them, that reduces the chance that the artifacts were present at all. It may still be possible for the artifacts to exist, but the chance of this decreases each time there is an unsuccessful search.

            1.3.4.10.2.4.10. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon refers to glass, yet glassmaking was brought to the Americas by [914] the English colonists.

              1.3.4.10.2.4.10.1. Pro:
              Ether 2:23 [915] refers to glass windows.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.10.1.1. Con:
                The phrase "for they will be dashed in pieces" does not [916] necessarily mean that the windows are glass.

                  1.3.4.10.2.4.10.1.1.1. Pro:
                  "they" in "they will be dashed in pieces" refers to the vessels themselves containing the windows, which makes sense because [917] windows (glass or not) will weaken the structural integrity of the ship.

              1.3.4.10.2.4.10.2. Pro:
              Ether 3:1 [918] compares some stones to glass.

              1.3.4.10.2.4.10.3. Pro:
              Glass takes 4,000 years [919] to decompose, meaning we should expect to find glass artifacts in the Americas if there were any.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.10.3.1. Pro:
                According to the EPA, glass bottles can actually last a million years [920] in a landfill.

            1.3.4.10.2.4.11. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon mentions silk, yet silkworms didn't come to America until the colonial era [921].

              1.3.4.10.2.4.11.1. Pro:
              Alma 1:29 [922] mentions silk.

              1.3.4.10.2.4.11.2. Pro:
              Alma 4:6 [923] mentions silk.

              1.3.4.10.2.4.11.3. Con:
              The "silk" in the Book of Mormon could have come from the ceiba tree [924].

              1.3.4.10.2.4.11.4. Con:
              The "silk" in the Book of Mormon could [925] have been "of thread spun from the fine hair on the bellies of rabbits."

            1.3.4.10.2.4.12. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon refers to advanced metallurgical technology that did not yet exist at the times and places depicted in the book.

              1.3.4.10.2.4.12.1. Con:
