              The Book of Mormon describes Laban's sword having a blade of the most precious steel which is interesting because in that time, real steel was far more precious than gold. It was possibly made of meteoric iron. It was also likely a ceremonial weapon, an heirloom carried by the aristocratic Laban. Lehi in the Desert [926] The carbon contents of early irons ranged from low .07% to high .8%, the latter constituting genuine steel. In 900 BC, Egypt had tempering technology suited for swords. britannica.com [927]

                1.3.4.10.2.4.12.1.1. Con:
                The Book of Mormon also mentions metal swords being used in the New World in passages such as Mosiah 8:11. The cited source doesn't mention the New World having the technology needed for metal swords by the time of the Book of Mormon stories, so the objection still stands.

              1.3.4.10.2.4.12.2. Pro:
              The Book of Mormon falsely claims that indigenous American people described in the book had the technology to smelt gold, silver, iron, brass, and other metals from ore.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.12.2.1. Con:
                In Colombia alone, tens of thousands [928] of metal objects have been recovered with chronologies spanning over two thousand years. The Muisca, who appeared later in the region, (800 - 1600 AD) were accomplished goldsmiths. Research is currently attempting to characterize an earlier period between 100 - 1000 AD of metal work of the Nahuange, who used a depletion gilding process of copper rich-gold alloy to remove the copper and create a more golden appearance.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.12.2.2. Pro:
                -> See 1.3.4.10.1.8.1.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.12.2.3. Pro:
                The Book of Mormon claims that in the 20s B.C.E., the indigenous American people described in the book "did have an exceeding plenty of gold, and of silver, and of all manner of precious metals, both in the land south and in the land north," and that there was all manner of gold in both these lands, and of silver, and of precious ore of every kind; and there were also curious workmen, who did work all kinds of ore and did refine it; and thus they did become rich." (Helaman 6:9-11 [929])

                1.3.4.10.2.4.12.2.4. Pro:
                The Book of Mormon falsely claims that centuries before the Common Era, an indigenous American king went to a hill and "did molten out of the hill, and made swords out of steel" for his army. (Ether 7:9 [930])

                  1.3.4.10.2.4.12.2.4.1. Con:
                  Archaeological finds in Kaman-Kalehoyuk, Turkey demonstrate steel production as early as 1800 BC.Kaman-Kalehoyuk [931], which is thought to be the general area from which the Jaredites were from. They could have brought metallurgical technology with them.

                  1.3.4.10.2.4.12.2.4.2. Con:
                  Further analysis of finds at Kaman-Kalehoyuk indicate that steel was in production in 3000 BC in Anatolia. See discussion number 6. jiaa-kaman.org [932] The Jaredites emigrated approximately 2000 BC..

                  1.3.4.10.2.4.12.2.4.3. Con:
                  Steel can refer to any metal hardening with alloys. Metalurgy and use of alloys is well known throughout the Americas. This should not be confused with what our definition of steel is (iron and carbon), which was not invented until 1846. Steel is used throughout history, by all peoples, to describe hardened alloys.

                    1.3.4.10.2.4.12.2.4.3.1. Con:
                    The BoM swords probably contained iron because Mosiah 8:11 [933] describes them rusting.

              1.3.4.10.2.4.12.3. Pro:
              The Book of Mormon falsely claims that items made from gold, silver, and other precious metals were widespread among the indigenous American people described in the book.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.12.3.1. Con:
                This is an easily refuted claim. Not only do we know of these items but there are whole specialties of study surrounding them and which people did what with certain metals. en.wikipedia.org [934]

                1.3.4.10.2.4.12.3.2. Pro:
                -> See 1.3.4.10.2.4.12.2.3.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.12.3.3. Pro:
                The Book of Mormon falsely claims that in the early 300s B.C.E., an indigenous American civilization "became exceedingly rich in gold, and in silver, and in precious things,...and in machinery, and also in iron and copper, and brass and steel, making all manner of tools of every kind to till the ground, and weapons of war--yea,...the dart, and the javelin, and all preparations for war." (Jarom 1:8 [935])

              1.3.4.10.2.4.12.4. Pro:
              The Book of Mormon falsely describes the use of steel-making technology in Jerusalem circa 600 B.C.E., and then by indigenous Americans civilizations.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.12.4.1. Con:
                The Book of Mormon account of Laban's sword is accurate based on archaeological finds of swords found near Jerusalem as early as 700 BC.museum.imj.org.il [936] Vered Jericho Sword of Ancient Israel, 600 BC Steel [937]Steel Swords [938]

                1.3.4.10.2.4.12.4.2. Con:
                Although steel was certainly made in the Middle East around 600 BC [939], the Book of Mormon never suggests steel was made any later than 361 B.C. (Jarom 1:8 [940], See chapter summary) and never suggests steel was passed down to future indigenous American populations.

                  1.3.4.10.2.4.12.4.2.1. Con:
                  -> See 1.3.4.10.2.4.12.2.4.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.12.4.3. Pro:
                -> See 1.3.4.10.2.4.12.2.4.

                1.3.4.10.2.4.12.4.4. Pro:
                The Book of Mormon falsely claims that the sword of Laban [941], one of the items Nephi took from a man in Jerusalem, had a blade that "was of the most precious steel." (1 Nephi 4:9 [942])

          1.3.4.10.2.5. Pro:
          The Book of Mormon dates Christian ideas to an era before those ideas were developed.

            1.3.4.10.2.5.1. Con:
            -> See 1.3.4.10.2.3.

            1.3.4.10.2.5.2. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon's religious concerns date the book to the early 19th century United States.

              1.3.4.10.2.5.2.1. Pro:
              The doctrinal arguments presented in the Book of Mormon are, with a few exceptions, consistent with Methodism because Joseph Smith was "somewhat partial to to the Methodist sect [943]" at the time he dictated the book.

                1.3.4.10.2.5.2.1.1. Pro:
                The Book of Mormon is essentially consistent with Methodist views about fallen humanity that needs redemption.

                  1.3.4.10.2.5.2.1.1.1. Pro:
                  At Alma 34:14 [944], the Book of Mormon teaches that all of humanity is hardened, fallen, lost, and must unavoidably perish except through Jesus' atonement.

                1.3.4.10.2.5.2.1.2. Pro:
                The Book of Mormon is essentially consistent with Methodist views about a universal atonement, as opposed to a limited atonement as Calvinism teaches.

                  1.3.4.10.2.5.2.1.2.1. Pro:
                  The Book of Mormon teaches that the Christian god invites all humans to partake of salvation, which echoes Methodist teachings and is inconsistent with Calvinism

                    1.3.4.10.2.5.2.1.2.1.1. Pro:
                    The Book of Mormon teaches that the Christian god does not deny his grace to anyone who comes to him.

                      1.3.4.10.2.5.2.1.2.1.1.1. Pro:
                      At 2 Nephi 26:33 [945], the Book of Mormon teaches that the Christian god will not turn away anyone who comes unto him, without exception.

                    1.3.4.10.2.5.2.1.2.1.2. Con:
                    Historic Calvinism and Presbyterianism has always advocated the free offer of the gospel to all people in all times and all places, without excpetion.

                  1.3.4.10.2.5.2.1.2.2. Pro:
                  The Book of Mormon teaches that the atonement of Jesus is an unlimited sacrifice, which mirrors the teachings of Methodism in opposition to Calvinism.

                    1.3.4.10.2.5.2.1.2.2.1. Pro:
                    In Alma 34:12 [946], the Book of Mormon teaches that "there can be nothing which is short of an infinite atonement which will suffice for the sins of the world." This substantially mirrors Methodist teachings.

                    1.3.4.10.2.5.2.1.2.2.2. Pro:
                    John Wesley taught [947] that the atonement of Jesus was for all of humanity, including sinners, which is a teaching substantially mirrored in the Book of Mormon, and in opposition to Calvinism.

                1.3.4.10.2.5.2.1.3. Pro:
                The Book of Mormon is essentially consistent with Methodist views about humanity's free will to follow Jesus and be redeemed, or to turn from Jesus and lose salvation.

                  1.3.4.10.2.5.2.1.3.1. Pro:
                  At 2 Nephi 2:27 [948], the Book of Mormon teaches that all people are free to choose eternal life or to choose captivity of the devil.

                  1.3.4.10.2.5.2.1.3.2. Pro:
                  At 2 Nephi 10:23 [949], the Book of Mormon teaches that people are free to act for themselves, to choose eternal life or everlasting death.

                1.3.4.10.2.5.2.1.4. Pro:
                Early commentators about Mormonism noted the similarity between the faith and Methodism.

                  1.3.4.10.2.5.2.1.4.1. Pro:
                  Edwin Salter, upon listening to a speech by early Mormon John Taylor, stated that the Mormon doctrine he preached "seemed to differ but little from an old-fashioned Methodist sermon on the necessity of salvation."  Edwin Salter, A History of Monmouth and Ocean Counties (Bayonne, NJ, E. Gardiner & Sons, 1890), 253.

                  1.3.4.10.2.5.2.1.4.2. Pro:
                  In the 1840s, a writer named Josephine published an article describing Mormons in the New Yorker that said, "Setting aside [Mormons'] near approach of the Millennium and the Book of Mormon, they resemble in faith and discipline the Methodists." Times & Seasons, Feb. 1, 1841, 305 [950].

                1.3.4.10.2.5.2.1.5. Pro:
                The Book of Mormon's condemnation of infant damnation substantially mirrors the views of John Wesley, except that Wesley accepted infant baptism.

                  1.3.4.10.2.5.2.1.5.1. Pro:
                  John Wesley wrote to a friend, "No infant ever was, or ever will be, 'sent to hell for the guilt of Adam's sin;' seeing it is canceled by the righteousness of Christ, as soon as they are sent into the world." Letter, Nov. 21, 1776 [951]. This mirrors the Book of Mormon's position on infant damnation.

                  1.3.4.10.2.5.2.1.5.2. Pro:
                  At Mosiah 3:16 [952], the Book of Mormon teaches that "even if it were possible that little children could sin they could not be saved; but I say unto you they are blessed; for behold, as in Adam, or by nature, they fall, even so the blood of Christ atoneth for their sins." This substantially mirrors the views of John Wesley.

                  1.3.4.10.2.5.2.1.5.3. Pro:
                  At Moroni 8:19 [953], the Book of Mormon teaches, "Little children cannot repent; wherefore, it is awful wickedness to deny the pure mercies of God unto them, for they are alive in him because of his mercy." This substantially mirrors the views of John Wesley.

                1.3.4.10.2.5.2.1.6. Pro:
                The concept that living out of one's faith leads to sanctification as expressed in Helaman 3:35 is a feature of Wesleyan thought.

              1.3.4.10.2.5.2.2. Pro:
              The doctrine in the Book of Mormon strongly rejects Calvinism [954], because Joseph Smith disagreed with the Presbyterianism of his mother.

                1.3.4.10.2.5.2.2.1. Pro:
                In Joseph Smith's published history, he said that in 1820, he told his mother "I have learned for myself that Presbyterianism is not true."

                1.3.4.10.2.5.2.2.2. Pro:
                According to historian John Brooke, the Book of Mormon "explicitly rejected Calvinism." John Brooke, The Refiner’s Fire: The Making of Mormon Cosmology, 1644–1844 (Cambridge: Cambridge University Press, 1994), 13.

                1.3.4.10.2.5.2.2.3. Con:
                Objection! Speculation!

                1.3.4.10.2.5.2.2.4. Con:
                Correlation does not indicate causation.

                  1.3.4.10.2.5.2.2.4.1. Con:
                  While correlation alone does not prove causation, it is evidence of causation as long as there is a plausible mechanism for the causation.

              1.3.4.10.2.5.2.3. Pro:
              The doctrine of the Book of Mormon strongly rejects Deism [955], because Joseph Smith disagreed with the deist views of his father and grandfather.

                1.3.4.10.2.5.2.3.1. Con:
                -> See 1.3.4.10.2.5.2.2.3.

                1.3.4.10.2.5.2.3.2. Pro:
                The character Korihor in the Book of Mormon represents deists from the perspective of Joseph Smith as he dictated the Book of Mormon circa 1829.

                  1.3.4.10.2.5.2.3.2.1. Con:
                  Korihor denied that there is a God, whereas deists believe that there is a God. Therefore, deists do not assume the attitude of Korihor.

                1.3.4.10.2.5.2.3.3. Pro:
                In Mormon 9:15-21 [956], the Book of Mormon condemns deism by roundly condemning people "that have imagined up unto yourselves a god who can do no miracles."

              1.3.4.10.2.5.2.4. Con:
              The doctrinal claims and themes of the Book of Mormon are more internally consistent than modern religious discourse.

              1.3.4.10.2.5.2.5. Pro:
              The Book of Mormon generally reflects the common religious concerns of the Second Great Awakening [957].

                1.3.4.10.2.5.2.5.1. Con:
                Postmillennialism, which was predominant during the Second Great Awakening, is directly at odds with the very clear Premillennialism doctrine in the Book of Mormon. 1st Nephi 22 [958] 3rd Nephi 20 [959]

              1.3.4.10.2.5.2.6. Con:
              True doctrine is unchanging and, as such, would be the same for an ancient or contemporary audience.

                1.3.4.10.2.5.2.6.1. Con:
                While doctrines do not change, the Bible supports the claim that church policies and practices can and do change over time.

              1.3.4.10.2.5.2.7. Con:
              The Book of Mormon preempted religious concerns in the centuries following its inception. This explains why it appears to be written in modern times.

              1.3.4.10.2.5.2.8. Pro:
              The Book of Mormon teaches against the Christian Universalism [960] of the early 19th century.

                1.3.4.10.2.5.2.8.1. Pro:
                The Book of Mormon says that in the last days, "there shall be many who will say, Do this, or do that, and it mattereth not, for the Lord will uphold such at the last day. But wo unto such, for they are in the gall of bitterness and in the bonds of iniquity." (Mormon 8:31 [961])

                1.3.4.10.2.5.2.8.2. Pro:
                When the Book of Mormon was published, Christian universalists had been growing in numbers in the United States and had been in battle with other religious groups.

                  1.3.4.10.2.5.2.8.2.1. Pro:
                  Soon after the Book of Mormon was published, Alexander Campbell [962] argued that the book took a position on "all the great controversies" of the era, including "eternal punishment." (Campbell 1832, p. 13 [963])

                  1.3.4.10.2.5.2.8.2.2. Pro:
                  According to historian Ann Lee Bressler, the 1820-1840 period was when "Universalists were most openly and consistently engaged in battle with other religious groups, [and] was also the period of the denomination's most rapid growth and greatest overall vitality." (Bressler, The Universalist Movement in America, 1770–1880
                  (New York: Oxford, 2001), 54).

                1.3.4.10.2.5.2.8.3. Pro:
                Scholars, both Mormon and non-Mormon, generally agree that the Book of Mormon argues against universal salvation.

                  1.3.4.10.2.5.2.8.3.1. Pro:
                  According to Mark Thomas, the Book of Mormon "attacks" Universalism.  Mark Thomas, "Revival Language in the Book of Mormon," (Sunstone 8:20, 1983)

                  1.3.4.10.2.5.2.8.3.2. Pro:
                  According to Dan Vogel, "the Book of Mormon...explicitly attacked the notion of universal salvation." (Dan Vogel, "Anti-Universalist Rhetoric in the Book of Mormon" in New Approaches to the Book of Mormon, Metcalfe, ed., 1993, p. 47 [964])

                  1.3.4.10.2.5.2.8.3.3. Pro:
                  According to Mormon historian Terryl Givens [965], "the Book of Mormon refuted universal salvation." (Terryl L. Givens, By the Hand of Mormon, 2002, p. 187)

                  1.3.4.10.2.5.2.8.3.4. Pro:
                  According to Mormon historian Richard Bushman [966], "The Book of Mormon argued against universal salvation." (Joseph Smith: Rough Stone Rolling 2005, 199)

                  1.3.4.10.2.5.2.8.3.5. Pro:
                  According to Clyde D. Ford, "The Book of Mormon refutes 'modern' Universalism." (Clyde D. Ford, Dialogue 47(1), 2014 [967])

                1.3.4.10.2.5.2.8.4. Pro:
                Both Mormons and non-Mormons in the 1830s recognized anti-Universalist teachings in the Book of Mormon.

                  1.3.4.10.2.5.2.8.4.1. Pro:
                  -> See 1.3.4.10.2.5.2.8.2.1.

                  1.3.4.10.2.5.2.8.4.2. Pro:
                  E.D. Howe wrote about the Book of Mormon in 1834, "The name of our ancient Universalist is called Nehor [968]." (Howe 1834, 70 [969]).

                  1.3.4.10.2.5.2.8.4.3. Pro:
                  In 1835, Mormon apostle Orson Hyde [970] referred to "Nehor the Universalian" and "Amlici the Universalist." (Hyde, References to the Book of Mormon [971])

                  1.3.4.10.2.5.2.8.4.4. Pro:
                  In his journal, Mormon apostle Orson Hyde [972] referred to Corianton [973] (see Alma 41:3-4 [974]) as a Universalist. (Orson Hyde Journal Feb. 4, 1932).

                  1.3.4.10.2.5.2.8.4.5. Pro:
                  In 1833, a Mormon church publication noted that "the Universalist says [the Book of Mormon] reproaches his creed." (Evening & Morning Star 2: 108)

                  1.3.4.10.2.5.2.8.4.6. Pro:
                  A Mormon convert wrote in 1834 that he had read the Book of Mormon "just after its publication," and remembered how "it bore hard upon my favorite notions of universal salvation." (Messenger & Advocate 1: 9)

                  1.3.4.10.2.5.2.8.4.7. Pro:
                  Soon after the Book of Mormon was published, Joseph Smith's paternal grandfather Asael Smith declared on his deathbed (he died Nov. 1, 1830) that he "acknowledged that the doctrine of universalism which he had so long advocated, was not true." (quoted in Vogel, in New Approaches to the Book of Mormon n.3 [975])

                1.3.4.10.2.5.2.8.5. Pro:
                Joseph Smith had exposure to Universalism, in part, because his paternal grandfather Asael Smith was a Universalist.

                  1.3.4.10.2.5.2.8.5.1. Pro:
                  -> See 1.3.4.10.2.5.2.8.4.7.

          1.3.4.10.2.6. Pro:
          The Book of Mormon uses text and narrative patterns derived from sources that post-date the era in which the book is set.

            1.3.4.10.2.6.1. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon purportedly quotes passages from the book of Isaiah in an ancient Old Testament manuscript inscribed on brass plates [976] no later than 600 BCE, but these passages were not yet written in 600 BCE.

              1.3.4.10.2.6.1.1. Pro:
              Chapters 48–54 of the Book of Isaiah were written no earlier than 586 BCE.

                1.3.4.10.2.6.1.1.1. Con:
                The seven part structure of the Book of Isaiah is evidence that the entire book was written by Isaiah ben Amoz in 8th century BCE.

              1.3.4.10.2.6.1.2. Pro:
              The book of Mormon contains references to passages in the book of Isaiah written about historically later events.

            1.3.4.10.2.6.2. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon uses phrases, idioms, and analogies from the New Testament in passages ostensibly dated prior to the Christian era.

              1.3.4.10.2.6.2.1. Pro:
              1 Nephi 22:17 [977] appears to quote 1 Corinthians 3:15 [978], but 1 Corinthians was written long after Nephi supposedly lived.

              1.3.4.10.2.6.2.2. Pro:
              Alma 60:23 [979], quotes a letter, ostensibly dated to about 62 BCE, states that "God has said that the inward vessel shall be cleansed first, then shall the outer vessel be cleansed also." This refers to a Jesus saying that post-dates the story in the Book of Mormon. (Matthew 23:26 [980] KJV: "clean first that which is within the cup and platter, that the outside of them may be clean also").

              1.3.4.10.2.6.2.3. Pro:
              1 Nephi 4:13 [981], ostensibly dated to the early 500s BCE, states, "It is better that one man should perish than that a nation should dwindle and perish in unbelief." This likely borrows directly or indirectly from John 11:50 [982] ("Nor consider that it is expedient for us, that one man should die for the people, and that the whole nation perish not.")

              1.3.4.10.2.6.2.4. Pro:
              1 Nephi 11:22 [983], in a passage ostensibly dating to the 500s BCE, states, "Yea, it is the love of God, which sheddeth itself abroad in the hearts of the children of men." This is a predating reference to Romans 5:5 [984] (KJV): "[T]he love of God is shed abroad in our hearts by the Holy Ghost which is given unto us."

              1.3.4.10.2.6.2.5. Pro:
              1 Nephi 12:11 [985], in a passage ostensibly dated to the 500s BCE, describes a vision stating, "[A]nd their garments were white even like unto the Lamb of God. And the angel said unto me: These are made white in the blood of the Lamb, because of their faith in him." This borrows from Revelation 7:14 [986] (KJV): "These are they which came out of great tribulation, and have washed their robes, and made them white in the blood of the Lamb."

            1.3.4.10.2.6.3. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon uses material derived from William Shakespeare.

            1.3.4.10.2.6.4. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon uses material derived from John Bunyan's The Pilgrim's Progress [987].

          1.3.4.10.2.7. Pro:
          -> See 1.3.1.10.

        1.3.4.10.3. Pro:
        The Book of Mormon contains many ideas that were popular during Joseph Smith's era. The idea that America was a destined land [988] was a popular one in the early nineteenth century. It is an idea taught many places in the Book of Mormon as well.

          1.3.4.10.3.1. Pro:
          Popular historical sequences such as the coming of Columbus, the arrival of the early colonists, the Revolutionary War, and the introduction of the Bible among the Indians are all recognizable themes [989] in the Book of Mormon.

        1.3.4.10.4. Con:
        The Book of Mormon declares itself [990] to contain revelations to ancient prophets of future events in America. This could explain the "modern influences" within it.

        1.3.4.10.5. Pro:
        -> See 1.3.4.10.2.5.2.

        1.3.4.10.6. Pro:
        The names and language features of the Book of Mormon are best explained by a modern author.

          1.3.4.10.6.1. Pro:
          It's curious that Joseph Smith translated the plates he discovered into a version of 17th century English rather than that of his own time or an approximation of the era they were buried. This factor, along with the substantial borrowings from the KJV Bible, point very strongly to Smith having merely fabricated the Book.

            1.3.4.10.6.1.1. Con:
            If there was indeed a device that could translate any language by divine power, it would make sense that it would translate it into a dialect familiar to the person using it.

          1.3.4.10.6.2. Pro:
          "Moroni" and "Cumorah," significant names in the Book of Mormon associated with the buried golden plates from which Joseph Smith claimed to translate the book, may plausibly have their origin in stories of Captain Kidd that would have been familiar to Joseph Smith.

            1.3.4.10.6.2.1. Con:
            "Comoros", a significant name referring to the French islands may plausibly have their origin in ancient native American languages found in the Book of Mormon.

              1.3.4.10.6.2.1.1. Con:
              A more likely and generally accepted view of the origin of Comoros is that it comes from qamar, the Arabic word for moon.

            1.3.4.10.6.2.2. Con:
            "Treasure Ghosts" and simply put "Ghosts" are a major aspect of any nations mythological culture and are not exclusive to captain Kidd.

            1.3.4.10.6.2.3. Pro:
            Joseph Smith was familiar with Captain Kidd [991] stories prior to dictating the Book of Mormon.

              1.3.4.10.6.2.3.1. Con:
              Hadley's claim is not one of an eyewitness but rather based on "the history as considered by the townsmen." In other words, he is retelling unsubstantiated rumors. There is no verified connection between people searching for Captain Kidd's treasure and any of Joseph Smith's activities.

              1.3.4.10.6.2.3.2. Con:
              An attempt was made in 1830 by the Gem in Rochester to compare Joseph Smith's activities to an earlier story of a family with the last name of Smith (unrelated to Joseph) who moved into the vicinity of Rochester around 1812. They had a son who was 18. He was reported to have led a band in search of Captain Kidd's treasure. He used a stone that he looked at in his hat to aid in finding the location. They hunted for treasure at night. The Gem (15 May, 1830) [992] Later it was attributed to Joseph Smith.

              1.3.4.10.6.2.3.3. Pro:
              According to Jonathan A. Hadley, editor of the Palmyra Freeman [993] (1828-29) in Joseph Smith's home town, the Smith family spent most of their discretionary time "digging in the hills of Manchester for money, under the often-repeated impression that Capt. Kidd or some other person of wealth, had there deposited their treasures." (Hadley, Lowell Courier, July 28, 1842) [994])

          1.3.4.10.6.3. Con:
          The language of the Book of Mormon is consistent with the book being an actual translation by Joseph Smith, using language with which he was familiar.

            1.3.4.10.6.3.1. Pro:
            The events say that Smith "translated" it, not that it was given to him pre-translated.

            1.3.4.10.6.3.2. Pro:
            Translating a work from one source language into another target language requires you to make concessions in favor of the target language in order to make sense of the writing, especially when the translator is uneducated in the nuance of the source language.

          1.3.4.10.6.4. Con:
          The existence of Hebraic poetic structures in the Book of Mormon (such as chiasmus), which were almost certainly unknown to Joseph Smith, indicates a historical background to the book rather than a contemporary creative background.
          
          Chiasmus in the Book of Mormon [995]

          1.3.4.10.6.5. Con:
          -> See 1.3.4.7.1.1.1.8.

          1.3.4.10.6.6. Pro:
          -> See 1.3.4.10.2.6.

          1.3.4.10.6.7. Pro:
          -> See 1.3.1.10.

          1.3.4.10.6.8. Pro:
          The Book of Mormon uses Greek names, even though by the book's own terms, the people described in the book would not have known Greek.

            1.3.4.10.6.8.1. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon uses Hellenized [996] versions of Hebrew names, even though the cultures described in the Book of Mormon would have had no reason to Hellenize them.

            1.3.4.10.6.8.2. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon uses the Greek name "Timothy" even though by the book's own terms, the cultures described in the book would not have spoken Greek. (3 Nephi 19:4 [997])

            1.3.4.10.6.8.3. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon uses the Greek name "Lachoneus" even though by the book's own terms, the cultures described in the book would not have spoken Greek. (3 Nephi 1:1 [998])

          1.3.4.10.6.9. Pro:
          The Book of Mormon contains material directly copied from a 1769 edition of the King James Bible that Joseph Smith's family owned.

            1.3.4.10.6.9.1. Con:
            The Book of Mormon is a record of emigrants from Jerusalem and is, as to be expected, full of scriptural quotations also found in the Bible. The translation (1830) is also given in the language of the day, which is also typical, because when God speaks to men, he does so according to their own language and understanding.  Chapter 10, The Prophetic Book of Mormon - Hugh Nibley [999]

            1.3.4.10.6.9.2. Pro:
            The King James bible includes italicized words indicating where the translators have inserted words for readability. The Book of Mormon in general includes these italicized words when copying bible passages.

            1.3.4.10.6.9.3. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon includes passages that perpetuate known translation errors that existed in the 1769 version of the King James Bible.

              1.3.4.10.6.9.3.1. Pro:
              The KJV [1000] mistranslates Isaiah 3:24 [1001] as "instead of a girdle a rent" rather than "instead of a girdle a rope," and this mistranslation appears in 2 Nephi 13:24 [1002].

                1.3.4.10.6.9.3.1.1. Con:
                There is more support for the Book of Mormon translation of Isaiah than for the King James Version of Isaiah when comparing them with several other variants of Isaiah [1003] including Hebrew Massoretic, Hebrew scrolls found at Qumran, the Aramaic Targumim, the Peshitta, Septuagint, Old Latin. Vulgate, and Isaiah passages quoted in the New Testament.

                  1.3.4.10.6.9.3.1.1.1. Pro:
                  The variants [1004] prove that the Isaiah verses in the Book of Mormon weren't singularly pulled from the King James Bible.

                1.3.4.10.6.9.3.1.2. Con:
                One definition [1005] for the noun rent is "an opening" caused by rending. The verse using this definition for "rent" doesn't significantly change the meaning of Isaiah 3:24 [1006]. The Hebrew footnote for rent says, "rags".

              1.3.4.10.6.9.3.2. Pro:
              The KJV [1007] mistranslates Matthew 5:15 [1008] as "neither do men light a candle" rather than "neither do men light a lamp," but this translation error appears in 3 Nephi 12:15 [1009].

                1.3.4.10.6.9.3.2.1. Con:
                Using the word candle or lamp in this scripture doesn't change the meaning of this scripture and since the translation of the Book of Mormon was intended to use the style of the King James Bible, candle appears appropriate.

              1.3.4.10.6.9.3.3. Con:
              The Book of Mormon could have used KJV verses, even the mistranslated ones, to be more familiar-sounding to the Book's audience [1010].

                1.3.4.10.6.9.3.3.1. Pro:
                Doctrine and Covenants 1:24 [1011] says, "Behold, I am God and have spoken it; these commandments are of me, and were given unto my servants in their weakness, after the manner of their language, that they might come to understanding."

                1.3.4.10.6.9.3.3.2. Con:
                If the Book of Mormon author had chosen to use KJV verses in their corrected form rather than their original form, this would have had a negligible impact on the audience's ability to understand the BoM. Therefore, being "familiar-sounding" is not a good reason for the BoM to have retained KJV errors.

                1.3.4.10.6.9.3.3.3. Con:
                English Bibles other than [1012] the KJV would have been in use at the time.

              1.3.4.10.6.9.3.4. Con:
              The language used by the book of Mormon is much more complex [1013] than the KJV errors and word lexicon.

                1.3.4.10.6.9.3.4.1. Con:
                The complexity of the Book of Mormon's language is not relevant to whether it does indeed copy erroneous KJV passages.

              1.3.4.10.6.9.3.5. Pro:
              The KJV [1014] mistranslates Isaiah 13:21 [1015] as "and satyrs shall dance there" instead of "and wild goats will dance there", but this error appears in 2 Nephi 23:21 [1016].

              1.3.4.10.6.9.3.6. Pro:
              The KJV [1017] mistranslates Isaiah 13:22 [1018] as using "dragon" rather than "jackal," but this error also appears in 2 Nephi 23:22 [1019].

              1.3.4.10.6.9.3.7. Pro:
              The KJV [1020] mistranslates Isaiah 11:3 [1021] as "And shall make him of quick understanding in the fear of the Lord ..." rather than "And his delight is in the fear of the Lord ...", but this error also appears in 2 Nephi 21:3 [1022].

            1.3.4.10.6.9.4. Con:
            Under an all-knowing God, stranger things have happened than copyright infringement. The only way to disprove God's involvement would be to ask him directly.

            1.3.4.10.6.9.5. Pro:
            -> See 1.2.9.6.4.

          1.3.4.10.6.10. Pro:
          Some place names in the Book of Mormon are strikingly similar to modern place names that would have been familiar to Joseph Smith.

            1.3.4.10.6.10.1. Pro:
            -> See 1.3.4.10.6.2.

            1.3.4.10.6.10.2. Con:
            A significant number of place names in modern history are strikingly similar to places in the book of Mormon which argues for the books Ancient Origin.

        1.3.4.10.7. Pro:
        Some material from the Book of Mormon was derived from Joseph Smith's family and experiences.

          1.3.4.10.7.1. Pro:
          A vision [1023] by the character Lehi in the Book of Mormon is essentially the same dream [1024] that Joseph Smith's father had years prior to the publication of the Book of Mormon.

            1.3.4.10.7.1.1. Con:
            Lehi's dream contains significant differences and parallels what a bedouin would be familiar with, but unknown in Joseph Smith's day. Wandering in mists of darkness, such as those in a desert setting, would include a mixture of dust and fog which if experienced at night would lead to feelings of helplessness. Arabic poets also describe the world as a large and spacious field. Desert rivers are typically either clear small streams close to their origin or raging "filthy water" floods downstream.

            1.3.4.10.7.1.2. Con:
            Lucy Mack Smith first recorded her husband Joseph Smith, Sr.'s second vision well after the Book of Mormon was published describing Lehi's dream, and was influenced by the description of Lehi's dream.

              1.3.4.10.7.1.2.1. Pro:
              The similarities between Joseph Smith Sr.'s second vision and Lehi's dream may be explained by memory contamination. “You can’t just go by the verbal report and know whether something is a genuine memory or whether it’s a product of some other process—external information, suggestion, inference—unless you have corroboration.” - Elizabeth Loftus, a psychologist at the University of California, Irvine. popsci.com [1025]

            1.3.4.10.7.1.3. Con:
            Lehi's vision of the tree of life in the Book of Mormon mirrors that of Joseph Smith, Sr. because God gave both of the men the same dream.

            1.3.4.10.7.1.4. Pro:
            Because it happened years earlier, Joseph Smith would have known about his father's second vision, and had a memory of it in his mind, while dictating Lehi's dream [1026] for inclusion in the Book of Mormon.

          1.3.4.10.7.2. Pro:
          The family of Nephi, the main character of the first part of the Book of Mormon, in many ways mirrors the family of Joseph Smith, suggesting that the stories are informed by Smith's family dynamics.

            1.3.4.10.7.2.1. Pro:
            The character Lehi in the Book of Mormon was modeled on Joseph Smith's father.

              1.3.4.10.7.2.1.1. Pro:
              -> See 1.3.4.10.7.1.

              1.3.4.10.7.2.1.2. Pro:
              Like Joseph Smith's father, Nephi's father Lehi was a "visionary man [1027]," suggesting that the character of Lehi was modeled on Smith's father.

                1.3.4.10.7.2.1.2.1. Pro:
                Prior to Joseph Smith's dictation of the Book of Mormon, Smith's father had seven visions, which he told to his family.

              1.3.4.10.7.2.1.3. Pro:
              Just as Joseph Smith, who heard his father describe visions to the family, said he sought knowledge and received a vision [1028], the Nephi character in the Book of Mormon sought knowledge and received his own vision after hearing the vision of his father Lehi. (1 Nephi 11:1 [1029])

            1.3.4.10.7.2.2. Con:
            There is no resemblance to Lehi, a traveling merchant in the Arabian desert, who lived in a tent to Joseph Smith Sr. who lived in a log cabin in the woods of NY.

          1.3.4.10.7.3. Con:
          -> See 1.3.4.10.7.1.1.

          1.3.4.10.7.4. Con:
          -> See 1.3.4.10.7.2.2.

          1.3.4.10.7.5. Pro:
          Much of the Book of Mormon text relates to magic [1030] or esotericism [1031] that would have been familiar to the Smith family.

            1.3.4.10.7.5.1. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon text contains treasure guardian lore.

              1.3.4.10.7.5.1.1. Pro:
              In Helaman 13:31-36 [1032], a Book of Mormon character prophesies that there would come a time when the Nephites [1033] would be unable to bury treasure because the ground would become cursed, and treasures would "become slippery."

              1.3.4.10.7.5.1.2. Pro:
              In Mormon 1:18-19 [1034], the Book of Mormon states that the ground had "become slippery" because of a curse, so that any buried treasure would slip out of reach of the one who buried it.

                1.3.4.10.7.5.1.2.1. Pro:
                -> See 1.2.1.1.2.7.7.5.3.

              1.3.4.10.7.5.1.3. Pro:
              -> See 1.2.9.13.12.4.1.

            1.3.4.10.7.5.2. Pro:
            Certain Book of Mormon names are associated with magic or treasure guardian lore

              1.3.4.10.7.5.2.1. Pro:
              In English treasure guardian lore, the name "Alma" or names beginning with "Alma" ("Almazim" and "Almazin") were used to conjure a treasure guardian spirit. (D. Michael Quinn, Early Mormonism and the Magic World View, 1998 ed., pp. 197-98).

              1.3.4.10.7.5.2.2. Pro:
              In English descriptions of Cabala found in a book available near Joseph Smith's home, "Nephes," a name similar to "Nephi," was described as a name "called out by Magicians and Necromancers." (D. Michael Quinn, Early Mormonism and the Magic World View, 1998 ed., pp. 198-99).

              1.3.4.10.7.5.2.3. Pro:
              The name "Laman" in the Book of Mormon, which one scribe spelled "Lamen," is pronounced the same as the word "lamen," a magical scroll. The Smith family owned at least three lamens.  (D. Michael Quinn, Early Mormonism and the Magic World View, 1998 ed., pp. 200).

              1.3.4.10.7.5.2.4. Pro:
              The name "Mormon" is derived from "mormo," a word that to Joseph Smith would have referred to a specter that scared little children.

                1.3.4.10.7.5.2.4.1. Pro:
                People of Joseph Smith's era were quick to associate the word "Mormon" with a "mormo[o]," a bugbear or hobgoblin. (Howe, Telegraph, Mar. 1, 1831 [1035]; Mormonism Unvailed 21 [1036])

            1.3.4.10.7.5.3. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon refers to magical seer stones [1037] ("interpreters") and validates their use by "seers" consistent with Joseph Smith's practice prior to producing the Book of Mormon.

              1.3.4.10.7.5.3.1. Pro:
              2 Nephi 3:6-22 [1038] in the Book of Mormon describes Joseph Smith as a "choice seer."

            1.3.4.10.7.5.4. Pro:
            The reference to "secret combinations" in the Book of Mormon is a surrogate for a discussion about Freemasonry [1039].

              1.3.4.10.7.5.4.1. Con:
              References to secret combinations that do exist makes the book more authentic, not less. The insinuation here seems to be that because some of the words relate to Joseph Smith's era, the entire book is inauthentic. This is a huge leap of logic.

                1.3.4.10.7.5.4.1.1. Pro:
                The Book of Mormon predicted that a secret society aims to create a world government in Ether 8:25 [1040]: "For it cometh to pass that whoso buildeth it up seeketh to overthrow the freedom of all lands, nations, and countries; and it bringeth to pass the destruction of all people, for it is built up by the devil, who is the father of all lies." Today, evil powers are on the verge of accomplishing this, according to author and WWII commander William Guy Carr [1041].

                  1.3.4.10.7.5.4.1.1.1. Pro:
                  The secret society in question is spread throughout several groups. Most notable are the Freemasons [1042] and the Rosicrucians [1043]. Collectively, they are the "synagogue of satan" that Jesus refers to in the book of Revelation 3:9: King James Version (KJV) [1044], according to Carr.

                    1.3.4.10.7.5.4.1.1.1.1. Pro:
                    "Behold, I will make them of the synagogue of Satan, which say they are Jews, and are not, but do lie; behold, I will make them to come and worship before thy feet, and to know that I have loved thee (Revelation 3:9 [1045])."

                    1.3.4.10.7.5.4.1.1.1.2. Con:
                    Freemasonry is not the "synagogue of Satan" because Revelation 3:9 [1046] says this group will falsely claim to be Jews, yet freemasonry claims [1047] to be nonreligious, not Jewish.

              1.3.4.10.7.5.4.2. Pro:
              There are too many parallels between "secret combinations" in the Book of Mormon (e.g.,  the Gadianton robbers [1048]) and the popular, late-1820s understanding of the Freemasons for the similarity to be a coincidence.

                1.3.4.10.7.5.4.2.1. Con:
                -> See 1.3.4.10.7.5.4.1.

                1.3.4.10.7.5.4.2.2. Pro:
                Like the Freemasons [1049], the Gadianton robbers [1050] entered into oaths of mutual protection. (Helaman 6:21-22 [1051].)

                1.3.4.10.7.5.4.2.3. Pro:
                Like the Freemasons [1052], the Book of Mormon describes theGadianton robbers [1053] as having secret signs and secret words for identification. (Helaman 6:21-22 [1054]).

                1.3.4.10.7.5.4.2.4. Pro:
                In William Morgan [1055]'s 1827 exposé [1056] on Freemasonry, he described the Freemasons as wearing "a lambskin or white apron." Likewise, the Gadianton robbers wore "a lamb-skin about their loins." (3 Nephi 4:7 [1057]).

                1.3.4.10.7.5.4.2.5. Pro:
                On July 18, 1828, the Wayne Sentinel [1058], the anti-Masonic newspaper in Palmyra, New York, described Freemasons as an example of "secret societies and combinations" that are dangerous to government. This is equally descriptive of the Gadianton robbers [1059] in the Book of Mormon

                1.3.4.10.7.5.4.2.6. Pro:
                In March 29, 1827, the Morgan Investigator, published in Batavia, New York, ran an article warning "beware of secret combinations" in words attributed to George Washington, and calling Freemasonry dangerous to the best interests of society.

                1.3.4.10.7.5.4.2.7. Pro:
                The Book of Mormon describes a particular "secret combination" as requiring all its members to swear an oath of secrecy on penalty of losing one's head. (Ether 8:14 [1060]). Similarly, the Wayne Sentinel described Freemasonry on March 14, 1828 as requiring that masons swear to keep the secrets of freemasonry, "binding myself under the no less penalty than to have my skull struck off, and my brains exposed."

                1.3.4.10.7.5.4.2.8. Pro:
                In the Book of Mormon, the leader of the Gadianton robbers boasts that his "secret society...and the works thereof...are of ancient date and they have been handed down to us." (3 Nephi 3:9 [1061]). Likewise, Freemasons of Joseph Smith's era claimed that their practice dated back to ancient times.

              1.3.4.10.7.5.4.3. Pro:
              Freemasonry [1062] was an intense issue of concern where Joseph Smith lived in western New York after the William Morgan affair [1063] in 1826-27.

            1.3.4.10.7.5.5. Pro:
            -> See 1.2.9.7.4.

            1.3.4.10.7.5.6. Pro:
            -> See 1.2.1.1.2.9.

          1.3.4.10.7.6. Con:
          -> See 1.3.4.7.1.1.1.

          1.3.4.10.7.7. Pro:
          -> See 1.3.4.10.1.7.

          1.3.4.10.7.8. Pro:
          -> See 1.3.4.10.6.10.

    1.3.5. Con:
    The Book of Mormon makes many implausible historical claims which suggests that it is an inauthentic modern invention.

      1.3.5.1. Con:
      Archaeology has proven [1064] that writing on metal plates was a practice in ancient Middle East and Mesoamerica.

        1.3.5.1.1. Pro:
        Carved writings have been found [1065] repeatedly in Mesoamerica and around the world.

        1.3.5.1.2. Pro:
        There are examples [1066] of other ancient writings on plates from around the world.

        1.3.5.1.3. Con:
        -> See 1.3.1.4.7.

        1.3.5.1.4. Pro:
        The Future is Bright: As time goes on, the archaeological argument in favor of the Book of Mormon is likely to improve as new discoveries are made.

          1.3.5.1.4.1. Pro:
          The archaeological problem: [1067] Archaeology is at best a game of surprises. At any moment something might turn up (and often does) to require a complete reversal of established views.

            1.3.5.1.4.1.1. Pro:
            Modern archaeology's assertions regarding the origin of Indigenous Americans change as quickly as high school clothing fads. (For those not familiar with the life cycles of adolescent fashion modes, that means often.) 1491: New Revelations on the Americas Before Columbus. [1068]

              1.3.5.1.4.1.1.1. Con:
              None of the revisions to the prehistory of the Americas accepted by scholars have ever been compatible with the idea of migrations similar to those of the Jaredites or Lehi's family. Given that our refinements to American prehistory have never moved in the direction of verifying the BofM, it is safe to assume they never will.

              1.3.5.1.4.1.1.2. Pro:
              Archaeological reports are under constant scrutiny and revision as new evidence comes forward into the scientific community.

            1.3.5.1.4.1.2. Pro:
            If you lived in the 1700s and said that giant lizards roamed the Earth long before man, archaeologists could not have shown you any evidence to support your theory. That all changed in 1824 [1069] when the first giant lizard bones were found.

            There was no Hittite archaeology until some object was definitely proven to be Hittite, yet men were perfectly justified in searching for such objects long before they discovered them.

          1.3.5.1.4.2. Pro:
          Still learning: The archaeological argument in favor of the Book of Mormon is continually being strengthened as archaeologists make additional relevant discoveries.

            1.3.5.1.4.2.1. Pro:
            Our understanding of Mesoamerican civilizations is still changing [1070]. Scientific consensuses are continually updated as new evidence is found [1071]. A consensus is not enough to nullify evidence contrary to it.

            1.3.5.1.4.2.2. Pro:
            There is no archaeological evidence showing that the Book of Mormon is not an authentic ancient text.

          1.3.5.1.4.3. Con:
          The archaeological evidence for Book of Mormon civilizations is just as likely to become weaker as it is to become stronger.

        1.3.5.1.5. Con:
        Major archaeological institutions do not support The Book of Mormon's authenticity.

          1.3.5.1.5.1. Pro:
          The National Geographic Society stated in 1998 [1072] that "Archaeologists and other scholars have long probed the [Western] hemisphere's past, and the Society does not know of anything found so far that has substantiated the Book of Mormon."

          1.3.5.1.5.2. Con:
          Regarding the NGS, their statement of not knowing of anything that can substantiate the Book of Mormon, does not equal dismissal. Rather it leaves the door open to exploration as any responsible organization should.

          1.3.5.1.5.3. Pro:
          The Smithsonian Institution [1073] does not use the Book of Mormon to Guide Research.

            1.3.5.1.5.3.1. Con:
            The Smithsonian's current statement says simply that it does not use the Book of Mormon to guide research. There are contributors to the Smithsonian [1074] who do not share that view. Any earlier statements of an unlikely connection have been redacted.

        1.3.5.1.6. Con:
        The idea that ancient people sometimes wrote on metal plates would have been known to people in Joseph Smith's time. Thus, he could have gotten this idea without divine aid.

          1.3.5.1.6.1. Pro:

              1.3.5.3.9.3.1.1. Con:
              Multiple examples [1108] of human bone exist across the Americas that predate the Jaredites by thousands of years. Anzick-1's skeletal remains included 28 cranial fragments, the left clavicle, and several ribs, around 13000 years old.

              1.3.5.3.9.3.1.2. Pro:
              Human hair, muscle, skin, eyes, and other soft tissues are likely to disintegrate after a few hundred years in most conditions.

              1.3.5.3.9.3.1.3. Con:
              In some conditions, hair can be preserved for thousands of years, depending on humidity, proximity to the corpse, and other factors. One example is the 9,000-year-old Chinchorro mummy from Arica, Chile. Another example is a 200,000-year-old hair sample found in dung. nbcnews.com [1109]

            1.3.5.3.9.3.2. Pro:

              1.3.5.3.9.3.2.1. Con:
              It takes 200-400 years to form a single centimeter of soil under regular conditions and just under 200 years in tropical climates to form a single centimeter of soil.

              1.3.5.3.9.4.1.1. Con:

            1.3.5.3.9.4.2. Con:

The Veracruz strike-slip fault segment in this location is capable of producing the type of destruction indicated in the Book of Mormon. Book of Mormon Geology [1116]

              1.3.5.3.9.4.2.1. Con:
              Merely pointing out a possible location where the destruction could have occurred is not enough to show that the destruction actually did occur.

            1.3.5.3.9.4.3. Con:

              1.3.5.3.9.4.3.1. Con:

        1.3.5.3.13. Con:

          1.3.5.3.13.1. Con:
          Stephens and Catherwood [1123] in 1841 did the first comprehensive comparative architectural assessment of Mayan ruins versus Old World equivalents, finding the Mayan architecture unique and distinct from Egyptian and other Middle Eastern ancient architecture. This debunked earlier, speculative claims attributing Mayan ruins to a Lost Tribe of Israel. Subsequent archaeology has confirmed that the ruins were built by Mayan Indians and have no link to the Old World.

            1.3.5.3.13.3.1. Con:

Pyramids are found among many ancient cultures [1124] because they allow people with primitive architecture to build high structures with a greatly reduced risk of collapse.
