      1.3.5.5. Pro:
      Genetic evidence does not support the Book of Mormon's claim that some native people of the Americas are descendants of the peoples described in the Book of Mormon.

        1.3.5.5.1. Con:
        Genetic evidence is not make or break for the Book of Mormon.
        
        The vast majority of ancestors which any given human, or group of humans have, have given that person or group no genetic heritage. This is because inheritance is binary, not analog. So things like the founder effect [1132] can leave you with a heritage that you will never directly be able to see in any genetic test you take, even if your entire genome is mapped.

          1.3.5.5.1.1. Con:
          The appeal to the Founder Effect requires that genetic ancestry of the American Natives is not traceable otherwise--that lack of genetic evidence supporting the Book of Mormon is attributable to a circumstance preventing our ability to distinguish that ancestry. However the ancestry of the American natives is positively traced [1133] (to the detriment of The Book of Mormon's claim), and with reasonable confidence, to originate around the Russia Far East region [1134]. So the Founder Effect is not applicable.

          1.3.5.5.1.2. Con:
          The Founder Effect results in a population retaining a subset of alleles from the source population. By bottlenecking the gene pool, remaining alleles available to the descendant population are attributable to this source population [1135]. This is especially the case where the derived population is then relatively isolated from other gene pools (i.e. by settling the Americas). Producing genetic evidence of the Book of Mormon's claim should have been easy as a result, had the claim been true [1136].

          1.3.5.5.1.3. Con:
          Genetic drift can be adjusted for to determine not only ancestral origin, but also the point in time at which the two populations diverged [1137]. The "drift" in genetics would serve to also corroborate the chronological claims of The Book of Mormon (i.e. if Lehi [1138]'s descendants lacked only those genetic characteristics developed by the Israelite population following roughly 500 BC [1139]). Rather the absence of such evidence is attributable to a falseness in the claim made by The Book of Mormon.

        1.3.5.5.2. Con:
        Nearly one-third of Native American genes come from west Eurasian people linked to the Middle East and Europe, rather than entirely from East Asians as previously thought, according to a newly sequenced genome."Great Surprise"—Native Americans Have West Eurasian Origins [1140]

        1.3.5.5.3. Con:
        -> See 1.3.5.2.

        1.3.5.5.4. Con:
        The text of the Book of Mormon does not directly connect any particular native American group with the people discussed in the Book of Mormon.

          1.3.5.5.4.1. Pro:
          Many modern-day Native Americans may have come from other migrations that occurred before or after those described in the Book of Mormon.

        1.3.5.5.5. Con:
        Haplogroup X [1141] (Origin: The Hills of Galilee) is one of the 12 Judaean lineages and has been discovered [1142] in North American (Hopewell) Indian genetics.

          1.3.5.5.5.1. Con:
          The X2a clade of this haplogroup is found among Native Americans, but "lacks close relatives in the entire Old World, including Siberia." [1143] Additional evidence suggests an "early split from the other X2 clades." [1144] This would not be expected if Haplogroup X came into North America from Israelites sailing to the New World as described in the Book of Mormon.

        1.3.5.5.6. Pro:
        No indigenous population from the Americas has been found to exhibit a contribution from any Middle Eastern population during the time frame of the Book of Mormon.

          1.3.5.5.6.1. Con:
          This is a bit of a spurious claim seeing as the reliability of access to human dna samples, in significant sizes, coming simultaneously from the same time period covering both the Americas and the Middle East are hard to find and hard to verify.

          1.3.5.5.6.2. Con:
          -> See 1.3.5.5.5.

        1.3.5.5.7. Pro:
        Apart from genetic contributions from post-Columbian immigrations, all known human DNA haplogroups among indigenous Americans are shared [1145] with or descend from haplogroups found in Asian populations.

          1.3.5.5.7.1. Con:
          One of these that is shared with Asian populations is also shared with Mid-Eastern populations. y-dna haplogroup 2 (M242) [1146]

          1.3.5.5.7.2. Pro:
          Human Y-chromosome DNA haplogroups [1147] show that the Y-chromosomes in indigenous Americans descended from Y-chromosomes found in Northeast Asian populations.

          1.3.5.5.7.3. Con:
          -> See 1.3.5.5.5.

          1.3.5.5.7.4. Con:
          The above reference only concerns MtDNA affinities.

            1.3.5.5.7.4.1. Pro:
            Mitochondrial DNA [1148] is inherited from the organelle called mitochondria in the egg from the mother. It is not paternal, as this organelle's DNA is not considered a chromosome. Studies of MtDNA cannot be regarded in the same light as paternal ancestries found in scripture. The mothers of scriptural peoples could have been from anywhere, and it would still fit the narrative.

          1.3.5.5.7.5. Pro:
          The phylogenic history [1149] of DNA haplogroups including those in indigenous American populations is most consistent with migrations to Beringia [1150], and then from Beringia to the Americas while the Bering land bridge existed during the last glacial period [1151].

        1.3.5.5.8. Con:
        "DNA studies cannot be used [1152] decisively to either affirm or reject the historical authenticity of the Book of Mormon."

          1.3.5.5.8.1. Con:
          DNA studies could, theoretically, be used to positively affirm the historical authenticity of the Book of Mormon, yet no such evidence has appeared.

          1.3.5.5.8.2. Con:
          One can never prove a negative with 100% certainty in any scientific field; however, you can reach a negative conclusion with a high probability.

      1.3.5.6. Con:
      Minimalist Facts Comparison: When comparing the evidences [1153] for the resurrection of Jesus Christ and coming forth of the Book of Mormon (manuscript reliability, empty tomb / empty stone box in New York Hill, first hand witnesses, women testimonies, etc), the evidence is equal to or stronger for the Book of Mormon than the resurrection.

        1.3.5.6.1. Pro:
        Both Christianity and the Restoration religions begin at the same location of a historical activating event. Jesus Christ was crucified (historical fact [1154]) in Jerusalem. The Jewish Sanhedrin could have squashed Christianity - just produce the body. The Book of Mormon prints in Palmyra NY, within 1 mile of the alleged hill where an empty stone box was excavated containing the alleged golden plates [1155]. The Book of Mormon was printed and the Restored church begins close enough to look for yourself.

        1.3.5.6.2. Pro:
        Gary Habermas and other Christian scholars show early and multiple sources bring documents close to the activating event of Jesus empty tomb and resurrection [1156]. Similarly, the original and printer's manuscript bring us directly to the activating event and evidence supports dictation and writing as claimed by early participants in the translation process. [1157]

        1.3.5.6.3. Pro:
        The geology of the empty stone box matches the geological resources available to Moroni [1158] from the areas nearby Palmyra New York. The descriptions of the stone box match something which could have been crafted by Moroni before depositing the plates. The burial of Jesus in a tomb [1159] also presents a plausible historical account.

        1.3.5.6.4. Pro:
        Groups witness the resurrected Jesus Christ, making it hard to dismiss their claims as hallucination or alternate theory. Also, two different groups witness the golden plates. The three witnesses see the plates in two groups of 3 & 2 with a angelic appearance. The 8 witnesses tangibly hold the plates and review the artifact very pragmatically in 2 groups of 4. In both cases, it is hard to dismiss claims of the golden plates or Jesus Christ as contrived. [1160]

        1.3.5.6.5. Pro:
        Early apostles and Christian believers suffered and died for their direct witness of the Resurrection. This is different than dying for a belief or a cause - it was suffering for their claimed witness. Joseph Smith and Hyrum Smith were imprisoned and died as well. David Whitmer was tarred, feathered, and threatened at gunpoint to recant his witness of the plates.In both religious movements, witnesses are willing to suffer extreme persecution while upholding their witness to their truth claims. [1161]

          1.3.5.6.5.1. Con:
          Joseph and Hyrum Smith died in a situation where recanting their claims about Mormonism would have been unlikely to save them, unlike in the case of the Christian apostles.

        1.3.5.6.6. Pro:
        There are a number of unlikely testimonies supporting the Book of Mormon.

          1.3.5.6.6.1. Pro:
          Enemies testify to the empty tomb and empty stone box in New York [1162]. In Matthew, even the Jews admit the tomb was empty when they claim the disciples stole the body - in itself an admission of the empty tomb. Multiple enemies from Palmyra believed Joseph Smith Jr obtained something from the hill [1163]. Enemy testimony convinced David Whitmer to investigate the matter - leading to his eventual conversion.

          1.3.5.6.6.2. Pro:
          Unlikely testimonies from women: Women as the first witness to the resurrection is extremely unlikely considering Josephus and the Talmud claimed that a woman’s testimony was to held in less regard than a thief's. Their being the first witnesses of the resurrection supports the overall testimony of the event because it would be an unlikely admission from the earliest sources. Women provide early and strong attestation to the golden plates and other artifacts [1164].

          1.3.5.6.6.3. Pro:
          Unlikely & skeptical conversions in early Christian & Restoration movements [1165]. Jesus' brother James is an unlikely convert turned leader. Josiah Stowell, someone who paid Joseph to money dig & never received financial benefit from the investment, testified to having seen the plates of gold and joins the church [1166]. His skeptical conversion is an unlikely testimony to the truth of the historical religious claims.

            1.3.5.6.6.3.1. Con:
            Joseph Smith could have gotten a set of gold plates with fabricated writing on them through naturalistic means.

        1.3.5.6.7. Con:
        The evidence for the resurrection is not persuasive.

        1.3.5.6.8. Con:
        There is no Mormon equivalent of the 500 witnesses to Jesus's resurrection mentioned in 1 Corinthians 15:6 [1167].

      1.3.5.7. Pro:
      The Book of Mormon incorrectly dates the date of Christ.

        1.3.5.7.1. Pro:
        1 Nephi 1:4 [1168] describes the Book of Mormon starting in the first year of the reign of Zedekiah, or 597 BC [1169], yet according to [1170] 3 Nephi 1, 4, 26 [1171] Christ was born 601 years after this. This would incorrectly imply Christ was born in 5 AD.

      1.3.5.8. Pro:
      If the Book of Mormon were true, we would expect its stories to have been preserved in Native American oral tradition. However, this has not happened.

        1.3.5.8.1. Pro:
        Dramatic stories such as the battle at Hill Cumorah would have been excellent candidates for becoming oral traditions.

        1.3.5.8.2. Con:
        As Native American oral traditions are indeed "oral", it would be difficult to evaluate whether any Book of Mormon related stories have been preserved, modified, or lost to the culture.

        1.3.5.8.3. Con:
        This implies that all Native American oral traditions are widely publicized and known to the author of the claim

    1.3.6. Pro:
    Theology: The theology laid out by the Book of Mormon is consistent with that of the Bible.

      1.3.6.1. Pro:
      Jesus Christ states His "gospel" message in 3 Nephi. The key theological gospel points match the "Apostles Doctrine" ref Acts chapter 2, Peter's 1st sermon on the day of Pentecost & a pre-Pauline church creed in Philippians 2:5-11. The match provides independent attestation of the same gospel being present in both Jerusalem and Bountiful supporting the narrative. Book of Mormon Gospel Matching New Testament [1172]

      1.3.6.2. Con:
      According to Helaman 14:27 [1173] the darkness at Christ's death lasted for 3 days, but according to Luke 23:44 [1174] it lasted for 3 hours.

        1.3.6.2.1. Con:
        Latin America is known for volcanic activity and "three days" of "darkness upon the face of the land" [1175] there is plausible without those in the middle east experiencing it for that long..

          1.3.6.2.1.1. Pro:
          As a note, Luke 2:1 [1176] says that Caesar taxed the whole world, yet he clearly did not tax everyone outside the Roman empire. Likewise, the darkness described in the Book of Mormon was localized.

      1.3.6.3. Con:
      According to Alma 7:10 [1177] Jesus was born in Jerusalem, but according to Matthew 2:1 [1178] he was born in Bethlehem.

        1.3.6.3.1. Con:
        Alma 7:10 [1179] doesn't say Christ was born in the city of Jerusalem, but that Christ was born in the land of Jerusalem. Bethlehem is only 5 miles south of the city of ancient Jerusalem, so definitely in the land..

          1.3.6.3.1.1. Pro:
          The land Jerusalem is consistent with the usage of the ancient Middle East. El Amarna letter #290 [1180] reports that "a town of the land of Jerusalem, Bit-Lahmi [Bethlehem] by name, a town belonging to the king, has gone over to the side of the people of Keilah."

    1.3.7. Con:
    Much of the theology and social viewpoint represented in the Book of Mormon is consistent [1181] with that of Joseph Smith's 19th century society, not that of antiquity.

  1.4. Pro:
  God Says So: God does communicate and has communicated to man that the Book of Mormon is an authentic book of scripture.

    1.4.1. Con:
    Claims to divine revelation are not a sound basis for proving historical authenticity.

      1.4.1.1. Con:
      If we accept the existence of an all-knowing God of truth, then God would be the ultimate sound basis for historical authenticity.

    1.4.2. Pro:
    Personal Revelation: God will verify [1182] the authenticity of the Book of Mormon to those who earnestly pray to him and ask whether or not it is true.

      1.4.2.1. Pro:
      It is logical for God to intervene on criticial questions that are too complex for us to fully answer on our own. Knowing whether the Book of Mormon is true is one such critical question.

        1.4.2.1.1. Con:
        This goes against scriptures throughout acts in which people tested the scriptures or verified the claims of the apostles through other scriptures or by reasoning and debating with the apostles.

        1.4.2.1.2. Pro:
        God has always worked through both reason and revelation.

        1.4.2.1.3. Pro:
        From a believer's point-of-view, (1) God is the loving father of all, (2) most of children are not archaeolgists, aincient near eastern literary experts or historians who might be able to answer this question scientifically, and (3) the question of the Book of Mormon's authenticity is critically important to the eternal welfare of each of God's children. Therefore, a loving God logically must provide a way whereby all of his children may come to know if the book is authentic.

      1.4.2.2. Pro:
      -> See 1.2.1.3.1.1.

      1.4.2.3. Pro:
      The Book of Mormon claims that a person can know its authenticity by: 1) Reading the Book of Mormon and pondering its message. 2) Remembering the mercy of God from the time of Adam until the present. 3) Ask God with a sincere heart, with intent to adhere to the implications if it is true, in the name of Jesus Christ if what you have pondered is true. God has promised that he will manifest the answer by the power of the Holy Ghost. [Source [1183]]

      1.4.2.4. Con:
      People of all faiths receive religious experiences within that associated faith's paradigm. For example, see said link youtube.com [1184]

      1.4.2.5. Con:
      People have sincerely prayed about the Book of Mormon without receiving an answer from God that it is non-fictional.

        1.4.2.5.1. Con:
        Simple prayer is not sufficient; action, experimentation, examination, and an understanding of revelation are also important to receive an answer through prayer.

          1.4.2.5.1.1. Con:
          People have prayed, acted, experimented, examined, been active in the church with good standing, served missions, and have been considered effective teachers of the doctrines of revelation, yet still have not received the above stated answer from God that the Book of Mormon is non-fictional. Anecdotal evidence of personal experiences. [1185]

          1.4.2.5.1.2. Con:
          This makes the belief about praying about the Book of Mormon unfalsifiable. If someone claims to have read and prayed about the Book of Mormon without being convinced it is true, anyone could just say "you didn't examine/understand it well enough."

            1.4.2.5.1.2.1. Con:
            The goal is to achieve a connection with God, not to use the Book as a means to find fault with others.

      1.4.2.6. Con:
      There is no credible evidence that religious feelings are messages from God.

        1.4.2.6.1. Con:
        Witnesses mention that they received messages from God after intense religious praying.

        1.4.2.6.2. Con:
        There is no credible evidence that chemicals in your brain that cause emotions, aren't released because of spiritual power, or divine instruction.

          1.4.2.6.2.1. Con:
          Brain chemicals, such as dopamin [1186], influence mood. Thus, chemicals do cause emotions.

        1.4.2.6.3. Con:
        God speaks through the Book of Mormons. Therefore religious feelings derived from the Book of Mormons are linked to the existence of god.

          1.4.2.6.3.1. Pro:
          -> See discussion #2629: God [1187] exists.

      1.4.2.7. Con:
      Anecdotal evidence that people have received answers from God about the Book of Mormon is unreliable to establish a genuine cause-and-effect relationship between praying and receiving a message from God that the Book of Mormon is non-fictional.

        1.4.2.7.1. Con:
        Circumstantial evidence is an important and relative part of establishing truth.

        1.4.2.7.2. Con:
        If someone had seen God, their anecdotal account of the experience may not be good enough to convince another, but  it would certainly be enough evidence for the person who had the experience.

      1.4.2.8. Con:
      If one follows logic that praying to a book will render it true, praying to the Lord of the Rings would be equally possible and transform a book of fiction into reality.

        1.4.2.8.1. Con:
        Unlike the Book of Mormon, Lord of the Rings is a work of fantasy, using supernatural artifacts and a magical premise.  It is very much the product of the crew and director. Therefore, it would not be credible to suggest that God would confirm its truth.

          1.4.2.8.1.1. Con:
          Praying to know if LOTR is true, and receiving an answer of its truthfulness from god according to your own understanding, would render it true. The answer would be magnified if there was found doctrine in LOTR to test in your life from which you could draw potential blessings.

          1.4.2.8.1.2. Pro:
          Orc stories never had a holy aura nor Spirit by which to judge them true.  Orcs therefore, of all the LOTR fantasy writing, must not have existed.

      1.4.2.9. Con:
      Joseph Smith proclaimed to"[s]eek learning, even by study [1188]" which implies that even believers of the Book of Mormon must verify its veracity by study, and not merely by feelings.

        1.4.2.9.1. Pro:
        Study also involves living the teachings of the book of Mormon and noticing the blessings it brings in your life. Study does not involve conjecturing to pinch out every possible way the book might be wrong as it's impossible to verify the power of god, in any religion, exclusively with science.

      1.4.2.10. Pro:
      Evidence is secondary to the witness of God to our hearts of the veracity of a thing. Evidence has power to strengthen a testimony based on true principles, but testimony based on evidence is flimsy.

        1.4.2.10.1. Con:
        Many people of different faiths claim to receive witnesses from God, but those witnesses often contradict each other.

    1.4.3. Pro:
    Spiritual Evidences: There are spiritual evidences that the Book of Mormon is an authentic book of scripture.

      1.4.3.1. Pro:
      The Book of Mormon inspires millions to do good.

        1.4.3.1.1. Pro:
        In 2012 alone, the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints participated in immunizing 8 million children in 13 countries [1189].

        1.4.3.1.2. Pro:
        Volunteers from The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints are often among the first to arrive at the scene of natural disasters [1190].

          1.4.3.1.2.1. Con:
          So are groups from almost every denomination of Christianity.

        1.4.3.1.3. Pro:
        The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints has been very active [1191] in helping refugees displaced from Syria and other war-torn areas [1192].

        1.4.3.1.4. Con:
        The Book of Mormon does not uniquely inspire people to do good.

          1.4.3.1.4.1. Pro:
          Even the Bible has 'inspired' people to do some very bad things to other people. The book of Mormon cannot claim to be only influential to the doing of good. Those following its precepts still have full responsibility for their own actions.

        1.4.3.1.5. Con:
        The Book of Mormon has inspired murder.

          1.4.3.1.5.1. Pro:
          The Book of Mormon story of Nephi's beheading of Laban [1193] inspired Ron Lafferty [1194] to slash the throats of his sister in law and her 15-month old daughter.

            1.4.3.1.5.1.1. Pro:
            Part of the motivation for Dan Lafferty to carry out his brother's revelation to kill a list of people came from the story in the Book of Mormon of Nephi's allegedly inspired decision to kill Laban as a sacrifice in order to carry out God's higher purposes. (Krakauer, Under the Banner of Heaven, pp. 165-168, 2003)

            1.4.3.1.5.1.2. Pro:
            Onias, a religious associate of Ron and Dan Lafferty [1195], said that Ron's revelation to kill a list of people "was similar to Nephi's commandment when he was commanded by the Lord to terminate the life of Laban [1196]." (Onias, The Controversy Between Ron and Dan Lafferty And the School of the Prophets [1197], p. 6)

            1.4.3.1.5.1.3. Pro:
            In a pre-trial hearing in his murder case [1198] to determine his competency to stand trial, Ron Lafferty used the Book or Mormon story of Nephi killing Laban to justify his murders. (Salt Lake Tribune, Jan. 3, 1985)

          1.4.3.1.5.2. Con:
          There is always a crazy in any group big enough.

          1.4.3.1.5.3. Con:
          The Book of Mormon teaches "Thou shalt not kill." Mosiah 13:21 [1199]

          1.4.3.1.5.4. Con:
          There is an account in the Book of Mormon where a murderer is tried by a prophet-judge and sentenced to death. Alma 1:9-15 [1200]

          1.4.3.1.5.5. Con:
          This is an exception fallacy: A: "This is how things usually are"    B: "Well, here's one isolated case where they are not. So you're wrong!"socialresearchmethods.net [1201]

          1.4.3.1.5.6. Con:
          Evil people can use scriptures to justify evil purposes: after Jesus Christ's 40-day fast, Satan tempted him to sin three times by quoting scripture. lds.org [1202]

            1.4.3.1.5.6.1. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon teaches: "Behold, the scriptures are before you; if ye will wrest them it shall be to your own destruction." Alma 13:20 [1203]

          1.4.3.1.5.7. Con:
          Widespread belief in The Book of Mormon reduces a society's murder rate.

            1.4.3.1.5.7.1. Pro:
            Utah has the 9th lowest homicide rate in the nation.deathpenaltyinfo.org [1204]

            1.4.3.1.5.7.2. Con:
            These two facts are correlated, but there no clear evidence that one causes the other.

            1.4.3.1.5.7.3. Con:
            It is easy to imagine that the Mormon prohibition of alcohol would lower incidences of homicide but that is a result of action, not faith.

          1.4.3.1.5.8. Con:
          The law in Israel had provisions of protection (sanctuary) for those who killed after God had delivered the person into their hands in the same way Laban was delivered into the hands of Nephi. Exodus 21:13 "And if a man lie not in wait, but God deliver him into his hand; then I will appoint thee a place whither he shall flee."

          1.4.3.1.5.9. Pro:
          The story of Nephi beheading Laban in the Book of Mormon is cold-blooded, premeditated murder. (1 Nephi 4:5-19 [1205])

            1.4.3.1.5.9.1. Pro:
            Abraham's household was built on peace and helping the weak.

              1.4.3.1.5.9.1.1. Pro:
              The only time that Abraham fights is when he is saving captives from a king who had kidnapped them, and he refuses to be rewarded for his violence.

              1.4.3.1.5.9.1.2. Pro:
              The only time Abraham fights it is to save his nephew Lot and the the kings of Sodom and Gomorrah from captivity, but the story ends in tragedy as Lot is unable to change the culture of Sodom and Gomorrah and it's destroyed.

              1.4.3.1.5.9.1.3. Con:
              God's work has always been built on peace and helping the weak. But this is not an honest look at the Bible. Joshua, Saul, David and others were commanded to go to war. Sometimes to kill the inhabitants of entire cities. This is very, very rare, both in the Bible and the Book of Mormon.

              1.4.3.1.5.9.1.4. Con:
              Abraham's houshold was built on sustaining God's work and obeying him. Abraham was willing to kill his innocent son [1206] because God commanded it.

            1.4.3.1.5.9.2. Con:
            At no point in the Book of Mormon does God command anyone named Nephi to kill his or her uncle. There are four figures named Nephi in the Book of Mormon. Of these four, only two have uncles who are mentioned. Nephi 3 (3 Nephi 1:2 [1207]) has an uncle named Lehi (the brother of Nephi 2, Helaman 3:21 [1208]), and Nephi 4 (4 Nephi 1:19 [1209]) has an uncle named Timothy (the brother of Nephi 3, 3 Nephi 19:4 [1210]). At no point does God command either one of these figures named Nephi to kill his uncle.

              1.4.3.1.5.9.2.1. Con:
              God commanded Nephi to slay Laban so that Laban wouldn't order his army to pursue them and prevent them from establishing a new nation in the promised land. 1 Nephi 4 [1211]

            1.4.3.1.5.9.3. Con:
            -> See 1.4.3.1.5.9.2.1.

            1.4.3.1.5.9.4. Con:
            -> See 1.4.3.1.5.8.

            1.4.3.1.5.9.5. Con:
            There is a Biblical parallel to this. When Israel was threatened, Judith beheaded the Assyrian leader Holofernes when he was drunk with wine. Judith was honored by God and her people for her act. (Judith 13:1-6) [1212]

            1.4.3.1.5.9.6. Con:
            It was an act of self-defense. Laban stole all Nephi's family's considerable property and actively sought to kill Nephi and his brothers. It is almost certain that if Laban lived, he would have sought out the family and killed them. (1 Ne 13:25-26) [1213], (1 Ne 13:12-13) [1214]

        1.4.3.1.6. Con:
        A book may inspire its readers to do good deeds, without being inspired by any gods.

          1.4.3.1.6.1. Con:
          According to the Bible, all that is good comes from God. bible.com [1215]

          1.4.3.1.6.2. Pro:
          Hindu, Buddhist and other religious texts inspire others to do good. It does not make them scripture.

            1.4.3.1.6.2.1. Pro:
            Many Muslims believe [1216] the Quran inspires them to do good.

        1.4.3.1.7. Pro:
        The overall Doctrine [1217] of the Chruch of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints, i.e. "Wickedness never was happiness", creates the correct motives for a happy life.

          1.4.3.1.7.1. Pro:
          Members of the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints are encouraged to fast (abstain from eating or drinking) once per month [1218] for two consecutive meals and to contribute the value of the food saved to the poor and needy.

          1.4.3.1.7.2. Pro:
          "By their fruits ye shall know them." Matt 7:20 [1219]

            1.4.3.1.7.2.1. Con:
            This is a misapplication of the scripture. This scripture is referring to knowing a person as a follower of Christ by their spiritual fruits [1220]. A person can be a follower of Christ and still have terrible theology. Just because they are a follower of Christ does not immediately validate every thought that they have.

          1.4.3.1.7.3. Pro:
          The Doctrine of the preexistence teaches that free will is critical [1221] to to growth and happiness.

          1.4.3.1.7.4. Pro:
          People are more inclined to follow guidance when it is the word of God because members of the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints believe that we were "created in the image of God" and that God is the Father of our spirits. It makes Him much easier to understand and believe in.

      1.4.3.2. Pro:
      -> See 1.2.1.3.1.2.

      1.4.3.3. Pro:
      The Book of Mormon is inspired by God.

        1.4.3.3.1. Pro:
