        1.4.3.3.2. Pro:
        The Book of Mormon contains prophecies that accurately predicted the future

          1.4.3.3.2.1. Pro:
          -> See 1.3.4.10.7.5.4.1.1.

          1.4.3.3.2.2. Pro:
          An entire book has been written on the accuracy ofBook of Mormon prophecies [1222].

          1.4.3.3.2.3. Pro:
          2 Nephi 29 verse 2 [1223] sates: [2] "And also, that I may remember the promises which I have made unto thee, Nephi, and also unto thy father, that I would remember your seed; and that the words of your seed should proceed forth out of my mouth unto your seed; and my words shall hiss forth unto the ends of the earth, for a standard unto my people, which are of the house of Israel..." This passage predicted the Book of Mormon spreading worldwide despite many critics who proclaim its falsehood.

          1.4.3.3.2.4. Con:
          Even if the Book of Mormon has successfully predicted events, it does not necessarily follow that its predictive power is due to divine foresight.

            1.4.3.3.2.4.1. Pro:
            By that measure, Nostradamus [1224] could be considered as superior to the Book of Mormon.

        1.4.3.3.3. Con:
        One cannot prove that anything is inspired by God, although many human actions could be inspired by people's belief in God. There is no logical or scientific way to prove that human creations are either designed by or initiated by God.

          1.4.3.3.3.1. Pro:
          Even if it could be proven that the Book of Mormon is inspired by God, that is not evidence for historical authenticity. The Book of Mormon could be "inspired fiction", teaching moral lessons using a narrative that never actually occurred.

            1.4.3.3.3.1.1. Pro:
            The Bible sets a pattern for "historical fiction" in that it contains numerous stories, such as those about Jonah and Noah, that most Christians view as inspired even though they are not historical.

        1.4.3.3.4. Pro:
        The doctrinal message is the same inspired message that is contained in the Bible.

          1.4.3.3.4.1. Pro:
          The God of the Book of Mormon encourages all of his children to repent and come to Jesus Christ. This is identical to the message of the Bible and is the key determinant of an inspired text.

            1.4.3.3.4.1.1. Con:
            Encouraging to follow Jesus Christ – and any other statement – does not prove it being inspired by God.

            1.4.3.3.4.1.2. Pro:
            "Whatsoever thing persuadeth men to do good is of me." - God (Ether 4:11-12) [1225]

          1.4.3.3.4.2. Pro:
          The Book of Mormon teaches that salvation only comes through Jesus Christ.

            1.4.3.3.4.2.1. Pro:
            "[T]here shall be no other name given nor any other way nor means whereby salvation can come unto the children of men, only in and through the name of Christ, the Lord Omnipotent." Mosiah 3:17

            1.4.3.3.4.2.2. Con:
            This claim alone is evidence of nothing.  The sermons of all ministers in Christianity can be supported by this.

            1.4.3.3.4.2.3. Con:
            The BOM teaches that you are saved by grave AFTER you do all you can do (2 Nephi 25:23) in contradiction to Ephesians 2:8-9

            1.4.3.3.4.2.4. Pro:
            "But behold, that which is of God inviteth and enticeth to do good continually; wherefore, every thing which inviteth and enticeth to do good, and to love God, and to serve him, is inspired of God." (Mormon 7:13) [1226]

          1.4.3.3.4.3. Con:
          The Book of Mormon teaches ethics and morality that are inconsistent with its being inspired by a benevolent god.

            1.4.3.3.4.3.1. Con:
            The Book of Mormon teaches: "Yea, come unto Christ, and be perfected in him, and deny yourselves of all ungodliness; and if ye shall deny yourselves of all ungodliness, and love God with all your might, mind and strength, then is his grace sufficient for you, that by his grace ye may be perfect in Christ" Moroni 10:32

            1.4.3.3.4.3.2. Con:
            The Book of Mormon teaches: "when you are in the service of your fellow beings ye are only in the service of your God." Mosiah 2:17

            1.4.3.3.4.3.3. Con:
            The Book of Mormon teaches: "God is mindful of every people, whatsoever land they may be in; yea, he numbereth his people, and his bowels of mercy are over all the earth." Alma 26:37.

            1.4.3.3.4.3.4. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon inspires racism.

              1.4.3.3.4.3.4.1. Pro:
              Based on Book of Mormon teachings, Mormon leaders taught until at least the 1970s that the pigmented skin of indigenous Americans was a mark of God, and that their skin would become lighter within a few generations if they embraced Mormonism.

                1.4.3.3.4.3.4.1.1. Pro:
                Joseph Fielding Smith [1227] wrote, as a leading LDS Church official, "The dark skin of those who have come into the Church is no longer to be considered a sign of the curse. Many of these converts and delightsome and have the Spirit of the Lord. Perhaps there are some Lamanites today who are losing the dark pigment. Many [native American Mormons] could readily pass as of the white race." (Answers to Gospel Questions 3, p. 123, 1953)

                1.4.3.3.4.3.4.1.2. Pro:
                A 1974 missionary pamphlet, published by the LDS Church, claimed that indigenous Americans were descended from Lamanites [1228], and that "Lamanites were marked by the Lord with a darker skin." (Lamanites and the Book of Mormon [1229], p. 2, 5-6)

              1.4.3.3.4.3.4.2. Con:
              The Book of Mormon contains an account of a group of pacifist Lamanites (indigenous Americans) who converted to the Gospel of Jesus Christ and were taken by a neighboring nation and protected militarily at great cost of resources and life. Alma 27:20-24 (and much of the Book of Alma) [1230]

              1.4.3.3.4.3.4.3. Con:
              The Book of Mormon teaches openness to all, regardless of race, sex, or ethnicity

                1.4.3.3.4.3.4.3.1. Pro:
                From the Book of Mormon: "he [the Lord] denieth none that come unto him, black and white, bond and free, male and female; and he remembereth the heathen; and all are alike unto God, both Jew and Gentile."

                1.4.3.3.4.3.4.3.2. Pro:
                Many passages in the book of Nephi attest to the openness of the gospel, e.g. 2Nephi 24 [1231]:" He doeth not anything save it be for the benefit of the world; for he loveth the world, even that he layeth down his own life that he may draw all men unto him. Wherefore, he commandeth none that they shall not partake of his salvation."

              1.4.3.3.4.3.4.4. Con:
              The terms in the Book of Mormon (and its literary analog, the KJV Bible) suggest that the various colored skins in the BOM can be understood more as a kind of authoritative garment. The BOM associates garment-skins with both the Nephite temple and competing Lamanite claims to kingship. This suggests that the garment skins (as the mark of the Lamanites’ curse) are understood as being self-administered, removable, and inherited in the same way that authoritative vestments in the KJV are inherited.

              1.4.3.3.4.3.4.5. Pro:
              -> See 1.3.4.10.1.5.

              1.4.3.3.4.3.4.6. Pro:
              The Book of Mormon teaches that, in contrast to the white skin of God's chosen people, the darker skin of indigenous Americans is the result of a curse from God.

                1.4.3.3.4.3.4.6.1. Pro:
                The Book of Mormon teaches that when indigenous Americans become righteous, their skin becomes lighter.

                  1.4.3.3.4.3.4.6.1.1. Con:
                  A Lamanite convert is used as a military spy specifically because his skin does not change color. Alma 55 [1232]

                  1.4.3.3.4.3.4.6.1.2. Pro:
                  The original edition of the Book of Mormon states that in the last days, when the Lamanites [1233] are taught Christianity, "many generations shall not pass away among them, save they shall be a white and delightsome people." (Book of Mormon, 1830 ed., p. 117 [1234])

                  1.4.3.3.4.3.4.6.1.3. Pro:
                  3 Nephi 2:14-16 [1235] states that "those Lamanites [1236] who had united with the Nephites [1237] were numbered among the Nephites; and their curse was taken from them, and their skin became white like unto the Nephites; and their young men and their daughters became exceedingly fair."

                    1.4.3.3.4.3.4.6.1.3.1. Con:
                    This sign is a chiastic parrallel with a night of no darkness [1238] that occurred in the Americas when Jesus Christ was born, and therefore may have occurred for a reason other than "becoming righteous."

                    1.4.3.3.4.3.4.6.1.3.2. Con:
                    Some of the Lamanites mentioned here may not have become righteous.

                      1.4.3.3.4.3.4.6.1.3.2.1. Pro:
                      The Nephites and Lamanites joined together to battle a common foe who threatened to destroy them all: "the Gadianton robbers had become so numerous, and did slay so many of the people, and did lay waste so many cities, and did spread so much death and carnage throughout the land, that it became expedient that all the people, both the Nephites and the Lamanites, should take up arms against them." 3 Nephi 2:11 [1239] Therefore, many Lamanites may have joined for military reasons rather than religious.

                  1.4.3.3.4.3.4.6.1.4. Pro:
                  4 Nephi 1:10 [1240] states that during a long period of righteousness, the Nephites [1241] "did wax strong, and did multiply exceedingly fast, and became an exceedingly fair and delightsome people."

                    1.4.3.3.4.3.4.6.1.4.1. Con:
                    The top definition of fair in Merriam Webster [1242] is "marked by impartiality and honesty : free from self-interest, prejudice, or favoritism."

                    1.4.3.3.4.3.4.6.1.4.2. Con:
                    The second top definition of fair in the Merriam Webster [1243] dictionary is: "conforming with the established rules."

                    1.4.3.3.4.3.4.6.1.4.3. Con:
                    Fair here is a reference to the character of those who repent of their sins and come unto Christ and is defined as "pleasing to the eye or mind especially because of fresh, charming, or flawless quality - The innkeeper had two fair daughters." merriam-webster.com [1244]

                1.4.3.3.4.3.4.6.2. Pro:
                1 Nephi 12:23 [1245] prophecies that after the Lamanites [1246] "had dwindled in unbelief they became a dark...people."

                1.4.3.3.4.3.4.6.3. Pro:
                In contrast to the Lamanites, 1 Nephi 13:15 [1247] describes European immigrants to the Americas as "white, and exceedingly fair and beautiful, like unto [the Nephites [1248]] before they were slain."

                1.4.3.3.4.3.4.6.4. Pro:
                2 Nephi 5:21-23 [1249] states that the Lamanites [1250] were initially "white, and exceedingly fair and delightsome," but because of their wickedness, "that they might not be enticing unto my people the Lord God did cause a skin of blackness to come upon them." Unless they repent, "they shall be loathsome unto thy people,...[a]nd cursed shall be the seed of him that mixeth with their seed; for they shall be cursed even with the same cursing. And the Lord spake it, and it was done."

                1.4.3.3.4.3.4.6.5. Pro:
                The Jacob character in the Book of Mormon preaches to the Nephites [1251] about the "cursing which hath come upon [the Lamanites [1252]'] skins...because of their fathers," and states, "I fear that unless ye shall repent of your sins that their skins will be whiter than yours, when ye shall be brought with them before the throne of God." (Jacob 3:5-9 [1253])

                1.4.3.3.4.3.4.6.6. Pro:
                Alma 3:6-7 [1254] states that "the skins of the Lamanites [1255] were dark, according to the mark which was set upon their fathers, which was a curse upon them because of their transgression,...therefore they were cursed; and the Lord God did set a mark upon them."

                1.4.3.3.4.3.4.6.7. Pro:
                Alma 3:14-15 [1256] states that "the Lamanites [1257] have I cursed, and I will set a mark on them that they and their seed may be separated from thee and thy seed....And again: I will set a mark upon him that mingleth his seed with thy brethren, that they may be cursed also."

                1.4.3.3.4.3.4.6.8. Pro:
                Mormon 5:15 [1258] is a prophecy that in the last days, the Lamanites [1259] "shall be scattered, and shall become a dark, a filthy, and a loathsome people, beyond the description of that which ever hath been amongst us, yea, even that which hath been among the Lamanites, and this because of their unbelief and idolatry."

              1.4.3.3.4.3.4.7. Pro:
              The Book of Mormon teaches against miscegenation.

                1.4.3.3.4.3.4.7.1. Pro:
                -> See 1.4.3.3.4.3.4.6.4.

                1.4.3.3.4.3.4.7.2. Pro:
                Alma 3:8-9 [1260] teaches that curse of dark skin God put upon the Lamanites [1261] "was done that their seed might be distinguished from the seed of their brethren, that thereby the Lord God might preserve his people, that they might not mix and believe in incorrect traditions which would prove their destruction. And it came to pass that whoever did mingle his seed with that of the Lamanites did bring the same curse upon his seed."

                1.4.3.3.4.3.4.7.3. Pro:
                -> See 1.4.3.3.4.3.4.6.7.

            1.4.3.3.4.3.5. Con:
            The God described in the Bible was not "benevolent" in our regular mortal human definition of the word. On the contrary, He was quite violent and commanded His followers to murder, harm themselves, or lay down thier lives on many different occasions.

            1.4.3.3.4.3.6. Con:
            By this same reasoning the Bible fails spectacularly. Case in point- the flood, which wiped out every living thing including children (except a few who were chosen and whatever animals could fit on the ark). Nothing short of genocide.

            1.4.3.3.4.3.7. Con:
            The Bible, by comparison, contains similar accounts. For example, when Israel took the city of Jericho, (Joshua 6:21) they slew everyone, young and old, even animals, except Rahab and her household. The Lord also slew over 50,000 men who looked into the ark. (1 Sam 6:19) Saul is commanded to kill all the Amalekites including infants and animals. (1 Sam 15:3) The Lord killed 70,000 by pestilence. (2 Sam 24:15) Elijah killed 450 prophets of Baal. (1 Kings 18:22,40)

            1.4.3.3.4.3.8. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon teaches that God is responsible for the ethnic cleansing [1262] of indigenous Americans.

              1.4.3.3.4.3.8.1. Con:
              God punishing nations who abandon Him is consistent with the God of the Bible: "For the LORD will strike Israel, as a reed is shaken in the water. He will uproot Israel from this good land which He gave to their fathers, and will scatter them beyond the River, because they have made their wooden images, provoking the LORD to anger" (1 Kings 14-15 [1263]).

              1.4.3.3.4.3.8.2. Con:
              -> See 1.4.3.3.4.3.4.3.1.

              1.4.3.3.4.3.8.3. Con:
              Like the Bible [1264], The Book of Mormon teaches that those who serve and love God and their fellow men will be blessed and prospered and those who sin against him will be punished. Nothing in the Book of Mormon teaches there will be an ethnic cleansing.

              1.4.3.3.4.3.8.4. Con:
              The Book of Mormon teaches that God will "remember my covenant which I have made unto my people (Lamanites). . . and I will bring my gospel unto them. . . . The Gentiles shall not have power over you; . . . and ye shall come unto the knowledge of the fulness of my gospel. But if the Gentiles will repent and return unto me, . . . behold, they shall be numbered among my people, O house of Israel. And I will not suffer my people (Lamanites). . . [to] tread them down” (3 Nephi 16:10—14)

              1.4.3.3.4.3.8.5. Con:
              The Book of Mormon contains many prophecies that the Gentiles will join the Lamanites, not the other way around. If we are to be saved, we must move in their direction. This land is given for their inheritance (3 Nephi 16:16 [1265]) and they will build the New Jerusalem (3 Nephi 21:19 [1266],22-24). (The Two Ways, Brother Brigham Challenges the Saints- Hugh Nibley) [1267]

              1.4.3.3.4.3.8.6. Pro:
              The Book of Mormon teaches that if indigenous Americans are wicked, Jesus "will bring other nations unto them, and he will give them power, and he will take away from them the lands of their possessions, and he will cause them to be scattered and smitten." (1 Nephi 1:11 [1268])

                1.4.3.3.4.3.8.6.1. Con:
                -> See 1.4.3.3.4.3.4.3.1.

                1.4.3.3.4.3.8.6.2. Con:
                This is not an ethnic cleansing. It is a punishment for sin, which is a consistent message across all Christianity, Judaism, and many other religions.

                  1.4.3.3.4.3.8.6.2.1. Pro:
                  -> See 1.4.3.3.4.3.8.3.

              1.4.3.3.4.3.8.7. Pro:
              1 Nephi 13:14 [1269] the character Nephi [1270] has a vision of the last days in which he "beheld the wrath of God, that it was upon the seed of my brethren, and they were scattered before the Gentiles and were smitten."

                1.4.3.3.4.3.8.7.1. Con:
                The Book of Mormon makes no claims anywhere that indigenous Americans are the descendants of the Lamanites.

                  1.4.3.3.4.3.8.7.1.1. Con:
                  This is somewhat correct within the ancient scripture itself, but Book of Mormon scholars agree that at least some, perhaps not all, are indeed descendants of the Lamanite people. Loosely, you could say the scripture itself does make this claim via prophecies that they would be reclaimed by the Gentiles.

                  1.4.3.3.4.3.8.7.1.2. Con:
                  The introduction to the Book of Mormon make the claim that the indigenous people of America are descended from the laminites (see the introduction page of the Book of Mormon, second paragraph, last sentence [1271]).

            1.4.3.3.4.3.9. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon tells a story of a character who is constrained by the Holy Spirit to commit premeditated murder, fraud, burglary, and kidnapping for a religious purpose. 1 Nephi 4:5-38 [1272].

              1.4.3.3.4.3.9.1. Con:
              John Welch [1273] and Val Larsen [1274] have argued that Nephi's actions were both morally acceptable and necessary, from the perspectives of the legal milieu of ancient Israel as well as a moral ethic applicable today.

              1.4.3.3.4.3.9.2. Pro:
              -> See 1.4.3.1.5.9.

              1.4.3.3.4.3.9.3. Pro:
              Nephi commits fraud and burglary when he impersonates Laban in order to steal the brass plates from Laban's treasury. (1 Nephi 4:19-27 [1275])

              1.4.3.3.4.3.9.4. Pro:
              Nephi commits kidnapping when he forcefully seized Zoram [1276], the servant of Laban [1277], and in exchange for his life made him swear an oath not to flee. (1 Nephi 4:30-38 [1278])

                1.4.3.3.4.3.9.4.1. Con:
                Kidnapping [1279] requires forced abduction, usually with malicious intent. Zoram goes willingly with Nephi et al., whose  motivations are to avoid their family's destruction [1280].

              1.4.3.3.4.3.9.5. Pro:
              After Nephi killed Laban with Laban's sword [1281], which Nephi admired for its pure gold hilt, precious steel, and fine workmanship, Nephi stole the sword. (1 Nephi 4:9, 19-21 [1282])

            1.4.3.3.4.3.10. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon teaches that Christopher Columbus [1283] was inspired by God, despite his immoral behavior in relation to the indigenous people he encountered. (1 Nephi 13:12 [1284])

            1.4.3.3.4.3.11. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon tells a story in which the character Alma could have stopped the massacre of women and children, but the Holy Spirit constrained him to allow the massacre to proceed, in order to ensure that the perpetrators were subject to an eternal punishment. (Alma 14:8-11 [1285])

          1.4.3.3.4.4. Con:
          God as portrayed in the Book of Mormon differs wildly from God as portrayed in the Bible. So if the Book of Mormon is inspired by a god, it must be a different god than the one portrayed in the Bible.

            1.4.3.3.4.4.1. Con:
            The Bible and the Book of Mormon teach that salvation comes only through Jesus Christ.

              1.4.3.3.4.4.1.1. Pro:
              The New Testament, Jesus says: "I am the way, the truth, and the life: no man cometh unto the Father, but by me." biblegateway.com [1286]

              1.4.3.3.4.4.1.2. Pro:
              The Book of Mormon teaches: "salvation cometh to none such except it be through repentance and faith on the Lord Jesus Christ."lds.org [1287]

            1.4.3.3.4.4.2. Con:
            The Bible and Book of Mormon teach that keeping the commandments leads to prosperity.

              1.4.3.3.4.4.2.1. Pro:
              The Bible teaches: "This book of the law shall not depart out of thy mouth; but thou shalt meditate therein day and night, that thou mayest observe to do according to all that is written therein: for then thou shalt make thy way prosperous, and then thou shalt have good success." Joshua 1:8 [1288]

              1.4.3.3.4.4.2.2. Pro:
              The Book of Mormon teaches: "And he [the Lord] hath said that: Inasmuch as ye shall keep my commandments ye shall prosper in the land." 2 Nephi 1:20 [1289]

            1.4.3.3.4.4.3. Con:
            The Bible and the Book of Mormon teach that through faith men are saved by grace.

              1.4.3.3.4.4.3.1. Pro:
              The Bible teaches: "For by grace are ye saved through faith" Ephesians 2:8 [1290]

              1.4.3.3.4.4.3.2. Pro:
              The Book of Mormon teaches: "it is by grace that we are saved." 2 Nephi 25:23 [1291]

            1.4.3.3.4.4.4. Con:
            The Bible and Book of Mormon teaches that God loves his children.

              1.4.3.3.4.4.4.1. Pro:
              The Bible teaches: "For God so loved the world, that he gave his only begotten Son, that whosoever believeth in him should not perish, but have everlasting life." John 3:16 [1292]

              1.4.3.3.4.4.4.2. Pro:
              The Book of Mormon teaches: "Yea, it is the love of God, which sheddeth itself abroad in the hearts of the children of men; wherefore, it is the most desirable above all things." 1 Nephi 11:22 [1293]

            1.4.3.3.4.4.5. Con:
            Faithful Latter-day Saints (Mormons) believe both the Bible and the Book of Mormon to be the word of God and have no trouble reconciling the two.

            1.4.3.3.4.4.6. Pro:
            The themes of the Bible differ widely from those of the Book of Mormon.

              1.4.3.3.4.4.6.1. Con:
              The Bible [1294] and Book of Mormon [1295] teach the Gospel of Jesus Christ.

              1.4.3.3.4.4.6.2. Con:
              The Bible [1296] and Book of Mormon [1297] teach the Ten Commandments.

              1.4.3.3.4.4.6.3. Con:
              The Bible [1298] and Book of Mormon [1299] teach the Golden Rule.

              1.4.3.3.4.4.6.4. Con:
              The Bible [1300] and Book of Mormon [1301] teach the importance of kindness to strangers.

              1.4.3.3.4.4.6.5. Con:
              The Bible and Book of Mormon teach the importance of attaining fellowship with God.

                1.4.3.3.4.4.6.5.1. Pro:
                The Book of Mormon teaches that entering the presence of God is an important part of redemption from the Fall of Adam. (Ether 3:13) [1302]

                1.4.3.3.4.4.6.5.2. Pro:
                The Bible teaches the importance of having fellowship with the Father and with Jesus Christ. (1 John 1) [1303]

              1.4.3.3.4.4.6.6. Con:
              The Bible [1304] and Book of Mormon [1305] have a shared theme of prophets ministering at God's command to encourage his people in the ways of righteousness.

              1.4.3.3.4.4.6.7. Pro:
              -> See 1.4.3.1.5.9.

        1.4.3.3.5. Con:
        -> See 1.2.1.3.7.

        1.4.3.3.6. Con:
        The Book of Mormon contains self-serving purported prophecies about Joseph Smith.

          1.4.3.3.6.1. Con:
          It is natural that a book of scripture destined to be hidden in the earth, to be brought to light and translated centuries later, would speak of the prophet that would translate it. It would be unnatural for it not to speak of its eventual translation.

            1.4.3.3.6.1.1. Pro:
            Jesus Christ testified of his divinity. That alone does not make the claim true or false.

            1.4.3.3.6.1.2. Pro:
            Joseph Smith translated a text. A translator cannot choose what a text says. If it has claims about his role as a prophet, he would have been a poor translator to leave them out.

            1.4.3.3.6.1.3. Pro:
            The Koran testifies of Muhammad as a prophet. That alone has no bearing on the truthfulness of the text.

          1.4.3.3.6.2. Con:
          A central message of the Book of Mormon is that the Nephite record (The Book of Mormon) will come to the gentiles and the house of Israel in the latter days and to convince the world that Jesus is the Christ. It is natural and necessary to mention the prophet who would bring that record to light.

          1.4.3.3.6.3. Con:
          This is inconsistent with other actions taken by Joseph Smith, who has a history of publishing things about himself that are not favorable.

            1.4.3.3.6.3.1. Pro:
            Joseph Smith was reprimanded by the Lord for losing pages of the Book of Mormon manuscript, which he published in the Doctrine and Covenants. D&C 3:5-8 [1306]

          1.4.3.3.6.4. Con:
          "Self-serving prophecies" about people instrumental in scriptural history are common. For example, in the New Testament Jesus Christ taught that he was the Messiah and the Koran, which was written by Muhammad, teaches that Muhammad was a prophet.

          1.4.3.3.6.5. Pro:
          -> See 1.3.4.10.7.5.3.1.

          1.4.3.3.6.6. Pro:
          The Book of Mormon analogizes Joseph Smith's scribe Oliver Cowdery [1307] to the biblical Aaron, spokesman for Moses. (2 Nephi 3:17-21 [1308])

            1.4.3.3.6.6.1. Pro:
            The Book of Mormon implicitly compares Aaron's rod [1309] to the divining rod [1310] used by Oliver Cowdery [1311]. (2 Nephi 3:17-18 [1312])

              1.4.3.3.6.6.1.1. Pro:
              A revelation that Joseph Smith dictated during the translation of the Book of Mormon stated that Oliver Cowdery [1313] had a heavenly gift (later described as the "gift of Aaron," 1835 D&C XXXVI:3 [1314]) of working with the "sprout" (divining rod [1315]), and that by means of this rod, Cowdery would receive revelation from God. (Revelation, April 1829-B [1316]; Book of Commandments VII [1317]).

              1.4.3.3.6.6.1.2. Pro:
              The Book of Mormon's reference to Oliver Cowdery immediately follows and parallels a verse that refers to Aaron's rod [1318]; the parallel structure implies a reference to Cowdery's divining rod [1319]. (2 Nephi 3:17-18 [1320])

            1.4.3.3.6.6.2. Pro:
            Oliver Cowdery [1321] is the "spokesman" mentioned in 2 Nephi 3:18-19 [1322].

          1.4.3.3.6.7. Pro:
          The Book of Mormon purports to predict Joseph Smith's first name, and the first name of his father. (2 Nephi 3:15 [1323])

          1.4.3.3.6.8. Pro:
          The Book of Mormon declares that whatever Joseph Smith and Oliver Cowdery would write would be "expedient in [God's] wisdom." (2 Nephi 3:19 [1324])

      1.4.3.4. Con:
      Spiritual Evidences are not valid [1325] for truth claims. Logically, if you are going to accept the spiritual evidences that the Book of Mormon is an authentic book you must also accept that spiritual evidences of Billions of others are also true.

    1.4.4. Pro:
    Prophets Say So: The Book of Mormon fulfills biblical prophecies.

      1.4.4.1. Pro:
      John (recording Jesus Christ's prophecy): The Book of Mormon people are among the "other sheep...which are not of this fold," that Jesus said he "must bring, and they shall hear my voice; and there shall be one fold, and one shepherd." (John 10:16 [1326])

        1.4.4.1.1. Con:
        The claim Jesus made in John 10:16 was referring to reaching out to the world.
        
        (Read the section titled "The Pride-Shattering News of Other Sheep") [1327]

      1.4.4.2. Pro:
      Jacob: The Book of Mormon people are metaphorically the "branch" that the biblical Jacob [1328] said would be broken off and made into a "fruitful bough" whose branches would run "over the wall" (Genesis 49:22-26 [1329])

        1.4.4.2.1. Con:
        That's referring to the fall of the walls of Jericho by Joshua (Of the tribe of Ephraim, Joseph's son). Full chapter [1330]

        1.4.4.2.2. Con:
        The only obvious way the "wall" could be made relevant to the Book of Mormon is to view it as the Atlantic Ocean, but there seems to be little relationship between an ocean and a wall, especially since the branches are described going "over" it rather than "across" it.

      1.4.4.3. Pro:
      Isaiah: The Book of Mormon is the voice that shall "speak out of the ground" mentioned in Isaiah 29 [1331]:4.

        1.4.4.3.1. Con:
        Isaiah is talking of what will happen to the city of Ariel there is no mention of a book or of scripture.

          1.4.4.3.1.1. Pro:
          The city of Ariel is the "city where David dwelt" according to Isaiah 29:1 and therefore cannot refer to any North American Book of Mormon authors or the places in which they live.

        1.4.4.3.2. Con:
        This interpretation ignores the context of the chapter which has nothing to do with ancient American tablets in the ground or anyone speaking to anyone out of anything but agony.Isaiah 29 [1332]

        1.4.4.3.3. Con:
        When describing how this voice will speak, Isaiah 29:4 [1333] says it will be "like a medium's [voice]", where the same word for "medium" is used as in the passages condemning mediums.

        1.4.4.3.4. Pro:
        -> See 1.2.9.7.4.1.

      1.4.4.4. Con:
      Prophecies are written in metaphorical and symbolic language and are usually not falsifiable. Therefore, a claim that they are being or have been fulfilled is not a strong argument for proving authenticity.

      1.4.4.5. Pro:
      Ezekiel: The Book of Mormon is the "stick of Joseph" discussed in Ezekiel 37:15-28 [1334]

        1.4.4.5.1. Con:
        The two sticks in Ezekiel 37:15-28 [1335] symbolize the nations of Judah and Israel, which the passage says are to be reunited under a single king.

          1.4.4.5.1.1. Pro:
          Ezekiel 37 [1336]:18-28 literally and explicitly explains the meaning of joining the sticks of Judah and Ephraim in verses 15-17, and there is no mention of joining any religious texts in that explanation.

        1.4.4.5.2. Con:
        In Ezekiel 37:15-28 [1337], only a specific phrase is written on each of the sticks of Judah and Ephraim, and there is no reason from the context to suggest that either of the sticks contains other text or represents a lengthy document.

      1.4.4.6. Pro:
      Isaiah: The Book of Mormon is the "book that is sealed" and the "marvellous work" discussed in Isaiah 29:11-14 [1338] (King James Version).

        1.4.4.6.1. Con:
        In Isaiah 29 [1339]:11-14, the sealed document (or book) is the vision of the siege at Jerusalem described in the previous verses (Isaiah 29:1-13), not the Book of Mormon.

        1.4.4.6.2. Con:
        The "him that is not learned" in Isaiah 29 [1340]:12 refers, in the Hebrew, to "one who has not known books," and means "one who is illiterate." This cannot refer to Joseph Smith, who was literate.

        1.4.4.6.3. Con:
        In Isaiah 29 [1341]:11-14, the illiterate or unlearned one was, like the learned or literate one, unable to read the document. This passage cannot refer to Joseph Smith and the golden plates [1342], because Smith claimed to be able to read the golden plates.

        1.4.4.6.4. Con:
        The Hebrew god's "marvellous work" in Isaiah 29 [1343]:14 refers to the destructive siege of Jerusalem which is the subject of verses 1-16 (see NRSV [1344]).

        1.4.4.6.5. Con:
        Isaiah 29:11-14 could be about any religious text coming after the Old Testament, including the New Testament, the Quran, Dianetics, the Guru Granth Sahib [1345], etc.

      1.4.4.7. Con:
      Joseph Smith had a motive to ensure that the Book of Mormon and the circumstances of its publication appeared to fulfill biblical prophecies.

        1.4.4.7.1. Con:
        Joseph and those of his inner circle had a motive to deny the faith's teachings to save their lives. Had Joseph made it all up, it would make sense that a charlatan would renounce his made-up story to save his own life. The fact that Joseph Smith did not do that attests to his assertion that the BoM is authentic scripture.

          1.4.4.7.1.1. Con:
          The mob shot at Smith without letting him explain. There was no opportunity [1346] to confess the BofM was not authentic.

          1.4.4.7.1.2. Pro:
          Joseph Smith was arrested at least 42 times. [1347]

    1.4.5. Pro:
    -> See 1.4.2.6.3.1.

    1.4.6. Con:
    Many religions claims that their holy books are authentic because they are the word of God. This is not adequate given that the claim cannot be investigated or proven.

    1.4.7. Con:
    According to the Latter-day Saint movement, God says the Book of Mormon is authentic scripture. But according to many other religions God says the Book of Mormon is not scripture. Those claims are equally as valid.

      1.4.7.1. Con:
      Some believe the period of revelation ended with the original apostles and use Sola Scriptura as an argument to show the Book of Mormon is not scripture. If this is true, then God said nothing about the Book of Mormon being scripture, because if he did, that would be revelation.

        1.4.7.1.1. Con:
        Sola scriptura is an argument against unbiblical, extra-biblical and/or anti-biblical doctrines. It discredits the Book of Mormon as revelation from God not because it is warned of in the Bible, but because it is not the Bible.

          1.4.7.1.1.1. Con:
          Sola scriptura stems from human philosophy and misinterpretation of God's word (Matthew 15:9 [1348]). The term itself isn't clarified or taught by the original prophets or apostles.

            1.4.7.1.1.1.1. Pro:
            The origin of sola scriptura philosophy is not found in the Bible or taught in original Christianity, but taught 1,300 years later [1349].

          1.4.7.1.1.2. Con:
          There is very little in the Book of Mormon that is doctrinally disagreeable [1350] to orthodox Christians. Rather most differences in doctrine are found in the Doctrine & Covenants and Pearl of Great Price.

        1.4.7.1.2. Pro:
        Those who base their view of the Book of Mormon in the philosophical belief of Sola Scriptura, can't truthfully claim God said the Book of Mormon is not authentic scripture because the concept of sola scriptura itself isn't grounded in biblical Christianity.

          1.4.7.1.2.1. Pro:
          -> See 1.4.7.1.1.1.1.

  1.5. Pro:
  No Alternate Explanation: The Book of Mormon's authenticity is buttressed by the fact that there exists no plausible substitute explanation of the Book's existence.

    1.5.1. Pro:
    -> See 1.2.9.5.1.

    1.5.2. Pro:
    The theory that Joseph Smith authored the Book of Mormon is untenable.

      1.5.2.1. Con:
      That Joseph Smith claimed authorship in the 1830 edition of the Book of Mormon is evidence that he considered himself the sole author.

        1.5.2.1.1. Con:
        To obtain copyright at that time Joseph Smith had to claim authorship. [1351]

          1.5.2.1.1.1. Pro:
          Joseph Smith was listed as "author and proprietor" in the 1830 edition of the Book of Mormon in order to comply with federal copyright law. Other translators, including those who produced the 1824 edition of the King James Version of the Bible, were listed as "Author" to conform to this law.  (see www.fairmormon.org [1352])

        1.5.2.1.2. Con:
        If Joseph Smith did not claim authorship, others would have in attempts to discredit him.

        1.5.2.1.3. Con:
        Joseph Smith didn't claim to author the Book of Mormon, but he claimed to translate it.  In June 1829, Joseph Smith submitted a legal document to secure the copyright for the Book of Mormon.  Joseph Smith made the legal claim of "author and proprietor" as required in US copyright statutes, and stated that the original text was an ancient record "written by the hand of Mormon" rendered into English as an "interpretation" or translation "by the gift and power of God." (see www.fairmormon.org [1353])

        1.5.2.1.4. Con:
        The Title Page of the Book of Mormon [1354] states "An Account Written by the Hand of Mormon upon Plates Taken from the Plates of Nephi"

      1.5.2.2. Pro:
      Emma Smith, Joseph Smith's wife, stated that the Book of Mormon was translated in onlyabout 63 days [1355] by her 23 year-old husband, Joseph Smith, who had only three years of formal schooling. Emma stated that Joseph was not capable of dictating or writing a well-worded letter to a friend [1356].

        1.5.2.2.1. Con:
        Emma Smith is not creditable unless we believe this statement is also true about Joseph Smith: "There was no revelation on either polygamy, or spiritual wives...No such thing as polygamy, or spiritual wifery, was taught, publicly or privately, before my husband's death, that I have now, or ever had any knowledge of." Copy of "The Saints Herald" [1357]"

          1.5.2.2.1.1. Con:
          This is a 'Poisoning the Well [1358]' fallacy. Just because one statement by a witness is incorrect does not allow us to discard all statements from that witness as automatically false. Each statement must be evaluated individually.

      1.5.2.3. Pro:
      An experienced translator can normally translate about one page per day. Joseph averaged about nine pages per day. Russel M. Nelson, "A Testimony of the Book of Mormon." [1359]

        1.5.2.3.1. Con:
        The claim of the skeptic is that Joseph Smith fabricated, not translated, the Book of Mormon, so it would be more appropriate to use the writing rate of an author for comparison, not the translation rate of a translator.

      1.5.2.4. Pro:
      Joseph Smith's third grade formal education and near illiteracy would make creating a complex work such as the Book of Mormon impossible.

        1.5.2.4.1. Pro:
        "I was mearly instructid in reading and writing and the ground rules of Arithmatic" - Joseph Smith in 1832 [1360], two years after the publication of the Book of Mormon.

          1.5.2.4.1.1. Con:
          This is after the Book of Mormon, so Smith could have written poorly on purpose to make it look like he couldn't have fabricated the Book of Mormon.

          1.5.2.4.1.2. Con:
          Joseph Smith could have used scribes to help prevent spelling and grammar errors from making it into the Book of Mormon.

        1.5.2.4.2. Pro:
        Joseph "could neither write nor dictate a coherent and well-worded letter let alone dictate a book like the Book of Mormon" [1361] - Emma Smith, Joseph Smith's wife.

          1.5.2.4.2.1. Con:
          Emma Smith was [1362] an early Mormon leader and thus could have been biased against viewing her husband as capable of having fabricated the Book of Mormon.

        1.5.2.4.3. Con:
        Joseph Smith was [1363] educated enough to participate in a "juvenile debating club."

          1.5.2.4.3.1. Pro:
          -> See 1.3.4.7.1.1.1.2.5.1.1.3.1.1.1.

        1.5.2.4.4. Con:
        Joseph Smith could have been self-educated.

          1.5.2.4.4.1. Pro:
          Many autodictats, or self-taught [1364] people, exist.

            1.5.2.4.4.1.1. Pro:
            Abraham Lincoln didn't [1365] have much of a formal education and was self-taught.

            1.5.2.4.4.1.2. Pro:
            Benjamin Franklin had [1366] little formal education but taught himself how to be a good writer.

        1.5.2.4.5. Con:
        -> See 1.5.2.4.1.2.

        1.5.2.4.6. Pro:
        Critics and non-believers considered Joseph Smith unlearned and poorly educated [1367] around the time of the Book of Mormon's translation.

          1.5.2.4.6.1. Pro:
          Eber Howe, a well-known critic of Joseph Smith, stated in 1834 that he believed “it to be a fact” that “the common advantages of education were denied to [Joseph Smith], or that they were much neglected.” [1368]

            1.5.2.4.6.1.1. Con:
            -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.1.1.1.

          1.5.2.4.6.2. Pro:
          When asked whether Joseph was “sufficiently intelligent and talented to compose and dictate of his own ability the [Book of Mormon] written down by the scribes,” Joseph's brother in law, Michael Morse, responded in the negative, explaining that “he was confident that he [Morse] had more learning than Joseph then had. [1369]”

        1.5.2.4.7. Con:
        Other writers around the same period published works while claiming similarly few formal years of schooling. Consider [1370]: Jane Austen, 3; Abraham Lincoln, 1; Mark Twain, 5; Walt Whitman, 6.

      1.5.2.5. Pro:
      Joseph Smith believed the Book of Mormon was authentic [1371].

        1.5.2.5.1. Con:
        If Joseph Smith made up the Book of Mormon you must expect him to claim its authenticity. That's how cons work.

        1.5.2.5.2. Con:
        -> See 1.2.1.3.1.1.3.1.

        1.5.2.5.3. Con:
        -> See 1.2.

        1.5.2.5.4. Con:
        Belief has no intrinsic value in proving the truth.

          1.5.2.5.4.1. Con:
          It is relevant and logical to inquire whether Joseph Smith's actions were consistent with a man committing a fraud for personal gain or whether they were consistent with a prophet acting pursuant to a divine mandate.

            1.5.2.5.4.1.1. Con:
            This is not a binary choice model. According to Occam's razor, it would be more consistent with a mad-man fantasizing about being a prophet.

          1.5.2.5.4.2. Pro:
          Authenticity is not determined by popular vote nor a majority of believers, but by a preponderance of evidence as conceded by expert observers.

      1.5.2.6. Con:
      The idea that Joseph Smith must have had additional divine assistance in writing the Book of Mormon is partly based on the LDS Church's implication that it was written in a very short period (around 65 days). However there is evidence that Joseph was formulating ideas that would become the BoM years before its completion. His mother describes [1372] his “amusing recitals" of "the ancient inhabitants of this continent."

      1.5.2.7. Con:
      The theory that Joseph Smith authored the Book of Mormon has been maintained and defended for approaching two hundred years and shows no sign of fading. It is therefore tenable.

      1.5.2.8. Pro:
      -> See 1.3.4.7.1.1.1.

    1.5.3. Pro:
    The absence of evidence is evidence of absence where careful research has been done. The Book of Mormon has been studied carefully and there are university disciplines in non-Church affiliated schools devoted in large part to the subject. Claremont [1373], University of Virginia [1374], University of Utah [1375], Graduate Theological Union [1376].

    1.5.4. Con:
    Joseph Smith plausibly created the Book of Mormon at least in part by plagiarizing from other sources.

      1.5.4.1. Pro:
      Joseph Smith plagiarized from the KJV.

        1.5.4.1.1. Pro:
        -> See 1.3.4.10.6.9.3.

      1.5.4.2. Pro:
      Joseph Smith plagiarized from the Apocrypha [1377].

        1.5.4.2.1. Pro:
        "Nephi" plausibly came from 2 Maccabees 1:36 [1378].

          1.5.4.2.1.1. Con:
          Nephi in 2 Maccabees 1:36 [1379] is the name of a place, not a person.

          1.5.4.2.1.2. Con:
          Joseph taking “Nephi” from 2 Maccabees 1:36 [1380] doesn’t account for other names [1381] mentioned in 1 Nephi 1 [1382].

            1.5.4.2.1.2.1. Pro:
            The name Sariah [1383], isn’t found in the Bible or the apocrypha, but was a name later found to be used by 11 different men and a woman in the ancient Near East.

              1.5.4.2.1.2.1.1. Con:
              Sariah seems to be a variant of Seraiah, which is used in 2 Samuel 8:17 [1384], Jeremiah 40:8 [1385], Ezra 7:1 [1386], and Nehemiah 11:11 [1387].

                1.5.4.2.1.2.1.1.1. Con:
                Although Sariah seems to be a variant of Seraiah, all the mentions of Seraiah in the Old Testament are of men. It is significant that an ancient record [1388] has been found of a woman using the name Sariah.

            1.5.4.2.1.2.2. Pro:
            The name Laman [1389] isn’t found in the apocrypha or the Bible, but the Arabic names Laman and Lemuel are later found to be pendant names often given to the two eldest sons in the ancient Near East.

            1.5.4.2.1.2.3. Con:
            Ancient Hebrew names are simple [1390] and lack vowels, so it isn't extraordinary that made-up names could have sometimes matched real Hebrew names.

        1.5.4.2.2. Pro:
        2 Esdras 13:1 [1391] matches 1 Nephi 8:2 [1392].

          1.5.4.2.2.1. Pro:
          Both use the expression "I dreamed a dream."

          1.5.4.2.2.2. Con:
          Plausibly not plagiarism as the expression “dreamed a dream” appears to be a somewhat common expression used in the ancient Near East, a term found in Judges 7:13 [1393], Genesis 37:5 [1394], Genesis 40:5 [1395], Daniel 2:1 [1396], etc. in the Old Testament.

      1.5.4.3. Pro:
      Joseph Smith plagiarized from View of the Hebrews [1397].

        1.5.4.3.1. Con:
        Contemporaries of Joseph Smith never [1398] claimed that he copied from View of the Hebrews.

        1.5.4.3.2. Con:
        Smith quoted [1399] from the author of View of the Hebrews, Ethan Smith. It is [1400] implausible that he would draw attention to the source he had plagiarized from.

      1.5.4.4. Pro:
      Joseph Smith seems to have gotten some of his Book of Mormon names from places near him [1401].

        1.5.4.4.1. Con:
        Since some of the parallels of names between the Book of Mormon and places near Joseph Smith [1402] can also be found in the Bible, the names in the Book of Mormon are more likely appropriate coming from a people and their posterity who left Jerusalem. See Bible references for Lehi [1403], onidah [1404], angola [1405], Moria [1406]nton [1407], jacob [1408]ugath [1409], Alma [1410], Shilo [1411]m, Kish [1412]kum [1413]en, Moron [1414], Shur [1415]r, Teancum [1416], Riplia [1417]ncum [1418].

          1.5.4.4.1.1. Pro:
          "Lehi" in the BoM is the same as "Lehi [1419]" in the OT, so it is unlikely to have been derived from Lehigh Valley, Pennsylvania.

          1.5.4.4.1.2. Pro:
          "Onidah" in the BoM is similar to "Judah [1420]" in the OT.

            1.5.4.4.1.2.1. Con:
            They don't even start with the same letter or have the same initial vowel sound.

          1.5.4.4.1.3. Pro:
          "Angola" in the BoM is similar to "Tola [1421]" in the OT.

          1.5.4.4.1.4. Pro:
          "Morianton" in the BoM is similar to "Moriah [1422]" and "Eshton [1423]" in the OT.

          1.5.4.4.1.5. Pro:
          "Shilom" in the BoM is similar to "Shiloh [1424]" in the OT.

          1.5.4.4.1.6. Pro:
          "Jacobugath" in the BoM is similar to "Jacob [1425]" and "Gath [1426]" in the OT.

        1.5.4.4.2. Pro:
        "Lehi" came [1427] from Lehigh Valley, Pennsylvania.

          1.5.4.4.2.1. Con:
          -> See 1.5.4.4.1.1.

        1.5.4.4.3. Pro:
        "Onidah" came [1428] from Oneida, New York.

          1.5.4.4.3.1. Con:
          The village of Oneida, New York [1429] was incorporated in 1848 and became a town in 1901, long after Joseph Smith published the Book of Mormon.

        1.5.4.4.4. Pro:
        "Angola" came [1430] from Angola, New York.

          1.5.4.4.4.1. Con:
          The Village of Angola [1431] was incorporated in 1873. There was a post office established at that location in 1854 or 1855 with the name "Angola". The Book of Mormon was published long before these dates.

        1.5.4.4.5. Pro:
        "Morianton" came [1432] from Morgantown, Pennsylvania.

          1.5.4.4.5.1. Con:
          -> See 1.5.4.4.1.4.

        1.5.4.4.6. Pro:
        Teancum came [1433] from Tecumseh, Ontario.

          1.5.4.4.6.1. Con:
          Tecumseh, Ontario [1434] was only known by that name in 1912 which is long after the death of Joseph Smith.

        1.5.4.4.7. Pro:
        "Shilom" came [1435] from Shiloh, Ohio [1436].

      1.5.4.5. Pro:
      -> See 1.3.2.6.1.

    1.5.5. Con:
    Sidney Rigdon [1437] could have helped write the Book of Mormon.

    1.5.6. Pro:
    Stylometric studies support the idea that the Book of Mormon has a writing style different from that of either Smith or his contemporaries.

      1.5.6.1. Pro:
      -> See 1.3.2.1.2.

  1.6. Con:
  -> See 1.3.5.

  1.7. Pro:
  The Metal Plates: [1438] The plates from which Joseph Smith translated the Book of Mormon were a historical artifact made by ancient Americans.

    1.7.1. Pro:
    -> See 1.2.9.13.5.

    1.7.2. Pro:
    -> See 1.3.5.1.

    1.7.3. Pro:
    Every effort [1439] was made to steal the plates from Joseph. The chances of one maybe two people believing enough to try and steal them wouldn't be convincing but when we read of mobs of people over time including conjurers with seer stones getting visions of where they were hidden; it gets exponentially harder with each new figure to argue that this was simply a shared delusion.

      1.7.3.1. Con:
      All this demonstrates is that a lot of people believed that Joseph Smith really did have the plates. This doesn't help the Mormon case because both sides agree on this (at least the believing Mormons thought Smith really did have the plates).

    1.7.4. Con:
    The golden plates [1440], if they existed, are not available for examination to determine whether or not they are authentic.

      1.7.4.1. Con:
      The Mayflower [1441] is not in existence to examine. That is not evidence against its existence.

      1.7.4.2. Con:
      If we had the plates it would prove the Church true. This takes out the doctrinal need for faith [1442] which prophets have always said was necessary.

      1.7.4.3. Pro:
      The Book of Mormon and other artifacts Joseph Smith claimed to possess were conveniently repossessed by God.

        1.7.4.3.1. Con:
        God may have taken the plates, but there is nothing that states the other artifacts like the seer stones and Urim & Thummim were taken. Given that prophets throught the ages as documented in the scriptures had access to these tools, it stands to reason the current prophet still possesses them.

        1.7.4.3.2. Con:
        God's repossession of the golden plates was not merely 'convenient', but a religious teaching; God repossessed the plates to encourage people [1443] to learn how to turn to Him in faith for answers, rather than turning to physical objects which can't provide constant guidance for an individual's personal life.

    1.7.5. Con:
    -> See 1.2.1.1.2.6.7.2.2.

    1.7.6. Pro:
    -> See 1.2.9.13.

    1.7.7. Con:
    -> See 1.2.9.

  1.8. Con:
  There is a lack of archaeological evidence to support the fantastical claims in the BoM. Thomas Stuart Ferguson [1444] a member of the LDS set out to find evidence, but was stymied at every turn. Ferguson wrote "I must conclude that Joseph Smith had not the remotest skill in things Egyptian-hieroglyphics."
